madam president, i would like to thank you. it is unusual for a convinced eu sceptic like me to be able to praise a proposal and recommend that the house vote in favour. that is because we are dealing with transboundary environmental problems. we are aware of the possibilities of the single market. normally, however, the proposals are unreasonably bureaucratic - not so in this case. this is a proposal that avoids red tape. our speakers are on the right track. this suggests strict and tight control. the use of poisons such as alcohol and tobacco should be an individual decision. as a collective, we should be able to protect ourselves from poisoning. this is the proposal. at the same time, there is the flexibility, the mutual recognition, the zoning, the right of nations to ban pesticides beyond those listed here, which is very good. pesticides already authorised shall not be withdrawn. i find this rather dubious. we should be very strict about poisons of this kind. i would remind you that our colleague, mr mote, pointed out the risk that citizens will buy from other countries when we introduce stricter rules in the eu. i don't think he did. it is important to state that these are products from eu member states. then people will buy this. i therefore recommend that the house vote in favour of this excellent compromise.
for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. mr commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. i should like to begin by thanking my colleague from the liberal group, mr klinz, for a very good job. in a turbulent time like this autumn, the rapporteur has managed to find a reasonable compromise, as we have already heard. even without a financial crisis, these issues are difficult to deal with. i know this very well, because in 2001 i was responsible for piloting the ucits. the aim of ucits funds is not only to create a better and larger fund market, but also an open and consumer-friendly market. and that has happened on a large scale. many obstacles were removed in 2001. i'd like to remind you that it wasn't exactly easy back then either. besides, it was before the lamfalussy trial. however, some obstacles remain, and these obstacles are now being addressed. marketing is improving. it will be a stronger protection for investors. the merger of funds and master-feeder structures will be possible, and cooperation between supervisory authorities will be strengthened, as also mentioned by the commissioner. a sensitive issue has been the rules on the management company passport. we have already heard comments on this. we are aware of the different positions. i do think, however, that the proposals before us are good. competition and between relevant information and efficiency. too much information is not good, nor is too little. the question of language, which is certainly a sensitive one, but i think we must dare to raise it if in writing. - the report on the cap and global food security discusses important issues such as the impact of food price increases in poor and rich countries and the importance of ensuring food security for all. we swedish social democrats decided to vote against the report because it contains problematic texts on agricultural policy. we want, among other things, to reduce the share of agricultural policy in the eu budget, to maintain and develop cross-compliance and to adapt the system to the market. the report does not agree with these views, and that is why we are voting against it.
in writing. - it's important that we fight and alleviate hunger. i therefore welcome the contents of the mairead mcguinness own-initiative report on the cap and the global food supply. yet i chose to cast my vote. this is because the report was in part strongly protectionist. subsidising and regulating our domestic agriculture does not serve our ambition for an open, green, secure and entrepreneurial europe. a freer world market for agricultural products makes it easier for people in poor countries to develop their farms. we are seeing this now, especially in large parts of africa.
in writing. the committee on agriculture, fisheries and rural development has also adopted a number of amendments to the proposal for a regulation on the common organisation of the market in fishery products. it reads: the european parliament calls on the council and the commission, in close cooperation with the acp countries, to give priority to the question of how these countries are affected by eu export subsidies for agricultural products and to commit themselves to delivering on their commitments in this area in a concrete and lasting way by avoiding dumping. the report argues, however, that the eu has addressed those aspects of eu agricultural policy which could distort trade and have negative consequences for farmers in developing countries. the report complains that non-eu countries produce according to very different production standards, which has created unfair conditions of competition for eu agricultural products. these two statements in the report are controversial to say the least and not something that all political forces in the eu agree with. if that were the case, could the committee on development's proposals also be included in the text of the report? the report also opposes reductions in agricultural aid and opposes reform of the common agricultural policy. the commission is also proposing a policy of informing the public about the common agricultural policy, which i regard as political propaganda for a system which is highly controversial, not least in my own country. i have therefore voted against this report.
in writing. the european parliament has also called for the commission to be given the task of drawing up a report on the implementation of the lisbon strategy. strengthening dialogue with civil society is essential to creating a europe that listens to and represents the views of its citizens. we also share the report's call for greater openness in the council's work to enable civil society to participate meaningfully in the dialogue. we would like to stress, however, that we think it is wrong to give churches and religious communities a special status among civil society organisations. churches and religious communities shall participate in the dialogue with the union institutions in the same way as other organisations.
in writing. - i voted in favour of the grabowska report on the prospects for the development of civil dialogue under the lisbon treaty. i believe it is important to strengthen dialogue with civil society in order to create a europe that listens to and represents the views of its citizens. i also share the report's call for greater openness in the council's work to enable civil society to participate meaningfully in the dialogue. however, i do not think that there was any need to bring the treaty of lisbon into this, since it is no longer relevant after the irish people voted 'no' in the referendum. furthermore, it is wrong to give churches and religious communities a special status among civil society organisations. churches and religious communities shall participate in the dialogue with the union institutions in the same way as other organisations.
in writing. - the treaty of lisbon has not been adopted. it has been rejected by the voters of ireland in a referendum and must therefore be considered to have fallen. the same draft treaty had already been rejected in referendums in france and the netherlands. the federalist majority in the european parliament, however, does not want to listen and insists on an even more supranational union, despite the fact that citizens have shown their scepticism in several referendums and would show it in even more countries if they had the chance. this action by the federalist majority of the european parliament shows the kind of citizens' dialogue that is desired. you just want to listen to those from civil society who fit the federalist mold. the report before us, outside the legislative procedure, is not impressive. it states in paragraph 9 that all eu institutions should keep up-to-date records of all relevant ngos. it means an unnecessary increase in bureaucratic work that leads nowhere. the report also states in paragraph 11 that the aim is to promote an 'active european mindset' among the citizens of the union. what is the definition of this mindset? the worst part, however, is the call in paragraph 22 for european associations and civil society organisations to be given a common legal basis at eu level. once again, a building block for the eu state is proposed. i have therefore voted against this report.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' in the definition of the term 'common carrier'. we believe that the report does not make it clear that fisheries policy must be designed on the basis of environmental and sustainability criteria. the report also focuses too much on holding back the necessary reforms of fisheries policy and protecting the interests of the large-scale fishing industry.
in writing. - the report by mrs breyer on the placing of plant protection products on the market. the regulatory framework aimed at improving both food safety and the environmental impact of plant protection products is good and important. since the parliament's first reading, analyses have shown that the regulatory framework risks becoming very far-reaching and inflexible, and may make it impossible to grow common crops (such as carrots and onions) on a commercial scale in sweden. the situation is not improved by the fact that the impact assessments of the rules differ in essential conclusions, for example between the chemicals agency and its uk counterpart, the pesticides safety directorate. we regret that there has been no opportunity to vote on clarifications on this matter at the second reading of this parliament, but at the same time we note that the text adopted contains improvements compared with parliament's first reading. we would have liked the agreement reached between the european parliament and the council to have taken account of and clarified the rules so that they were more effective in prohibiting dangerous uses, but also continued to allow the necessary, responsible and safe plant protection which is now in danger of being prohibited.
in writing. - we support the report because we believe that sustainable public finances are very important. however, we reject the wording of paragraph 8 which says that there must be a gradual and sharp reduction in the tax burden on middle- and low-income earners and pensioners, with tax deductions, revised tax rates and compensation for higher marginal tax due to inflation. we believe that these are issues which should not be dealt with at eu level but which should be decided by the member states themselves.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the rapporteur, mr cappatto, has pointed out that there are major shortcomings in access to eu documents. i support the rapporteur's suggestions for improvements in the light of the annual report. i also support the amendments tabled by the rapporteur. i also assume that parliament will vote in favour of a genuine principle of publicity in the forthcoming vote on the transparency regulation, the cashman report. in the annual report i would like to stress in particular that it is totally unacceptable that transparency is not guaranteed in the area of delegated legislation, which covers 90% of the legally binding acts adopted each year by the eu institutions. the participation of members in the work of parliament and all the financial relations of members in connection with their duties in parliament must also be fully public. i support the proposal for a unified public register. finally, greater transparency is not a populist demand but a democratic one.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. transparency is a virtue of democracy. in a democracy, the people elect the government. they appoint officials to implement democratic decisions. it would be absurd if these elected representatives and employees had the right to withhold information from their clients. there are two possible explanations for this. the first is the legacy of a pre-democratic society that saw the people as subjects of unelected rulers. the investigators were not allowed to pry into the affairs of the ruler. the second is that national security, respect for the integrity of citizens, trade secrets, market manipulation and the like may require exemptions from transparency. the first explanation is totally irrelevant today. the government, as the government, cannot withhold information from the people. the second explanation, on the other hand, is solid and involves difficult balancing. unfortunately, these legitimate demands for trade-offs are often used by the superior to preserve the possibility of secrecy. the eu has no proud tradition of openness. in the eu's childhood and early youth, almost everything was secret, including the commission's internal telephone directory. the eu started as a mandarin empire and still is, but important steps have been taken. the code of conduct of 1993, the acceptance of the requirements of the swedish journalists' association in 1998, the regulation in 2001. it is with a certain pride that i say that my own country, sweden, has the longest historical tradition of statutory transparency. that is why the swedish state intervened in the so-called turco case and caused the court of justice to overrule the council and the court of first instance this summer. this was another step away from the mandarin rule, but there is still a long way to go to establish democratic openness in the eu. there's a lot of internal resistance to crush. mr cappatto's report should therefore have the full support of the house - another step in the long march.
mr president, i would like to thank you. transparency, please. transparency has really undergone a dramatic change in the eu - i think the commissioner would agree with that - since we have had the implementation of regulation 1049 and the websites. i would also like to emphasize that i think that the commission and the commissioner have made very fine and good contributions to this issue in recent years. it was not easy at first, but i think the cooperation in recent years has been very good, and you should be commended for that. everyone is always shouting at the commission, which i do not think is fair in all circumstances. of course, it cannot be that the rif legislation is not transparent as regards the reasons. alexander alvaro is absolutely right. it's not possible. but to go so far as to require an official or a member of the european parliament, for example, to report every day on what he has done every day is quite unreasonable. finally, you spend half your day writing a report on what you've done. i would like to conclude by saying that we support the capato report as long as it leads to legal certainty, clear legislation that is not unnecessarily bureaucratic.
member of the commission. − mr president, let me first of all apologise for the weakness of my voice, as i have a very heavy cold. you are about to vote on the proposal for a directive on the procurement of defence and security equipment, which the commission tabled in december 2007 as part of the so-called ‘defence package’. this proposal is a major step forward towards the establishment of a common european defence market, which, by itself, is an important contribution to the union’s security and defence policy. in other words, this directive is a politically highly important instrument which will help to strengthen europe’s defence capabilities, make public spending more efficient and enhance the security of our citizens. the negotiation process for the proposal has benefited from the close cooperation between parliament, the council and the commission. therefore, i would like to warmly thank the rapporteur, mr lambsdorff, for his intensive and efficient work to achieve an agreement with the council in first reading. i would also like to thank the shadow rapporteurs for their constructive cooperation. i also want to congratulate the french presidency, together with the slovenian presidency, for the way they successfully spurred the negotiations in council. and last but not least, i would like to thank the czech presidency for its commitment in finalising this file. the commission welcomes the text that we have now on the table. the new directive is tailor-made for defence and security procurement. it will give contracting authorities the flexibility they need to negotiate contracts which are often particularly complex and sensitive. it will also allow them to ask for specific security clauses to protect classified information and ensure their security of supply. all this makes the directive an instrument which member states can apply to most of their defence procurement without putting at risk their security interests. the new directive will also apply to sensitive procurements in the field of non-military security. this approach is in line with today’s strategic environment, in which transnational threats and new technologies have blurred the dividing line between military and non-military, internal and external security. public procurement cannot ignore these developments: in cases where equipment for police forces, for example, has features similar to those of defence equipment, it is only logical to make the same procurement rules applicable. all this will have major advantages: the new directive will make it possible for member states to limit the use of the derogation under article 296 of the ec treaty to exceptional cases. hence, the principles of the internal market will, at last, apply to important parts of defence and security markets in europe. fair and transparent procurement rules will become applicable throughout the union and allow companies to tender more easily in other member states. european industries will get a much larger ‘home’ market with longer production runs and economies of scale. this, in turn, will reduce costs and lead to lower prices. at the end, we will achieve greater openness of markets to the benefit of all: industries will become more competitive, armed forces will get better value for money, and taxpayers will benefit from more efficiency in public spending. all this is also the fruit of your work and your input. let me therefore once again thank you and congratulate you on this.
the ppe group is in favour of the report. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. a european defence equipment market is not something that is created overnight, but the rapporteur has built up a trusting cooperation which has helped us to take a very important first step together with the council, and not least the commission. defence equipment is now clearly part of the internal market, which should help to bring prices down. the opening up of the market will lead to increased european competitiveness and more efficient production of equipment. the french government also deserves praise for its constructive role, but the rapporteur, mr lambsdorff, has certainly done the most. i would also like to thank the defence industry for its valuable contributions, which have increased flexibility. many elements of the 2004 procurement directive are reproduced here. at the same time, national security interests and special conditions such as security of supply and information are respected. the application of article 296 remains under the existing rules, but abuse is prevented. the industry should welcome it too. the implementation of the directive may continue to influence industry as well as the development of new practices. we in parliament are pleased that the council has agreed with us on thresholds and transparency, to take a few examples of the many successes of the european parliament, imco and the rapporteur. i am personally pleased that the most controversial issues have been resolved with existing ec law. if parliament, as i believe and hope, comes together in broad support for this report, a significant and logical step has been taken. a warm thanks to all colleagues and stakeholders who have participated in a constructive and exciting process.
member of the commission. − while the experiences with budgetary coordination have, overall, been positive, some lessons can be drawn. the analysis of the first 10 years reinforces the case for strengthening the effectiveness of budgetary surveillance in good times. it also shows the need to address broader issues which may affect the macroeconomic stability of a member state and the overall functioning of economic and monetary union. fiscal policy coordination should better guide national budgetary behaviour over the whole cycle – that is, both in good times and in bad times. increased attention should be paid to monitoring public debt developments while medium-term budgetary objectives should be strengthened to address implicit liabilities. all these kinds of developments call for deeper budgetary surveillance. but surveillance should also be broadened to take account of developments within member states that may affect budgetary sustainability, such as the growth of current account deficits, persistent inflation divergences, persistent divergences in the evolution of unit labour costs, or trends of unbalanced growth. surveillance must build on the existing instruments. the key instruments for fiscal policy surveillance and economic policy coordination are clearly anchored in the treaty and, indeed, in the stability and growth pact. the recent adoption of the european economic recovery plan by the commission on 26 november last year also breaks new ground as far as governance and budgetary coordination is concerned. it highlights the commission’s role as a catalyst for short-term emergency economic stabilisation policies. based on our own economic assessment, the commission has quickly set up a fully fledged and quantified economic response to the economic slowdown. the commission’s quick delivery is a response to the clear risks of bigger-than-ever national economic policies at this juncture. the recovery plan recognises the inherent division of tasks embedded into the eu economic policy framework. since member states are responsible for budgetary policy, the commission set a global target for additional fiscal stimulus, taking into account the amount needed to kick-start the eu economy as a whole. member states are free to devise the size and composition of their respective fiscal stimulus. it now implies challenges in terms of coordination and surveillance of the implementation of national measures. the commission and the ecofin council will jointly monitor the implementation of the national measures in compliance with the principles laid down in the recovery plan. looking forward, judicious implementation of the fiscal surveillance framework will anchor expectations of future fiscal developments. together with the strengthening of national budgetary frameworks and rules, and the implementation of reforms curbing the rise in age-related expenditure, this will ensure that sustainable positions are restored. it thus contains adverse effects of expectations of rising deficits and debt on risk premia and private consumption and investment.
member of the commission. − what is the role of the eurogroup? as you know, the eurogroup is right now, and will continue to be after the lisbon treaty comes into force, an informal body. so, the eurogroup cannot adopt formal decisions even when they relate only to euro-area members belonging to the ecofin council. but over the past three or four years, since we began with the presidency of jean-claude juncker, in particular, in january 2005, the eurogroup has improved the content of the discussion agenda. members of the eurogroup together with the commission and the ecb discuss this deeper budgetary surveillance and broader economic surveillance that i mentioned in my introductory remarks. the eurogroup often discusses other issues concerning the external role of the euro and the external dimension of euro are. tax issues, as you know very well, are a very delicate issue. according to the present treaty but also according to the lisbon treaty in the future, tax decisions require unanimity at eu level, and i do not foresee any kinds of changes in the decision-making framework in the near future.

for the verts/ale group. president. - the debate is closed. i would like to thank the commission for its statement. i think it is good that you are raising environmental issues, for example. we have a growing problem with the refineries in terms of contamination of pharmaceuticals. i want to see the strictest possible restrictions on advertising for medicines. i'm getting pretty tired of this myself. one night i was watching television, and a quarter of all the commercials were for over-the-counter medications. i would like the commission to carry out an impact assessment to see how much advertising has affected the sale of these medicines. is it reasonable that we have so much drug advertising on television, even for over-the-counter medicines? in sweden, we had an example of the gardasil vaccine, which hardly falls within the scope of over-the-counter or epidemiological vaccines. i would therefore like to see us tighten up the legislation in this area. as far as the falsification of medicines is concerned, i think it also has to do with our current patent system. it's going to be a very big difference in price. i think that a solution to better manage research needs in the public interest might be to introduce more price funds for medicines. for example, neglected tropical diseases, an area where there is no purchasing power, could then have access to new medicines. medicines for groups of society that do not have strong purchasing power could be better researched. i would like the commission to look more closely at the possibility of using prize funds more widely, which means that the inventor of a medicinal product receives a lump sum. then it would be a license-free manufacturing. i believe that in some cases it may be a better system to improve research and, above all, to remove the need for falsifying medicines. i see many advantages in trying to politically direct research to solve a disease problem instead of having a commercially profitable symptomatic treatment with medication for 30 years. finally, i would just like to say something about nanotechnology. there are nanoparticles in medicine, but we have little knowledge of their toxicological effects. i wish we had better methods to investigate the toxicology of nanoparticles.
madam president, i would like to thank you. commissioner verheugen, this is the commission. as you say, there has been a lot of talk about this package even before it was tabled. perhaps this is not so strange, since drugs affect all people. at the same time, however, they also affect powerful companies with strong interests and the use of tax money. i myself have asked the commission to include the rapporteur. - madam president! - what is it? i would like the french presidency to be here tonight, because i have to say that we have had an extremely good cooperation. without their commitment and without their will to find ways forward, we would never have been able to reach an agreement. in the course of the process, there was always a blocking minority on some issue. it is therefore thanks to very good cooperation with the french presidency that this legislation is now coming into being, which is very good. so we're talking about dichloromethane, which is a dye-removing agent. it's also an industrial chemical that's actually used very heavily in the pharmaceutical industry. however, in industrial applications there is a good possibility to protect workers and the environment during use. the problem arises mainly when the product is sold to consumers. it's a sneaky chemical in the sense that it's carcinogenic, it's narcotic, it's harmful to health. it's easy to fool. because by the time you smell it, you've already exceeded the limit by three times. that makes it extremely difficult to protect oneself. proper protective equipment consists of very sophisticated gloves that must be changed every three hours. you need equipment that normally costs about 2 700 euros with independent air systems. the use of this chemical today is largely due to its illegal use. therefore, it was also important to restrict and prohibit the use of the product to professional users. often, it is a one-man or a few-man company that goes out and cleans up stains or removes paint. the safety equipment is very often left at home or not available at all. banning this chemical is therefore largely a matter of worker safety. we know that in the countries where it is used - which is 24 of the 27 member states today - it is almost never used correctly in accordance with national and european legislation. i think it is enough to quote the german chemical industry association's own text, which states that despite good ventilation, the removal of paint in limited areas and the collection of paint residues removed and the immediate closure of the cans, the exposure limit is regularly exceeded. that's why you need a separate respirator. i think it is very good that the commission has put forward a proposal and that we have now reached a compromise which also prohibits in practice even professional use, with the possibility for countries to obtain national exemptions. however, those granting an exemption must ensure that those working with this chemical have appropriate protective equipment, have sufficient training and knowledge of the alternatives and can justify why they cannot use these alternatives. in fact, for all uses of dichloromethane, there are viable alternatives today. we're talking about the 5 percent that's used in the dangerous way, which is the paint removal. the remaining 95% of the volume of dcm used is used in industry. it is good that we are also strengthening the protection of workers and the environment there. overall, i'm actually very happy with the deal. this will improve the ability of people to remove paint safely without exposure to carcinogens and hazardous chemicals. my colleagues in this parliament have contributed to the fact that this agreement has been reached so quickly, for which i thank them very much, and that all the shadow rapporteurs, including myself, have been able to reach agreement with the council. this is a good sign. because this was the last chance before reach to ban chemicals the old-fashioned way. so this was a sort of grand finale for the old-fashioned chemicals policy, and it was a good finale that we reached an agreement in an effective way. as regards dcm, some say that the alternatives may be at least as dangerous or more dangerous, but the evaluations of the commission and others have clearly shown that the alternatives are much less dangerous. now we're creating a market for alternatives. in fact, the companies that are now 'recycling' are also in many cases manufacturing the alternatives. however, there are also smaller companies that manufacture alternatives. it is good that they are now able to use their competitive advantage of better environmental protection in the internal market. we are moving towards a more secure future, and i thank everyone who has been involved in this process.
for the gue/ngl group. president. - the debate is closed. dichloromethane, dcm, is a dangerous chemical that can cause cancer, eye damage, and lead to acute damage to organs such as the heart, liver, and kidneys. dcm is used, inter alia, in the production of pharmaceuticals and as a remover of paint and stickers. some countries have already banned dcm, such as sweden, denmark and austria. it is excellent that the question of the dcm is now on the agenda. it is even better that this agreement implies a total ban on dcm among ordinary consumers. for this i can only commend our rapporteur, carl schlyter, very highly. good work, carl! unfortunately, pharmaceutical workers and those who clean walls and facades may still be at risk from the effects of dichloromethane. unfortunately, this agreement does not imply a total ban on the professional use of dcm. it is a major shortcoming for which i hold the commission fully responsible. however, this derogation is designed so that the hopefully few member states who want to use the dcm must ensure that workers are not affected. the burden of proof therefore lies on those countries that want limited use of dcm to prove that the product will be used as safely as possible and that the protection of workers is guaranteed. it's quite acceptable after all. this is, on the whole, a good deal. i would urge the commission to draw inspiration from this decision. yes, we can! now let's move on. can the commission please give us an indication that we might in future have more bans on dangerous substances, such as carcinogenic azo dyes, bisphenol a, the flame retardant deca-bd? if the eu cannot, why can you not at least allow individual member states to go ahead and introduce their own bans? you in the commission are even forcing member states to lift the restrictions they sometimes already have. for example, my own country, sweden, had to allow azo dyes after sweden joined the eu in 1995. following the commission's threat of legal action in the ec court of justice, sweden has now started to allow deca-bd. it is unacceptable and, above all, it is not environmentally friendly. that is not the way to make progressive environmental legislation. the commission, commissioner verheugen, please convince me! show that environmental considerations can take precedence over market requirements in more cases than just this one.
the rapporteur. - madam president! - what is it? i want to go back to what watson said. watson has also played a role. even though you're not a member of our committee, you've still influenced our committee and helped to bring about a compromise. of course, the staff who helped me reach this agreement have also been important. i can only confirm what commissioner verheugen says. at any rate, the commission has always made it clear to me that if the council and parliament had reached a more far-reaching compromise of a total ban, the commission would have accepted it. there has been no misunderstanding between the commission and myself on this point. i just want to illustrate this chemical. if i were to open a single one-kilogram jar here and now and spread it out over the benches, paint it out, we would actually in this whole incredibly large hall exceed the limit a little bit. that's how toxic this chemical is. i can only conclude the debate by appealing to the commission now to ensure that member states seeking exemptions for professional use are allowed to withdraw their exemptions if they regularly breach the new stricter rules. we know, we all know and all the studies show that if dichloromethane is to be used properly to protect the health of workers, it is both uneconomical and unenvironmentally harmful. if dcm is allowed to act under correct market conditions, i.e. if the legislation is followed, dcm itself would be competitive out of the picture very quickly and would be replaced by the alternatives. i can appeal to the commission now: make sure the rules are followed. then the dcm will phase itself out fast enough.
the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments. it is very true that we are in the midst of two crises: a climate crisis and a financial crisis. to this we can add an energy crisis. so it's important not to lose focus. my question to prime minister topolánek is therefore as follows: will the czech republic ensure that the emergency economic crisis programmes also work to solve the climate crisis? we now have the opportunity to help our citizens to have a better environment and new jobs, and to help our small businesses. history shows that every financial crisis is followed by a technological change. with investments in new green technologies such as district heating, biofuels, combined heat and power, energy efficient houses, solar panels, smart grids, etc., we are taking advantage of the necessary transitions to meet the climate challenge and create the economic recovery that europe and the world need. a simultaneous investment in climate-smart technologies and employment also stabilises the security situation vis-à-vis russia. those who opposed the energy package and a common european comprehensive energy policy may now understand why we have put so much effort into being able to get the eu to speak with one voice on these issues. at any rate, the freezing citizens of europe understand what needs to be done. we cannot have three or four european delegations running around like in the middle east crisis. i therefore welcome the czech presidency and the well-established cooperation with the incoming swedish presidency and wish them well.

madam president, i would like to thank you. i too welcome the czech presidency. under this presidency we will have low growth and unemployment will rise. we're in a very difficult financial situation. i read in the financial times last week about president klaus's solutions, with which i do not agree at all. he says we must reduce our ambitions in environmental and climate policy, and we must reduce our ambitions in social policy. this is the wrong way to go! if europe is to be competitive in the future, we must invest in modern environmental technologies. we need to invest in modern infrastructure to achieve a long-term sustainable society, and in an active climate policy. it leads to higher growth and more employment. the same is true of social policy. we must invest in a social policy with fair working conditions, equal treatment of employees, a good working environment, etc. that does not mean lower growth, but it does mean more sustainable growth in the long term. finally, i would like to touch on family policy. when i read on your programme about family policy, it's about an old-fashioned family policy. modern family policy is about equal responsibility for children and work for men and women. you're pointing in a completely different direction in your program.
in writing. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of the environment against the risks arising from the use of radioactive substances. the report clearly states that the member states have exclusive competence in matters of defence and national security, which we think is important to stress. we believe that equipment, works and services should normally be procured in this area as well. however, we believe that it is a natural consequence of the nature of this market that not everything can be procured in accordance with the rules of the directive. however, these exemptions shall apply only where substantial security concerns can be justified. we believe that this will help to put an end to the customary use of protectionist exceptions, which are not least harmful to swedish industry.
in writing. the commission also proposed that the council adopt a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens at work. this convention dates from 2007 and deals with important issues such as the working environment, rest periods and social security of fishermen. it shall be for the member states to decide democratically whether or not to ratify the ilo convention in question. i have therefore voted against the report in question, since the matter does not belong on the european parliament's table.

for the ind/dem group. president. - the debate is closed. the doharunda collapsed last year. this is deeply serious. the progress towards global free trade in recent decades has lifted untold numbers of people out of poverty, on a scale that has actually changed the world. but now the world economy is in a very deep crisis. it's not because of free trade and that form of globalization. it's because of a global financial crisis. the situation is therefore similar to that of the late 20th century. a global depression follows such a financial crisis. that time it brought hitler to power. it led to the horrors of world war ii, and it led to fifty years of communist slavery in half of europe and half of asia. we're talking about important things right now. the main reason it became a global depression at the time was a resurgence of protectionism. countries introduced tariffs, quantitative restrictions, buy-in rules and competing devaluations. in fact, there is a real risk that this will happen again. there are many worrying signs. president obama has actually won the election on a protectionist platform. we have the first signs. now here comes a big package and there is at least one "buy american" clause in the steel industry. this could be a start. if that gate has been opened, other countries will feel they can do something similar given how bad it looks. the countries that are now suffering very badly around the world and in the eu are tempted to promise their workers and their companies protection from foreign competition. the trends are clear. if that process starts, it can't be stopped, and then, in fact, there's a disaster waiting. the eu is the world's largest trading bloc and therefore has a very large responsibility. in the field of trade policy, the eu speaks with one voice, and that is good for once, but what will that voice actually say? i would like to begin by agreeing with the previous speaker that the doha round is extremely important, but i would add that it has probably never been more important than it is at the moment. in this particular financial crisis, i think we have a greater need than ever to show that the global trading system actually works. to put the doha round behind us and conclude that we are not capable of global trade deals, i think it would be a disaster that could undermine the entire global trading system. a failure of the dohar round will probably never cost more than it is right now. the reason why the doha round is more important than ever is precisely because of the financial crisis. the biggest risk, as i see it, with this financial crisis is not the lack of capital to the loan market. world economy, and it happened in the 1970s as well. already, i think, we can see a tendency for the world to think that it can solve these fundamental problems with more protectionism, even though sector, we are seeing protectionism increase at a very rapid rate. back to the dohar round as it is today. the criticism i have had throughout the period we have been negotiating the doha round is mr president, when we are discussing the strategic energy review, i think it is important to stress some of the risks we are running – not only the risk of an insecure energy supply, with all the problems that brings, but also the fact that we are exposed to political pressure from regimes which use energy supplies as a lever to influence other governments. as a result, there is also a risk of fragmentation of the european union, with member states divided according to their different interests, thereby undermining the common foreign and security policy. i think it is a good thing to see that the policies we need to tackle climate change are very much the same as the policies we need in order to strengthen our energy security. decreasing the use of fossil fuels means less dependence on unreliable suppliers. increased supply from other energy sources means a decreasing demand for fossil fuels, lower prices for european citizens and – not least – a reduced cash flow to the oil regimes of the world. this has huge security implications which we need to consider when we discuss the european union’s future energy strategy. i think some of the pieces will easily fall into place when we have this perspective. we need more of an internal market in the european union because, in reality, that is the only guarantee for solidarity between the member states. this means that we need to do more about cross-border connections and have a better grid, binding member states together and thereby binding the market together. we need to develop more biofuels. i do not agree with those who say that there is a risk; it is possible for us – in europe as well as in other parts of the world – to increase the area we are using. even small contributions from biofuels result in less dependence on fossil fuels and a change in prices. i must also emphasise the issue of nuclear power. sometimes i feel it is the elephant in the room, because it is one of the greatest potential contributors to the european union’s ability to reduce carbon dioxide emissions today and in the future. i would like to conclude on that note. if we try to bring all these elements together, we will have the opportunity not only for a stronger energy policy, but also for a stronger security policy.

for the gue/ngl group. the report has the support of the united left. the sexual exploitation of children and child pornography are heinous crimes that require international cooperation to stop. children are abused by being forced to pose sexually, photographed, filmed, and posted on the internet. these films and images can be seen all over the world and therefore require increased international cooperation to stop these violations. we know there are clear links between the sex slave trade and the sexual exploitation of children. the united nations estimates that 85 percent of the victims of the sex slave trade are children under the age of 18. we don't know how many children are being bought and sold as commodities for sexual exploitation, but we know that there are many, and we know that every child who is affected is one child too many. adults must take responsibility to protect our children from this, one of the worst crimes a child can be exposed to. we must not forget in this debate that many of the crimes against children are committed within the family or within the family circle of friends, and therefore it is important that society ensures that there are other adult contacts for children, so that children have someone to turn to for help. all forms of sexual abuse of children are criminal offences and should be treated as such in all member states. all citizens who commit sexual offences against children should be punished, whether the offence is committed within or outside the eu.
i would like to begin by thanking claudio and all the others who have made this report possible. in the past, in the debates on the future of asylum, refugee and migration policy, we have focused mostly on those who are here illegally, or on those who are well educated and are allowed to come here, or the group who were just poor and want to provide for their families and want to come here. now we're passing the responsibility on to the employers who hire illegal immigrants. there wouldn't be so many undocumented people living here if there weren't always some rogue employers who pay and exploit. i think that the sanctions are quite right and that an information requirement for employers is being introduced and that if you have exploited someone you should also compensate them after the person has gone home. there is, however, one proposal which i would have voted against if it had not been for a vote in committee, and that is that the majority introduce more lenient rules for employment in the home. for me, this is a gender equality issue, because it's mostly women who worked in low-wage homes. it is also a group that is in a more difficult situation than if you are in a multi-employee workplace, but i see this as a first step. i regret the news today that the swedish conservative government is opposed to the report which aims to support serious businesses and to criminalise frivolous businesses which exploit undocumented workers. finally, i am of course also concerned about the views we have received from the trade unions on the report. i hope we can resolve these before any decisions are made. the same rules must apply to all employers, as well as shared responsibility in the case of public works, otherwise we will be building loopholes and encouraging cheating.
we know that more and more people are hiding within the eu's borders. we know that the eu will need an increase in labour immigration in the near future. we also know that thousands of people every week are trying to seek a better life in europe. many of them die on their way to our continent. we know that many of the undocumented are exploited and live in miserable conditions. this is unworthy of the european union. among the universal values that the eu wants to spread throughout the world is the right of everyone to live in dignity. there is a shameful paradox here. the eu's common asylum and migration policy is a reasonable arrangement, but it must not mean that the countries that want to take a tougher line and chase people in different ways get to decide. the chances of a decent life in europe will be diminished. at the same time, it is important that prospective employers who exploit people in a dependency situation know that penalties and sanctions can be imposed throughout the eu. i admit that the report is a difficult balancing act. i can sympathize with mr. catania's reasoning on the reservation. the compromise is a step in the right direction, although i have some reservations about the extent of the employer's reporting obligations. i would like to say to inger segelström that we do not have a conservative government in sweden. we have a four-party government with strong liberal elements.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of radioactive waste, which is a matter for the member states. we support joint research on nuclear safety, for example, but we think that in several cases the report goes too far in the nuclear-friendly direction. those decisions shall be taken at the level of the member states. we are also generally in favour of investments in energy infrastructure, but we are questioning whether we should support all the projects and investments that the rapporteur wants. we would have liked to see clearer criteria for supporting such a position, particularly in the light of the debate on nord stream.
in writing. - we were very critical of anna záborská's report and we were going to vote against it because we thought it was very anti-women. the woman would take care of the home, children and the elderly instead of working. fortunately, we did not have to vote against the resolution, as the verts/ale amendment was accepted. although we ultimately decided to support the resolution, there were texts that we were against or doubted, and therefore it was not obvious how we should vote. as swedish social democrats, we believe that the right to work should apply to everyone. the social partners must also provide the tools and conditions for women to work, which is a prerequisite for their liberation. a well-developed child and elderly care system is one of the most important conditions for women to be able to work. of course there must be solidarity between the generations, but this solidarity must not lead to women being forced to stay at home and look after the elderly and children. we believe, however, that the resolution adopted by the majority sends a clear signal to the czech presidency that their aim of equating home care with work is both outdated and very anti-women.
in writing. - gender equality and equal treatment in the labour market, as in all other contexts, are self-evident in a democracy. the rapporteur is right, of course, on this point. but as usual, the measures proposed to redress the backsliding on human rights and democracy are seen as aimed at increasing the eu's political power at the expense of the member states. it always ends with an attack on subsidiarity. in practice, it is proposed that the eu should assume responsibility for the social policies of the member states and legislate on matters closely linked to labour market policy. the commission has also adopted a number of proposals for a common tax policy. all these are examples of policy issues which the member states should decide on their own. i have therefore, despite many of the good intentions, voted against both the own-initiative report and the alternative motion for a resolution.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the protection of the environment from harmful organisms. the fight against the spread of child pornography must be given priority. in this context, european cooperation in its various forms has a very important role to play. that is why we moderates voted for the report. we would like to point out, however, that we did not share the rapporteur's view on two of the many proposals presented. unlike the rapporteur, we do not believe that the strict confidentiality of certain professional groups, such as lawyers, priests and psychologists, should be compromised. we also believe that it is difficult to hold the owner of a website strictly responsible for all discussions that take place even in private conversations in closed rooms on a website. despite the purpose, it is not proportionate to require all website owners to monitor all private conversations on the website in order to guarantee the legality of the website in accordance with the proposal. instead, we need to focus on other and more effective ways of tackling child pornography networks that do not have such serious consequences for the privacy of ordinary internet users.
in writing. − the sexual exploitation of children and child pornography are abominable crimes and international cooperation is necessary to put a stop to them. therefore we voted in favour of the angelilli report today. however, there are aspects of the report that we do not support, such as creating uniform extraterritorial criminal legislation applicable throughout the eu, as well as defining at eu level what should be considered a crime and aggravating circumstances.
in writing. - sexual crimes against children and child pornography are among the most heinous crimes that humans commit. it's a crime that should result in severe criminal penalties or extensive serious care if the perpetrator is mentally ill. the report proposes many good measures to improve the management of this appalling social problem. member states are encouraged to ratify and implement all relevant international conventions, they should be helped to improve their legislation in this area and child sex tourism should be criminalised in all member states. this is exactly my view of the eu as a union of values. i very much support the report and have voted in favour of it in many individual votes. however, the report also calls for the harmonisation of eu criminal law and the establishment of a system of preventive measures to be financed from eu funds, despite the fact that this is a global problem that should be regulated by conventions and agreements at un level. it is difficult to avoid the impression that we are once again faced with an example of a terrible social problem being cynically used to advance the positions of the eu at the expense of the independence of the member states. the criminal justice system is a very central part of the powers of a sovereign state. i have therefore voted against the report as a whole.

for the ind/dem group. i think we're all very relieved by what's happened in the united states. it is similar to the situation we had in the early 1950s when the american people and the american system showed its ability to deal with mccarthyism. now it's doing it by electing a new president who on his first day in office declares guantánamo bay to be closed. thank god for this. what's wrong with this is that you arrest people on suspicion of terrorism or other crimes. you try them, you marry them, you flog them, you release them, you punish them. those who have not been convicted shall be treated as innocent. if we have suspicions, it is the task of the security service to follow up in the future. i find it difficult to understand why those who have not been convicted should not be allowed to remain in the united states, but of course european countries themselves should be prepared to take in those who have not been convicted. thank you for speaking.

for the gue/ngl group. finally, after more than one and a half years of work, we are going to decide on this climate resolution. i would like to mention three things in particular. the long-term reduction targets: in article 3 we call for reductions in eu emissions of between 25 and 40% by 2020 and at least 80% by 2050. that is good, and it means that we are going further than what the eu climate package from last year requires. we also call for action against the meat industry. according to the fao report livestock's long shadow, the meat industry accounts for 18 percent of global emissions. we are making a point of this report and we are calling for a reduction in meat consumption. it is a courageous and historic decision by this parliament, which is usually happy to support subsidies to this industry, but it is sad that the ppe in particular wants to vote those requirements down. just as we must reduce motoring, we should also dare to say that today's rampant consumption of meat is not sustainable. let me also remind you that the meat industry is actually the main cause of the deforestation of the amazon rainforest in the form of land for grazing animals but also for fodder production. a large proportion of the feed is exported as soya to europe. it's not sustainable. the automobile industry is another growing problem. between 1990 and 2005, emissions from the european transport industry have increased by 32%. massive investments are needed in public transport and other environmentally friendly modes of transport. fossil fuel cars must be replaced by electric cars and perhaps in the future also by hydrogen vehicles. we have to ask ourselves if it's sustainable to have all this transport. should we not encourage local production and consumption instead? in conclusion, i would like to draw attention to amendments nos 12 and 28, which call for more nuclear power. if they pass, my group will not be able to support this resolution. i therefore vote against amendments nos 12 and 28. thank you so much. thank you.
this report shows great promise. it covers pretty much every topic that has been mentioned in the climate debate for over two years. yet i think it lacks the edge and the drive and the pressure in this work that is actually needed to follow the political line that we have started in europe on climate issues. there are no measures to protect the soil. as far as water resources are concerned, there is a whole package of proposals in the world water forum that could have been raised. in the field of energy efficiency, there are opportunities that are not available where we have decisionmaking powers in parliament. alternative fuels are also treated far too lightly. in the important area of health, the focus is on the collection of facts and the control of mosquito bites, whereas we need major strategic decisions in europe to cope with the effects of climate change on human health. it's also true that there are opportunities here. we should have done more in terms of growth and jobs. there is a possibility of creating jobs in europe. they're needed here. we need to anchor these measures in economic policy. in a few weeks, the council of ministers will meet to discuss financing for the decisions to be taken in copenhagen. i am not sure that the commissioner has been able to give us a clear answer to this question. without this anchoring in economic policy and in a policy for employment and growth, this report risks becoming 'like a candle in the wind', a nice document, but without the drive and determination we need in these matters.
madam president, i would like to thank mr florenz. this is a rich report with a great number of concrete proposals. a specific call is made to use the stimulus packages all over the world to promote clean energy and green technologies, stressing thereby that the financial crisis and the climate crisis have the same roots – unsustainable use of resources. while i support the report, like caroline lucas i would have liked to see more attention paid to the most recent signs, which tell us that climate change is both more rapid and serious than we thought just a couple of years ago – contrary to what some colleagues here have said, in particular mr helmer. by the way, a recent screening of more than 900 climate articles in scientific journals – peer review – showed that not one of them questioned the main thrust of the ipcc. what i am particularly concerned about is not co2 emissions per se, but the positive feedback mechanisms that are now happening in the planetary system, like the acidification of the oceans, reduced albedo and the possible leaking of methane from thawing tundra. all these factors will accelerate warming. we can control emissions but we cannot control these factors. this is the main reason why, in my opinion, emission reductions have to be much more ambitious in the near future than what is currently being discussed in the eu and by the un. this means, by the way, that the 2°c target has to be revisited and that greenhouse gas concentrations have to be lowered, rather than continuing to increase. that is why some of us advocate very strongly the 350 ppm target. this dimension of the problem is referred to in the report but only in passing. i would have liked it to be at the core of the report. my guess is that, only a few years from now, the feedbacks i mentioned will be at the centre of the debate. finally, let me just endorse what guido sacconi said. in spite of its shortcomings, a temporary committee has been the right way to deal with a horizontal issue like this. i hope the next parliament will build on our experience and deal with climate change and sustainability in a similar way.
now i hope the whole parliament will listen! at the january partsession we unanimously adopted a resolution on the horn of africa. it contained a specific clause regarding the release of dawit isaak. dawit isaak is now very ill and i would ask the president to write to the eritrean authorities. the news of his illness has been confirmed by several sources and is in the swedish media today. the situation is very urgent and desperate, and i just want to remind you that dawit isaak is a swedish-eritrean journalist who has been imprisoned without trial since 2001. now his health problems are so serious that he is being held in a military hospital, and i fear for his life. i appeal for support for the release of dawit isaak. in writing. - there is no doubt that the climate is changing. however, it is not clear whether this is mainly or substantially due to human influence or whether it is mainly or substantially part of a natural process. uncertainty is significant both about what is going on and about what should be done. but it is precisely the uncertainty that suggests that we should take the first steps to reduce carbon dioxide emissions into the atmosphere, for example. i have therefore voted in favour of reducing these by 20% by 2020. the european parliament's ad hoc committee on climate change has now produced a report on how the eu should act on climate change. the report is very fragmented. it seems that the members involved are indulging special interests, such as agriculture and tourism. at the same time, it is also calling for more funding and new mechanisms, and in practice proposing major steps towards a centrally planned economy with brussels-led propaganda campaigns in schools and recreation centres. the report is so far removed from the core issues that i have had to vote against it. we cannot go on saying yes to everything that is put forward to demonstrate our legitimate concern, uncertainty and willingness to act on the climate issue.

madam president, on a point of order, i refer to rule 142(2)(a) and (b) on the allocation of speaking time. yesterday when we were discussing the guantánamo prison, here in the chamber, i and several other speakers were interrupted without mercy when we had exceeded our speaking time by a few seconds. that harsh treatment was meted out by mr pöttering and mr siwiec, the vice-president replacing him later in the afternoon. mr schulz, the socialist group leader, on the other hand, was permitted by mr pöttering to exceed his time limit by far more than a minute. now, i would not dream of insinuating that this was because mr pöttering and mr schulz are buddies – alte kameraden as one might put it in german – but i do spot a recurring pattern here. colleagues from big groups elaborating the political message the chair wants to hear are treated with great generosity. colleagues from smaller groups elaborating the political message the chair does not want to hear are treated with great meanness. now that is in breach of the rules of procedure, where it is clearly stated how speaking time should be allocated. i want to remind mr pöttering and all his vice-presidents – (the president informed the speaker that he had exceeded his speaking time.) i was sent here to defend subsidiarity and the sovereignty of member countries and the president and the vice-presidents of this parliament have no right whatsoever to try and silence the voice of 15% of the swedish electorate.

i was born in the balkans. i followed the unrest in kosovo closely in the late 1980s. i have seen slovenia and croatia become independent states and experienced war in the early 1990s. i myself experienced the war in bosnia and eventually left the country as a refugee. i know it is so incredibly easy to start a war, but i also know that it is much harder to make peace and restore people's trust in each other. kosovo is today in a situation which will be crucial for future generations of kosovo in terms of the very re-establishment of trust between different ethnic groups. i am glad that mr lagendijk has been very clear in his resolution that we should put the discussions on kosovo's independence and the conflicts surrounding it behind us. time and effort should now be devoted to the dialogue on how to strengthen the equal right of all people to live in peace, to work and to create a better future for themselves in kosovo. we must concentrate on the effective protection of minorities, the improvement of the economic situation, the fight against widespread corruption and organised crime. all people in kosovo should make it their own mission to help end inter-ethnic violence. the courts should ensure that war crimes are brought to trial. some members of this parliament deplore the eu's presence and involvement in kosovo, but we who have experienced the balkan wars deplore the fact that the eu has not been involved both more clearly and more fully. the work that remains is enormous and it will take time, but it is ultimately about rebuilding trust between people so that the new generations have a chance to educate themselves, live and work together in peace and with respect for each other's differences. that is what the whole european idea is about. (applause) i would like to thank you.
sweden, where i come from, is, like many other countries, heavily dependent on the automobile industry. volvo and saab are well-known brands. the swedish automotive industry, like the rest of the automotive industry, has been hit hard by the crisis. several factors have contributed to the crisis, but a particular factor is the lack of an early and necessary conversion of vehicle production. a shift to smaller, more energy-efficient and environmentally friendly vehicles is necessary. the eu has long called for unilateral flexibility on the part of workers. i and the united left demand that we also demand flexibility from the management of the large companies. so the lack of flexibility and innovation has contributed to the crisis we see in the automotive industry and other industries. finally, the automotive industry is an important workplace, an often predominantly male workplace, and it has all our support. i therefore assume that the eu is showing the same commitment when we have a crisis and a downturn in the so-called women's workplace.

in writing. the junilists believe that the common agricultural policy (cap) should be abolished and that agricultural products should be sold on a free market without the eu devoting financial resources to information campaigns and promotion measures for these products. the eu's financing of promotion measures in third countries is particularly serious, as it creates unfair competition with agricultural producers in non-eu countries. what is the eu doing? is it really reasonable for the eu to use european taxpayers' money in advertising campaigns to convince the same citizens to buy the goods they have already subsidised? of course not. i'm not going to. there is a stench of hidden protectionism about the whole proposal. in january 2009, a new advertising campaign was launched in sweden, finland, and denmark, urging swedes to buy more tulips. according to resumé magazine, the eu is investing a total of $1.4 million over three years in the tulip campaign in the three countries mentioned. this blatant waste of eu money must come to an end. i am strongly opposed to this report. i would like to say again that it is fortunate that the european parliament does not have a vote on the eu's agricultural policy, because otherwise the union would fall into a trap of protectionism and heavy subsidies to all groups in the agricultural sector.
in writing. - this report contains several points which i fully support, such as the dignity of asylum seekers and the need to guarantee human rights at all times. the criticism of a number of so-called asylum centres is justified. several european countries are failing to receive asylum seekers and refugees in a dignified manner. however, the report contains some points which i cannot support. the idea is that the eu should have a common immigration and asylum policy. it also states that the other member states will support those eu countries that 'most face the challenges of immigration'. the junilists believe that asylum and immigration policy is a matter for the member states, provided that international conventions and agreements are respected. a common immigration and asylum policy risks leading to a 'fortress europe', of which we are already seeing clear signs.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of animal by-products in the production of foodstuffs. however, the amendments in the report also contain details which should be handled by officials at the level of the authorities and not by politicians. for example, the references to the quotations: oral feeding: the delivery of feed by mouth to the gastrointestinal tract of the animal in order to meet the nutritional needs of the animal and/or to maintain the productivity of healthy animals or to mineral-containing broilers or to 'trachees, urine and separated gastrointestinal contents after emptying or removal of the digestive tract, irrespective of any treatment or interference. these are certainly important questions in relation to food safety, but should be left to the experts of national authorities. i voted in favour of the report because it contains some important proposals in principle, but that does not mean that i support its approach of going into detail.
writer. - i wish we had discussed the philippines instead, where the eu could have made a real contribution to saving many lives. we are now discussing a particular case in an ongoing court proceeding, where we have the courage to say that we will stand up for the rule of law. where i come from, one of the most fundamental principles of the rule of law is that a parliament does not interfere in individual cases in a court of law. both i and my group believe that it is completely wrong to bring up an individual ongoing judicial process in a parliament. unfortunately, this is not the first or last time, because we are about to vote on the medina report and they are doing exactly the same thing. in an ongoing court case in a swedish court against pirate bay in a copyright case, parliament gives its opinion on the question of liability during the ongoing court case. i really hope that this does not become a habit, because then we in europe would be the ones to challenge and fight our own rule of law, and that would be very unfortunate. thank you, thank you! sorry, i forgot something important. if we are dissatisfied with the way brazil and europe handle extradition and if we are dissatisfied with the way our laws are interpreted in a national court, we should change the laws so that they are equal for all. we're not going in there and trying to influence an individual case. that is the job of the judges, the prosecutors and the defence, not that of parliament. we make laws, and the rule of law says that the courts interpret them.
in writing. the situation in the gaza strip is very serious, as the civilian population is deprived of food, medicine and fuel as a result of the ongoing conflict. the situation is so serious that immediate and external assistance is required. that is why we voted in favour of the resolution. we find it very regrettable, but unfortunately not very surprising, that the european parliament is once again using a disaster to slowly but surely move its positions forward.
for the ind/dem group. is there a need for an economic european legal unit in the field of research, or is this yet another example of the eu's stubborn struggle against european pluralism? the truth is that successful institutional reform requires institutional competition. what if, 50 years ago, an international legal form had been established for research institutions! if that were the case, development in the area would have come to a standstill. amending international treaties is difficult and therefore slow. progress is made by countries being able to easily reform their national institutions. successful reforms then spread to other countries. the commission proposal is not a straitjacket. it offers an alternative to existing national proposals and is therefore an improvement. but the proposal is completely ruined by the fact that the commission also wants to regulate the taxation of this legal entity at eu level. therefore, the proposal must be rejected.
the committee on the internal market and consumer protection has tabled an opinion. - the report shows that tax issues are sensitive, and i respect that. but at the same time i think that we in the responsible econ committee could have handled the matter better. we are now issuing a text of a resolution, and that is good. the single market must not be disturbed by unfair and unfair competition. there is reason to repeat this in these days when state aid seems to be in the doldrums. however, the eu needs a more sober view of how reduced vat rates can be used to promote jobs and growth, not least in the local market and for labour-intensive services. that is what the commission's proposal is about, that is to say more jobs but also countering the black sector. we therefore need lower vat on, for example, restaurant services, on home care services for the young, sick, disabled and elderly, on hairdressing services and on new goods such as audio books, which is a very important issue in my own country, cds, baby car seats and diapers, home services such as renovation, repair and cleaning. in the imco committee we followed the commission's line and added amendments nos 6 and 7 in the name of malcolm harbour. i would also like to say that it is not easy to understand how these texts and the voting procedure are handled in this parliament. in the first amendment we stress the importance of reducing the black or grey sector. in the second amendment we want to see the possibility of having a lower vat rate on renovation and repair work to save energy. in these tough economic times, various means must be used to boost the economy, and here lower taxes can be an important method. finally, the promotion of alcohol by astrid lulling is here again. as usual, i'd like to politely but firmly reject her temptation.

in writing. - i regard the eu as a union of values and welcome several of the points in the report which draw our attention to the vulnerability of children around the world. it is clear that society must take responsibility for protecting children's rights, for reducing child mortality in poor countries and for combating all forms of exploitation of children. however, the report also contains some lines which i find difficult to support, including the proposal to appoint an eu special representative responsible for 'ensuring visibility and leadership on children's rights'. the measures needed to safeguard children's rights are and should remain a matter for national parliaments to decide. if international cooperation is deemed necessary, it should be sought within the framework of the un, not the eu. i have voted in favour of the report despite these reservations.
in writing. - we have today voted against the report by mr toia on the social economy (a6-0015/2009). we agree that the social economy plays an important role in today's society, but we cannot support it being subject to different rules than other businesses. it is not possible to draw a clear line between different companies and we do not believe that cooperative and mutual societies should be given preference over, for example, small entrepreneurs in terms of access to finance and other forms of support.
in writing. - we moderates voted today in favour of the own-initiative report on mental health by evangelia tzampazi (a6-0034/2009). we respect the decision of the member states to enter into the european pact for mental health and well-being mentioned in the report. we consider it natural that the member states should cooperate and exchange experience in this area on their own terms. however, we are opposed to the wording of the report which calls for european guidelines for media monitoring of mental health. we do not share the view that this should be done at european level, but we defend a free and independent media.
