in writing. - we moderates voted today in favour of the own-initiative report on applied research in the common fisheries policy by rosa miguélez ramos (a6-0016/2009). we share the basic view of the report that fisheries policy should be based on science and that a consensus between fishermen and scientists is the basis for a policy based on sustainability. however, we believe that the seventh framework programme, with its priorities and limitations, should be fixed.
in writing. - i have voted in favour of the report by mr ramos on applied research in the common fisheries policy. it is important to support increased research efforts on the impact of fisheries policy on fish stocks, ecosystems and biodiversity. i am therefore deeply critical of parts of the eu's fisheries policy. however, miguelez ramos stresses the importance of policy decisions being based on science and long-term sustainability, and not primarily on the short-term interests of the fishing industry, which i see as positive. i am, however, critical of any interpretation of the report which could mean more money being allocated to financial support for the european fishing industry and giving it a higher status. a clear environmental perspective should permeate all research in the fisheries sector.
in writing. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a fire. it is important to support increased research efforts on the impact of fisheries policy on fish stocks, ecosystems and biodiversity. we are deeply critical of parts of the eu's fisheries policy on environmental grounds. miguelez ramos stresses the importance of policy decisions being based on science and long-term sustainability, and not primarily on the short-term interests of the fishing industry, which we see as positive. we are, however, critical of any interpretation of the report which would mean that more money is allocated to financial support for the european fishing industry and to give it a higher status. a clear environmental perspective should permeate all fisheries research for which additional resources are allocated.
in writing. − the ability for european citizens to work and live in another member state is an important right and i support the intentions to facilitate the mobility of professionals and to guarantee the safety of consumers. however, there are aspects of the cederschiöld report that i do not fully support, such as the formulations regarding abolition of obstacles to the free movement of persons and services, which could be interpreted as a statement against collective agreements in the labour market. regarding the inclusion of health-care professionals in the agreement, i see a risk of deregulation of the healthcare sector. therefore i abstained in the final vote on the report.
in writing. - like the rapporteur, i support the global moratorium on commercial whaling and the ban on international trade in whale products. there are also good reasons to seek an end to what is called 'scientific whaling'. when these issues are dealt with at international level, cooperation should be sought through the international whaling commission (iwc), not through the eu. despite this objection of principle, i have chosen to vote in favour of the report.
in writing. - i voted against this report on community participation in the european audiovisual observatory. it is the responsibility of the member states to engage in and finance the european audiovisual observatory.
in writing. the european parliament has also called for the introduction of a european visa policy, which would allow the eu to grant visas to eu citizens. this is why we finally decided to support the fava report, as it sends a clear message that it is not acceptable for employers to exploit the vulnerability of undocumented immigrants and as it gives rights to the undocumented which are currently lacking in several eu member states. a precondition for our being able to vote in favour of the report was the council's acceptance of parliament's statement that article 9 would in no way be prejudicial in the future. this could make it difficult to adopt common rules on the liability of main contractors for their subcontractors. we take it for granted that the main contractor should be responsible for ensuring that subcontractors comply with collective agreements. employers should not be able to circumvent the application of labour law rules by using a complex chain of subcontractors. however, in this directive the liability is limited to the first level of subcontractors, which we do not see as acceptable. it is important to remember, however, that nothing prevents individual member states from introducing more comprehensive legislation. we are also opposed in principle to exempting private individuals who employ undocumented workers to work in their homes from sanctions. we believe that undocumented workers are equally vulnerable, even if they work for private individuals.
in writing. - i voted in favour of the report on sanctions against employers who employ illegally staying third-country nationals. the aim of the proposal is to combat the exploitation of illegally staying third-country nationals by employers and to strengthen workers' rights. the person to be punished for illegal employment is the employer who breaks the law, not the employee. in some eu countries, this type of legislation is lacking. in others, legislation exists, but it is not implemented or followed. common eu rules to combat the exploitation and employment of illegal labour are therefore a way to raise the bar for many eu countries. the proposal we are voting on today has several shortcomings. if i had my way, a lot of parts would look different. for example, i would like to see stronger protection for women in domestic work. but the alternative we face is no directive at all - and less protection for the 4-8 million illegally staying third country nationals who are currently working illegally in the eu, and are often exploited by employers in a very vulnerable situation. while it is important to combat illegal immigration and the exploitation of illegally staying third-country nationals, it is also important to ensure that opportunities to reside and seek work legally within the eu are strengthened. this is regulated in other directives which we in the european parliament have been involved in negotiating over the last year.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of workers against the risks of exposure to carcinogens. the aim of the proposal is to combat the exploitation of illegally staying third-country nationals by employers and to strengthen workers' rights. the person to be punished for illegal employment is the employer who breaks the law, not the employee. in some eu countries, this type of legislation is lacking. in others, legislation exists, but it is not implemented or followed. common eu rules to combat the exploitation and employment of illegal labour are therefore a way to raise the bar for many eu countries. the proposal we are voting on today has several shortcomings. if we had a choice, many parts of the world would look very different. for example, we would like to see stronger protection for women in domestic work. but the alternative we face is no directive at all - and less protection for the 4-8 million illegally staying third country nationals who are currently working illegally in the eu, and are often exploited by employers in a very vulnerable situation. while it is important to combat illegal immigration and the exploitation of illegally staying third-country nationals, it is also important to ensure that opportunities to reside and seek work legally within the eu are strengthened. this is regulated in other directives which we in the european parliament have been involved in negotiating over the last year.

in writing. − even though there are contentious elements in the cia resolution, like welcoming the eu-us agreements on judicial cooperation and extradition, i still voted in favour of the resolution to support human rights and the attempt to shed light on the extraordinary renditions programme.
in writing. - i have previously expressed my disgust at the information that has emerged regarding the treatment of suspects, but not convicted persons, who have been extradited and in some cases kidnapped by the authorities of different states. it is completely unacceptable and contrary to the principles of modern democracy. the question is not yet settled, which is why it is good to shed light on the events. the resolution of parliament contains several positive elements, but i think that the ambitions of foreign policy are being overreached by including irrelevant documents. this takes the focus away from the core issue, namely the alleged violations of human rights. this is very unfortunate. that is why i voted against the resolution.

in writing. - we support increased cooperation with the mediterranean countries, but we cannot support increased eu power in integration policy (as stated in paragraph 29). we also regret that the report does not make demands on the occupying powers, such as israel and morocco. in a report which talks about strengthening human rights in the mediterranean, this would be a minimum requirement. that is why we voted against it in the vote.
on behalf of the ppe-de group. − mr president, i oppose both parts of mr cohn-bendit’s proposals because less than a month ago we debated this topic and voted on it in the committee on the environment, as we heard was suggested earlier. so i would say it has been done, and especially at this time, at the end of the legislative period, when we hardly have time for important legislative talks, then i think it would be wrong to give this specific topic priority. we cannot give priority to debates, at this time, which have already been dealt with and especially to do that just because the greens feel that they are not getting enough attention in the run-up to the election would be really irresponsible.
madam president, i would like to thank you. many of my colleagues are concerned about the rise of anti-semitism in europe. in my home town of malmö, sweden played against israel in a davis cup tennis match over the weekend. it wasn't a regular game. it was played without an audience because the local political leadership did not believe that security could be achieved, an approach that many of us have criticized. there were demonstrations during the match, some very violent. it is legitimate to criticise the policies of the state of israel, but this criticism must not turn into hatred of jews in general, into anti-semitism. survivors of the holocaust should not have to hear that europe is cursing murderers, murderers when they gather to manifest their support for the state of israel. in the aftermath of the war in gaza, a series of attacks have occurred in the eu against jewish property and jewish interests, including in my home town. all democratic forces must be extremely clear here. europe has a terrible history. it must never be repeated.
for the ind/dem group. president. - the debate is closed. the eu is facing the global financial crisis with a budget designed half a century ago. the rapporteur believes that the eu must deal with the modern globalised world with a budget where virtually all the money is pledged to a crazy agricultural policy and an ineffective regional policy. it's like attacking a modern mechanized robotic brigade with cavalry. and the order of magnitude is grotesquely different. the cost of cleaning up the financial crisis is impossible to estimate at this stage, but one estimate presented gives a figure of 50 000 billion dollars. the total eu budget is only a few tens of thousands of this amount and is therefore already mortgaged to agriculture and regional policy. the eu has required each member state to implement a stimulus package equivalent to just over 1% of its gdp. this is already more than the entire eu budget, which amounts to just under 1%. it is also pathetic to see the way in which these two reports deal with the financing of climate and energy policy. in these areas too, the eu budget is completely negligible. the eu's task is to bring about cooperation and commitment, and these costs must be borne by the member states after being democratically anchored there. the same applies to energy policy. gas pipelines are cheaper to run on land than on the seabed. the fact that russia and germany are building a gas pipeline on the seabed directly between their countries is to exclude them, it is a new rapallo, and the eu is silent. "words fly, but thoughts stand still", says the king in hamlet.
member of the commission. − mr president, first of all i should like to thank the two rapporteurs. ladies first, and not only these days, i will start by thanking ms herczog for the quality of her report on the small business act (sba) and the strong support it provides for the commission’s initiative to establish a comprehensive policy on small- and medium-sized enterprises. i would also like to pay tribute to mr lehne, not only for his efficiency and work in steering the private company statute through the consultation procedure, but also for the interesting and creative solutions presented in the report. we are now really in a critical phase. we listen every day to the messages, such as the need to boost and renew confidence in the functioning of the global economy. the financial and economic crisis has hit european businesses really hard, and i believe that it is now more important than ever to exploit to the full the potential of small- and medium-sized enterprises. this is the very lifeline of the european economy. the small business act, which the commission adopted in june 2008, provides a comprehensive framework of short-, medium- and long-term actions for sme growth and competitiveness within the lisbon strategy. it proposes 10 policy principles and a number of concrete initiatives to make sure that the concerns of smes are placed at the centre of decision-making – as ms herczog said, mainstreaming smes into the centre of attention and strategies. the sba includes a set of legislative initiatives, including the proposal for a european private company statute. the sba is designed to ensure that the ‘think small first’ principle and priority for small businesses are lastingly and irreversibly anchored in policy-making across the union. the sba also calls for greater recognition of entrepreneurship and entrepreneurs. we need people who are prepared to take initiatives, draft projects and take risks more than ever at times like this. we also believe, therefore, that honest entrepreneurs who suffer business failure deserve a second chance. in the current economic crisis, we need to establish priorities and focus on delivering quickly on those actions which have the strongest potential to contribute to recovery. the sba action plan and the european economic recovery plan propose ambitious measures in three key areas: first, loosening the supply of much-needed credit; secondly, reducing the administrative burden; and, thirdly, helping smes do business abroad. making a success of the small business act depends on the seamless cooperation of all stakeholders. we are grateful for your support. the resolution that parliament adopted in early december was a timely and strongly welcomed initiative. as you know, at the summit later in december, the heads of state and government unanimously endorsed the small business act, under the french presidency. it is, therefore, important that this house gives its full support today to ms herczog’s report, to establish a solid and legitimate basis for further action. i would like to invite you to promote the small business act in your respective countries and your constituencies to help make sure that this ambitious policy programme becomes reality and that it really helps smes in their daily business. let me turn now to mr lehne's two reports. parliament has always been a strong supporter of the european private company statute. your own-initiative report of 2006 and your swift handling of the commission’s proposal are important contributions to the success of the new european company statute. the private company statute is a key element of the small business act. it is designed for smes. therefore, the commission proposed that a european private company may be set up just like any domestic company, without the requirement for doing business in different member states or having shareholders from more than one country – just like at home. there are those who consider such a cross-border requirement essential for a european company forum. parliament’s resolution, which allows companies a choice between a variety of cross-border elements, is flexible. european private companies could comply with this requirement, for example by setting up a foreign subsidiary or by simply recording their intention to do cross-border business. you have also suggested a flexible solution for the minimum capital requirement. as you know, the commission’s proposal required a minimum capital of just one euro, and beyond that left it to the shareholders to decide on how much capital their european private company needs. this provision has been questioned by those who consider that capital requirements have a role in protecting the creditors of small companies. the european parliament report has put forward alternative solutions to improve creditor protection. it means companies would be able to choose between a solvency-based or capital-maintenance-based creditor protection regime. the commission agrees with those elements of the report that aim to increase the uniformity of the statute and thus promote the legal certainty of its application. these include prescribing the registration of the company’s business object, clarifying that the ban on offering shares to the public does not preclude offers to the employees of the company, or improving the rules on directors’ duties and liabilities. all of these make a helpful contribution which will facilitate the application of this statute. i also welcome your useful suggestion of an arbitration clause which encourages the shareholders to use alternative means of dispute resolution. finally, i should like to mention the subject that has been the most debated in parliament and elsewhere, which is workers’ participation on the board of european private companies. as i have already mentioned, the commission designed a statute for smaller businesses. employee participation in smes exists only in a few member states, and this is why a solution based on national laws, which would allow for the coexistence of different models, seemed the most appropriate and reasonable approach. parliament’s amendments introduce additional and complex provisions that are not applicable to smes formed under national law in most member states. this is likely to make the european private company statute unattractive in those member states and a much less viable option for smes. to conclude, there are a number of useful solutions in your report. some of them could very well pave the way for a swifter compromise among the member states. the commission, therefore, welcomes the adoption of this report. finally, regarding the cross-border transfer of the seat of companies: the commission has informed parliament on previous occasions of its reasons for not proposing the 14th company law directive. these reasons are still valid. we agree with the rapporteur that companies should enjoy freedom of establishment within the single market and be able to move their seats from one country to another. however, we are not convinced that proposing new legislation now is the best solution to achieve this objective. court of justice case-law clearly allows for company mobility. in addition, existing legislation – the cross-border mergers directive – provides a framework for the transfer, including the necessary safeguards for third parties. in particular, the cross-border mergers directive allows a company to set up a new subsidiary in another country and then merge its existing company with that subsidiary. the transfer procedure and safeguards suggested by the committee on legal affairs are very similar to those put forward by the directive. our economic analyses show that the level of complexity of the cross-border transfer procedure would be comparable to that of the cross-border merger procedure. the cross-border mergers directive was due to be transposed by member states by december 2007. it is too early to assess its full application. before embarking on any new legislation, the commission should first evaluate how this new existing regulatory framework operates in practice. we are also not convinced that the directive would improve the existing situation under national law and the jurisprudence of the european court of justice in this area. finally, in the light of the above and with the current legislature about to end, it would be more appropriate for the next commission to decide on how to proceed on this issue of the transfer of a company's registered office. by then it will also become clearer how the issue of seat transfer was solved in the spe statute.
the committee on economic and monetary affairs has tabled an opinion. - mr president! - what is it? the conditions and opportunities of small businesses today are crucial to the growth of the jobs of tomorrow. it is absolutely crucial to our ability to emerge from the crisis into a better and more knowledge-based and competitive economy. it is small businesses that are driving innovation forward, but it is also small businesses that today have the greatest credit problems in the different member states and in the union as a whole. what we are going to do here with a european charter for small enterprises - and i would like to thank the commission for the proposal and the rapporteur for her efforts - is to open up the internal market to small enterprises as much as to large ones. that's where a new road needs to be dug. as we have discussed in the committee on economic and monetary affairs, this involves a wide-ranging regulatory work which opens doors for small businesses. it is about ensuring that small firms can participate in public procurement on an equal footing with large firms, through the way in which public procurement is carried out. it is about making it easier for small businesses to act across borders, including on issues such as paying vat, and that we can combat the bureaucracy that is a hindrance today. it is also about making the european financial markets more suitable for expansion and growth through the efforts of the european investment bank and others, by creating a better credit market for small businesses. may i finally say: small is beautiful. small will be bigger tomorrow and give new jobs and more prosperity.
madam president, i would like to thank you. we know that small businesses are the backbone of our economy. in particular, the eu and the member states must make life easier for small businesses, both existing and new ones, by reducing rules, red tape and costs. smes have a very different capacity than traditional large enterprises to adapt to new circumstances and to adopt new findings and technologies. in addition to making it easier for small businesses to participate in public procurement through better access to the internal market, facilitating access to capital and making state aid rules non-discriminatory, this is a change in attitudes that permeates all decision-making, in school, university and work. research shows that entrepreneurship is strongly linked to growth. the forthcoming swedish presidency will in particular address targeted efforts to facilitate female entrepreneurship, an important european strategy for the future.
for the ind/dem group. president. - the debate is closed. the creation of fortress europe is moving faster and faster with each passing year. this is very unfortunate, not least because a common asylum policy will most likely mean a tougher and more restrictive policy in which those in need of protection will be the big losers. that development is worrying to say the least. the report states that the aim is to establish uniform standards for the recognition of a person as a refugee. why, one might ask. there are already international conventions that provide for this. the creation of new eu standards that would most likely be more restrictive than, for example, the geneva convention is not to be pursued. almost every week, we receive harrowing news from refugee camps in southern europe. people who have been there live in terrible conditions that the authorities choose to ignore. the problem is, of course, not the individuals in the camps but the fact that human rights are not respected. this is despite the fact that all member states meet the copenhagen criteria, at least in theory, and this is the question that should be discussed here in parliament. access to the territory of a country must be a matter for each country to decide, but of course international conventions and agreements must be respected.
madam president, commissioner barrot, this is the commission. i would like to begin by thanking mr giusto catania for his report. i fully share the view that it is unfortunate that the entry into force has been postponed until 2012. i would like to mention three points: children's rights, support and opportunities for women in human trafficking and, finally, how to be better prepared when something happens suddenly in the world. i think it is good that parliament is insisting that children and minors should receive special support. what still worries me, though, is that children can be taken into custody. this is unacceptable to me. today is the day after international women's day. the issue that has engaged me in this election is how different countries treat women and children in trafficking, that is, whether they have the right to stay in the eu or to be helped to return home. when the social democrats ruled sweden, it was natural that women who had been violated in sweden - whether it was through human trafficking, marriage or if they were subjected to violence - had the right to stay. now, the question is whether or not the woman can stay, but only if she cooperates with the police and the prosecutor. for which crimes other than trafficking in human beings is cooperation a requirement for asylum? i think this is discrimination against women and children and i want to raise this issue today, after women's day. the third question is how different countries receive asylum seekers and which countries they choose. my country, sweden, is one of the countries that has taken in the largest number of iraqi refugees. compared to both the united states and canada, that is an enormous number, and the same is true of most eu countries. i hope that the new asylum system in the eu will be better prepared for shared responsibility when things happen in the world or, as in the case of iraq, when countries are invaded. a european solidarity system cannot only work in times of calm, it must also work in times of crisis and conflict.
for the alde group. i am not sure that this is the case. this is a good report with clear environmental and safety objectives. the committee was clear on noise and health. as many as 210 million europeans are exposed to traffic noise today, which according to the who is a direct health hazard. noise from tyres is not the only way to reduce traffic noise, but the best results are achieved with a combination of measures, of which noise emissions from tyres are an important part. some, perhaps here too, say that there is a contradiction between noise emission and the safety characteristics of tyres. this is not supported by any of the in-depth and independent studies that have been carried out. that is why we are disappointed that the compromise does not reflect what we voted for in committee. as regards lorries, 90% of tyres already meet the noise levels set out in the compromise. this is not what we call a high level of ambition, unfortunately.
mr president, i would like to thank you. this is very important legislation. it has led, among other things, to the improvement of a very big problem in sweden, with dried-up lakes. but it is also important not to allow any further derogations or longer time limits in this directive, in order to continue this positive development with less fall-off of dangerous emissions across europe. that means that we want to remove these possibilities for further extension of the derogations. we would also like to see a clear limit on co2 emissions. emissions trading is not a sufficient tool to deal with the peak crisis we face. we're going to see that now as we go into a recession, where we're going to automatically have a lot of available allowances. they will be sold at a very cheap price, which means that we will miss several years in the transition needed to make it in the long term and that after this recession we will be less equipped and less able to cope with this development if we do not introduce complementary measures such as emission requirements for large combustion plants. i therefore find it very regrettable that these amendments have been considered inadmissible for the vote.
mr president, since i have limited time, having thanked mr krahmer for his hard work, allow me to focus on amendments 136-139, where some 40 colleagues, including myself, suggest that this directive should include an environment performance standard for large combustion plants, limiting co2 emissions in the future. at this very moment it is unclear whether these four amendments will be voted on later today. a recast includes the provision stating that if new developments merit amendments and suggestions, going beyond the original proposal, then that should be possible. i believe that recent science is enough substantive evidence that we need to be more ambitious than we thought just one or two years ago, when it comes to climate change mitigation. the emission trading system (ets) is, in my opinion, not sufficient to reach the 2°c target that the eu has agreed on. as currently designed, the emission trading system will lead to a carbon-free power sector only after some 60-65 years. that is why we have tabled these amendments, where we call for all power plants that are built in the future to limit their emissions to 350 g co2 from 2020 and onwards and those which are already in existence should be retrofitted by 2025 or close down. the proposal is technology-neutral and can be achieved by different means. i would strongly urge the president to agree that we can vote on those amendments, and i encourage colleagues to give their full support.
