for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. transparency and publicity in all matters relating to legislation and policy decisions is one of the most important factors in a democratic society. transparency and openness create participation and confidence in the political system. conversely, secrecy and withholding of documents create distrust, a feeling of inaction, and can sometimes contribute to abuse of power and corruption. more and more of the national legislation, with the principle of publicity we have in sweden, for example, is now done at eu level. the decisions have been transferred to the eu level, but transparency and publicity have not followed. our citizens see this, of course, and this is one reason why we have low voter turnout in the european elections. citizens have difficulty penetrating and understanding the decision-making process in the eu system and rightly feel that decisions and legislation are taken at eu level without having had a real opportunity to see all the documents. as a result, they have no opportunity to discuss, debate and influence decision-makers. we all want to increase turnout in the european elections, but if we are to succeed, campaigning and calling on people to vote is not enough. for this to be meaningful, we must do everything possible to ensure that citizens have the knowledge and information they need. we must create a dialogue with the citizens instead of one-sided information from above. publicity shall be the main principle, secrecy shall be the exception and shall be decided in a specific order, and there shall be strong reasons to allow secrecy. i and the united left have tabled amendments which, among other things, broaden the definition of documents, make more documents public and prevent individual member states from vetoing them. commissioner wallström said that a good instrument can be made better. unfortunately, this report does not actually lead to improvements but to deteriorations, but we can do better by supporting my amendments and those of the united left. so for the sake of democracy, vote for the united left amendment and increase the citizen's participation.
mr president, commissioner wallström, i would like to thank you for your report. i would like to begin by thanking michael cashman and others who have helped us to take this new and long-awaited step in making our work more accessible to our citizens. vice-president wallström has also fought hard and for a long time. when sweden joined the eu, many were worried that documents would leak through sweden, which has a very strong public disclosure principle, but that has not happened at all. michael cashman can tell you that, because if you're for openness and accessibility, you also know where the boundaries are for work materials, secrecy and disclosure. in libe, the epp voted down. i hope you are now in favour of increasing publicity in the eu, so that the swedish presidency, together with the rest of us, can develop this very important and central issue of democracy for all eu citizens. but i can understand the conservative group's hesitation, since you were the group that forced us to hold a secret vote when turkey was about to start membership negotiations. is that what you want? i hope that parliament is now united and that we can proudly tell the voters in the eu elections in june that the eu is becoming more and more open that we have no hidden agendas and want to be scrutinized and judged for what we do and with a publicity we can be proud of. we do a lot of good things and it would be good if citizens could follow our work better.
mr president, i am sorry to prolong this discussion, but may i build on what was just said by my colleagues, mrs doyle and mrs frassoni? as one of the authors of several of the amendments, i was in contact with the president’s office until 11.50 and could not obtain a clear ‘yes’ or ‘no’ as to whether the amendments we tabled – 136 to 139 – would be voted upon or not. it was unclear, and the last word was that the vote would probably be postponed. as avril doyle said, we simply cannot understand why these amendments are inadmissible. in the interests of clarity, we should have some more time and have clarification from the president’s office.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy. we think it is an important complement to the lehne report on the statute for european private joint stock companies. we believe that the lack of a common framework for the relocation of companies' head offices poses problems for companies wishing to move across borders in the internal market, as companies are today forced to go into liquidation and thus be wound up in order to move their head offices. we also think it is good that the european parliament is proposing that the transfer of the seat of a company should not involve circumvention of legal, social or fiscal conditions. we also welcome the european parliament's emphasis on the tax neutrality of a transfer of the registered office. however, we do not share all the conclusions reached by the committee in the course of its discussions of the report. for example, we consider that the wording in recital g that the european parliament cannot legislate is contrary to the case law of the court of justice. we would point out that it is the european parliament which, together with the council, makes laws and it is then for the court of justice to interpret the law made, not the other way around. we would also have liked to see the wording of the report, 'the european parliament stresses the positive effects of tax competition on economic growth in the light of the lisbon strategy', deleted.
in writing. - in junilist we are of course for equal treatment, equal pay for equal work and the fundamental principle of equality between women and men. that is why we voted in favour of this report. however, it is a yes vote with a clear reservation. we oppose the european parliament trying to decide how individual member states should structure their national childcare system or apply quotas, for example. the present report is a typical example of the european parliament's regulatory zeal and its eagerness to do its job. instead of acting as a forum for the urgent challenges of crossborder cooperation, it is trying to meddle in matters that are and will remain national affairs.
in writing. − the original ippc directive and the other six directives have not been fully implemented in eu member states and thus do not fulfil their purpose. that is why we decided to review these directives, and we voted on this in the european parliament today. we swedish social democrats are in favour of a revision and see that it contains some improvements compared to the existing rules. we voted against the directive in the final vote because we believe that some of the amendments voted for represent a major deterioration in relation to the commission's original proposal. for example, we could not accept further exemptions for large combustion plants. another reason why we felt compelled to vote against is that the proposal misses the chance to seriously reduce greenhouse gas emissions. the rejection of the amendments tabled by our delegation, which advocated limit values for co2 emissions from new large-scale electricity generating installations, shows that the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions is not taken seriously. we cannot support such a proposal.
in writing. − the commission’s proposal on a statute for a european private company allows an opportunity for unserious companies to circumvent the rules on workers participation. if the private european company has its registered office in a member state with low or no workers’ participation and performs its activities in another member state with high participation, the company can circumvent the rules. the socialist group in the parliament has however, together with the etuc, reached a compromise, which improve the commissions proposal substantially. the compromise now state that when the companies have a certain quota of their employees in another member state with higher workers participation, compared to the member state where the company has its headquarters, the more favourable rules for workers participation will apply. although the compromise is far better than the original proposal, we have not succeeded fully. the levels to trigger workers participation are still high compared to the rules in some member states and there are also problems with the definition of what is regarded to be a higher level of workers participation. we - the danish and the swedish delegations in the socialist group - have therefore decided to abstain our votes in the final vote.
in writing. - i voted against the final vote on the spe articles of association. the basic idea of introducing a common european corporate form for private companies is a great one. it is a reform that is sorely needed. the commission's proposal is really bad. the distinction between the application of national law versus the spe charter is very unclear. a large part of the company's statutes is to be dealt with in the articles of association. while this may be beneficial for some companies, some issues are of such a nature that they must be clearly specified in the statutes, such as the delimitation of the powers of the company's organs and the protection of minority shareholders. the degree of employee representation in management is also poor. the spe charter has been substantially improved during the current council negotiations and i remain hopeful that the final result will be good. however, this is not the proposal on which we have had to take a position today. it is the commission's proposal, in the modified form of lehne. and then my decision is pretty simple: the ambiguities and problems of this proposal overshadow the positive aspects of the reform, and the risk is therefore imminent that we will have a statute which will defeat its purpose. moreover, an endorsement of this report in view of the positive progress already made by the council would put the wheels of their work in motion. we are not voting for the council's proposal, but for the commission's proposal. that is why i chose to vote no.
in writing. the report calls for even more financial resources to be allocated to each area of the eu, while member states must make savings in areas such as health, education and care. in addition, several areas identified in the report, such as the financial crisis, climate change and energy policy, will be associated with enormous costs that are out of proportion to the eu budget. these are questions which must be addressed in the individual countries following an internal political process leading to a democratic consolidation of the sacrifices in question. we have therefore voted against this report on the commission's budget for 2010.
in writing. − at a time when cuts are being made in health, education and care in the common sector, savings should also be made in the eu institutions. we believe that the budgets of the committee of the regions and the european economic and social committee should definitely be cut. the eu taxpayers would not notice any difference if these two institutions were to be charged with a major austerity measure. we also oppose an increase in the number of staff in the political groups of the european parliament. this is not a necessary expense in the current situation. the opening of a museum of european history, as decided by the bureau of the european parliament, is also not a good idea. experience shows that such a museum will be designed as propaganda for an even more federal europe. we have therefore voted against this report on, among other things, the european parliament's budget for 2010.
in writing. - this report, outside the legislative procedure, advocates a number of costly proposals such as an eu product quality authority and new promotion and support measures in agriculture. we also want to warn that the report contains formulations that could lead to an even more protectionist policy on agricultural products on the part of the eu. as usual, we in junilistan can say that it is fortunate that the european parliament does not have a vote on the eu's agricultural policy at this stage, because then the union would fall into a trap of protectionism and heavy subsidies to various groups in the agricultural sector.
in writing. - i chose not to support the report by mrs petre on the promotion and strengthening of food labelling. the report contained good proposals for simplification of rules and shorter processing times. however, in my view, these were overridden by protectionist writings on conditional market access and the desire to create a supranational authority for product quality.
for the gue/ngl group. president. - the debate is closed. i very much appreciated the fact that member states in the barcelona objectives adopted targets for children's access to childcare. i thought - and still think - that the targets were low, but it was a start. access to good childcare is a prerequisite for women's access to employment and it is also a prerequisite for gender equality. but now i am concerned that the objectives are not being achieved and, of course, that the czech presidency is sending signals to replace the childcare objective with home care as a viable alternative and that it is therefore desirable that the objective should be removed. but i would also like to thank commissioner špidla, who has indeed made it clear that the commission still considers it important to achieve the barcelona objectives in the area of childcare. i would also like to thank my colleague mr retsetaris for the proposal on mainstreaming children. i think we should work together to fix it.
for the pse group. madam president, commissioner špidla, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to begin by thanking the commissioner for his reply and the committee for its initiative. it is time that this matter was discussed in parliament. the lisbon treaty makes child issues an objective in the eu and gives them a legal basis. parliament also adopted a strategy for children a year ago, pending the treaty. it's a scandal that children are left to live on their own. of course, a mother and father may have to move for work or escape, but it is we as members of the european parliament who must take responsibility when we adopt rules that say, for example, that asylum is granted only to the applicant and not to his family, because it is usually men who flee and women and children who are left at home. or when employers import labor and don't pretend, ask or care about the presence of children at home. i therefore fully support the demands made by the socialist group in its question. it is urgent and it is necessary to have an impact assessment. the commission must act on the basis of the study commissioned by the commissioner, and this is urgent. better information on children's rights and schooling must be developed. we must also inform and help the children who are now in this situation. we have to involve the parties and the ngos and we have to come up with proposals. i also believe that a relatively new group, i.e. unaccompanied refugee children, could also be included in the kind of work described by the commissioner. children should be raised with warmth and care and are not something that the market should control. we politicians have an obligation and we have to take it on, so give us child mainstreaming and give us child impact assessments of this huge problem. otherwise, we'll be embarrassed in front of the next generation.
the rapporteur. mr president, the president of the council, the commission! there has been a discussion on whether or not the employment guidelines need to be amended. it is not a particularly important discussion, because within the employment guidelines there is every opportunity to act. the problem now is lack of action. we have had and have a financial crisis which has become an economic crisis, and now the employment crisis is coming and we will have social problems in the future. it is good that an employment summit is being held in may, but we must not isolate employment issues from economic issues. that is why they must be integrated into the discussion. i think that too little has been done and too late. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the budgetary impact of the single market is not undermined. we need more action, more coordinated action - certainly above 2 percent - to deal with it. the risk of not doing enough and of doing it too late is much, much greater than doing too much, because it will result in increased unemployment and it will result in reduced tax revenues, which will affect the social problems out there in the member states. what are we gonna do? we know that pretty well. we will link what is good in the short term in terms of fighting unemployment with what is good in the long term. it is about environmental investment, it is about new infrastructure projects, it is about energy efficiency in housing and it is about education, education and education. we've been talking about lifelong learning. we've never done enough, but now we have a chance to make a powerful educational effort. we're also going to stimulate demand, and then we're going to target those groups that are going to use the means of consumption: the unemployed, the families with children, the retirees and others who are going to use an increased amount of money to consume. we will do what we can at eu level and we are trying to speed up the processing of the social fund and the globalisation fund so that the funds can be channelled into the member states. but let us be honest, we know that the great financial resources are in the member states and unless the member states act sufficiently and sufficiently coordinated, we will not succeed. if we look around and clean up what the member states have done, only one country comes up to 1.5 per cent, and that is germany, which was not the country in the front line when it came to acting. other countries, such as the nordic countries, where i come from, are making very little effort to act despite their good economic situation. the social consequences. you have mentioned them, and they are extremely important. they affect not only social security systems but also the public sector. the public sector is doubly important. it is about giving people childcare, elderly care, social security but it is also important for employment. there are a lot of people in the public sector and we have to make sure that it has sufficient financial resources. i'll also take the young people. young people are now entering unemployment directly from university. we must create opportunities for young people to get jobs or further education or whatever it may be. otherwise, we're gonna be in trouble for the future. finally, we must act. we must act in a coordinated way, with solidarity, we must act now and not wait, and we must act with sufficient effort. (applause) i would like to thank you.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the lisbon strategy is one of the eu's best projects. member states will voluntarily reform their economies to create prosperity and good adaptability, both to foreseen changes such as ageing populations and to unforeseen changes such as collapsing financial markets. the strategy is to promote efficient markets, entrepreneurship, education, research and stable public finances, and now we are being tested. if we had all had a flexible economy, the right monetary policy and sound public finances when the financial crisis hit, europe would have fared much better. but we haven't. the lisbon strategy has not been implemented, and at the same time the euro has brought about an all too easy monetary policy for ireland, spain, italy and greece. in addition, several countries have been able to mismanage their public finances in protection of the euro. that's why the imbalances are so gigantic. the lisbon strategy is a good idea that has been scrapped. the euro is a bad idea that has made the problems worse.
mr president, this debate is about jobs, and jobs and new prosperity. that is why i am a little surprised to hear, from the socialist group, criticisms of those who have been responsible for realistic policies in europe, because the socialists, more than anyone else, called for lower interest rates at the peak of the economy, just as they implemented monetary policy in the us. it is lax monetary policy, more than anything else, that has eroded the us economy. mr schulz should feel thankful that europe and the european central bank did not listen to him, because, if it had, the european economy would have been much worse off. i appreciate we can agree on that point. that also goes for the policy that you are recommending today, because now you are talking about eurobonds which, among other things, would lead to higher interest rates for the countries of central europe. that is not solidarity in a time of financial crisis, and we would do well not listen to mr schultz this time either. we need to take action, but we need to take the right actions in order not to deepen the crisis and to secure stability. (interjection from the floor)no, you have not been in power, but you are guilty of a lot of things too, and if we had listened to you we would have been worse off. that was an agreement between you and me, was it not? i appreciate the consensus in parliament that your policy was wrong. mr president, what we need now is stability. we need to abide by competition rules and state aid rules in order to secure open barriers and trade, because exports need more imports and imports need exports. that is the way we can achieve more jobs.
the rapporteur. - madam president! - what is it? the crisis is now becoming real for people, unemployment is now starting to rise, and it's rising fast, and we're starting to see the social consequences of the crisis. the downturn is going to be bigger than we thought at first. there will be higher unemployment, there will be greater social consequences. i would like to say something to the conservative group in this parliament. hökmark is not here, but hökmark blamed the crisis on the social democrats' proposals. it's like shooting the pianist if you don't like the song. it is true that we have centre-right and right-wing governments in europe. they are the ones who show a lack of action, they are the ones who show a lack of coordination, they are the ones who show a lack of solidarity. now it is about jobs, now it is about social security systems and now it is about the public sector. i say to the commission and the council before the summit: we must act now, we must act in a coordinated way, we must act with sufficient effort and we must act with solidarity. this is the time to do it. we cannot wait for the summit in may; employment issues must be at the top of the agenda now. (applause) i would like to thank you.
vice-president of the commission. − madam president, i have the honour of giving the following declaration on behalf of the commission. the commission takes note of the amendments voted by parliament that it will study in detail. the commission confirms its willingness to seek a compromise with parliament and council. the commission will only consider its proposal after the two branches of the budgetary authority have adopted their positions. the commission intends to continue to pursue a constructive dialogue, in the mean time, with both institutions.
madam president, i would like to inform our group that there is a mistake in our voting lists regarding amendment 113: there should be a plus in the voting list, and not a minus.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the european parliament and the council. as regards amendments nos 61 and 103 to article 5, we believe that third-reading conciliation documents should be opened immediately after the closure of the final conciliation meeting, unlike the documents examined in the actual negotiation. documents from the first and second reading trilogues should be fully open throughout the procedure.
in writing. − the eu is a union of values and therefore has a responsibility to uphold respect for human rights within its borders. the eu therefore has a role, through its member states, in drawing attention to the vulnerable situation of the roma and in facilitating their integration into society. we have therefore voted in favour of this report.
in writing. - we voted today in favour of the own-initiative report on the social situation of roma and better access for roma to the eu labour market by kósáné kovács (a6-0038/2009). the report addresses a very serious problem and clearly points to the need for action to address the widespread exclusion of many roma today. we welcome the cooperation between member states to tackle these major problems. we want to make it clear, however, that we do not see different forms of special solutions as a way forward to reduce exclusion. special tax rates for employers employing roma women and other such measures risk rather cementing exclusion and preventing integration into the rest of society.
in writing. - the resolution contains many heartfelt appeals. but most of what is dealt with in the resolution is the political responsibility of the national parliaments to address. the resolution also calls for more resources to be allocated to the eu's globalisation adjustment fund. this means that the member states' contributions to the eu must be increased. this is at the same time as the member states need to retain their reduced financial resources for their own social and employment policies. we do not believe that the eu globalisation fund is the most effective way of supporting workers who have lost their jobs. member states are better placed to pursue an effective policy in this area. in addition, each member state spends an amount on stimulus packages in the same order as its contribution to the eu budget as a whole. we have voted against this resolution mainly because of the writing on the eu globalisation adjustment fund.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the resolution on the former yugoslav republic of macedonia is, in my view, a well-balanced text and i would like to thank mr meijer for concentrating his work on the reforms and objectives that have been achieved and on the issues on which more work still needs to be done. i am particularly pleased that the resolution sends a strong signal that the present situation, after three years of waiting for the start of negotiations, is very worrying and unacceptable. it is quite clear that the former yugoslav republic of macedonia is a european state whose place is in the european union. when we discuss this issue in parliament, i usually avoid mentioning the name conflict between greece and the former yugoslav republic of macedonia. i think there are many other issues that should be discussed in greater depth and that we never discuss because the name conflict takes up a disproportionate amount of time. but today, after reading some of the amendments, i feel the need to emphasize strongly that it is not acceptable to exploit any bilateral conflict to make it more difficult for a country to move faster towards european integration or to stop a country's participation in the international institutions. there are many countries which have had and are having bilateral conflicts, and we all want these conflicts to be resolved as quickly as possible in a way that is acceptable to both sides, but in the meantime, in my view, we should not block each other in the process of european integration, especially when it comes to countries which are in a sensitive position both geographically and politically.
on behalf of the ppe-de group. – mr president, i should like to propose a deletion of one part of the text, so the new text of the second part of paragraph 13 should read: ‘in view of the new round of negotiations announced under the “nimetz process”, expresses the hope that all governments in the neighbourhood will support the integration of this country in the eu, thus contributing to the stability and prosperity of the region;’.
in writing. − this report sets out a series of requirements for member states to act to limit the harmful use of video games. schools should inform children and parents about the advantages and disadvantages of video games, parents should take measures to avoid negative effects when children play video games, member states should explore the value of introducing a 'red button' to be installed on game devices and computers to block access to certain games, national information and awareness campaigns for consumers should be carried out, internet café owners should prevent children from playing games intended for adults, a specific eu-wide code of conduct for retailers and producers of video games should be introduced and member states should introduce civil and criminal legislation on the resale of violent tv, video and computer games. video games for minors are associated with many troubling cultural and social issues. but that is precisely why countries must seek solutions that are suited to their own culture and values, so that they can be democratically anchored in their own people. the sticks from the eu institutions have almost the opposite effect. the fact that countries can pursue different paths in this area is also important in order to broaden our experience and knowledge in this area. for these reasons i voted against the final vote on this report.
in writing. - this report, outside the legislative procedure, advocates, among other things, an eu forestry policy, a dedicated eu fund to finance preventive measures in relation to climate change and an eu-funded drought observatory, among other things. we believe that the environmental responsibility for agricultural land must be borne primarily by the member states. there is no need to delegate powers to the member states in this way. as usual, we in junilistan can say that it is fortunate that the european parliament does not have a vote on the eu's agricultural policy at this stage, because otherwise the union would fall into a trap of protectionism and growing subsidies to various special interests in the agricultural sector. i have voted against this report.
in writing. − as the barriers to entry in the eu have been removed, the possibility of seeking work in other eu countries has become increasingly more accessible. this is a very positive development, which gives people the opportunity to improve their own and their families' living conditions. the rapporteur admits this, but nevertheless insists on concentrating on the negative aspects of the absence of a parent seeking income abroad. i think it is unreasonable for the european parliament to direct the social and educational policies of individual member states in the clumsy way proposed. we must show respect and trust in the member states and their democratically elected assemblies to provide for their own people and their well-being. i have therefore voted against the resolution.
in writing. - the junilists are very positive about future enlargements of the european union. it is, however, of the utmost importance that the candidate countries de facto fulfil the requirements and thus are full democratic states of law upon accession. the copenhagen criteria must be met, the legislation we agree on must not only be implemented but also de facto upheld and the rule of law must be guaranteed. the three countries we have discussed today certainly have the potential to become member states in the future, but it is important that we do not take these requirements lightly. experience shows that progress is most rapid before accession negotiations have begun and slower during ongoing negotiations, especially if these are seen as moving towards successful conclusion.
in writing. − we favour the accession of turkey to the european union given that the country fulfils the copenhagen criteria and that accession is supported by the turkish population. we regret, however, that we were not able to vote in favour of the progress report on turkey voted today. the report unfortunately comprises both serious lacks and misdirected demands. for example, in paragraph 20 unreasonable demands are made of a democratic party. in paragraph 29, turkey is encouraged to cooperate closely with the imf, and in paragraph 31 the country is said to be obliged to conclude ftas with third countries. the report does not make sufficient reference to breaches of human rights or to the critical situation of national minorities, in particular the kurds. the armenian genocide is not mentioned at all, which differentiates this report from earlier resolutions from parliament.
in writing. water is essential for all life on earth. however, the responsibility for ensuring access to these supplies does not lie with the eu. it is through international cooperation within the framework of the united nations that the nations of the world will seek solutions to improve water resources. as the rapporteur's proposal is taking a very different direction, i have decided to vote against the resolution.
in writing. - the rapporteur's account of human suffering across southern africa is a chilling reminder of the importance of continuing and intensifying the fight against poverty. the proposals presented by the rapporteur are, however, based entirely on the eu's leading role in the member states' aid policy. we in the junilist are opposed to this. the eu shall not provide assistance or seek to influence the activities of the member states in this area. aid is an area where the experience is unfortunately quite depressing. it is therefore important that experiments with new forms can be made. our own country, sweden, is currently looking for new, interesting avenues. at this historic juncture, it is completely wrong to gradually deprive the member states of the opportunity to rethink and reform aid policy. the responsibility for aid is and will remain a matter for the member states. international cooperation to find solutions to improve healthcare in sub-saharan africa should be sought primarily within the un, not the eu. i have therefore voted against the resolution.
writer. - mr president! - what is it? i'm very happy that we can have this debate today. in these times of increased protectionism, and when poverty is spreading rather than decreasing, it is particularly important that we keep trade open between europe and some of the poorest countries in the world. that is what the interim agreements are all about. the economic partnership agreements aim to ensure continued trade and development in some of the world's poorest countries. these countries are likely to be hit hardest as the global recession unfolds and the protectionist arms race looks set to worsen. that is why i do not understand some of the criticisms that have been made. some argue that these agreements are too far-reaching and comprehensive. some critics prefer to talk about lost customs revenue rather than the potential for new trade. on the contrary, i think we should be happy that we have come this far! i am saying that there is no inherent conflict between trade and development as some speakers claim. i think it's the opposite. trade leads to development tariffs lead to poverty. i've been in charge of the interim agreement with ghana. first of all, i must admit that it has some beauty spots, such as continuing eu tariffs on rice and sugar for a transitional period, but in the end it is a very good agreement. that is why it is important that we get it signed as soon as possible. in the past, the presidential election in ghana got in the way. now i would like to call on the country's new president, john atta mills, to sign the interim agreement. i also hope that the eu will sign the agreement we have negotiated. it's unacceptable that it's taking so long. and it is particularly unacceptable that it takes so long for the translations in the council not to work well enough! i would like to take this opportunity to urge everyone to support the agreement. in these troubled times, the world needs more trade - not less!
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i and my group welcome the compromise that the council and parliament have managed to reach in this recasting of the cosmetics directive. in the negotiations with the council, nanomaterials have been the main stumbling block. so it's about using extremely small structures to make new materials that, because of the very smallness of the particles, have completely new properties or functions. for example, the material can be made tougher, firmer, thinner, water-repellent, heat-retention, etc. so far, we don't really know much about it. some makeup and lotions contain nanoparticles, and perhaps these particles can penetrate sore skin and find their way into the body where they absolutely should not be. should we then leave it unchecked as an active ingredient in cosmetics? the answer to that question, of course, is no. more knowledge and information needs to be developed on nanomaterials. that is why i am pleased with this agreement which we are going to vote on tomorrow. it's a step in the right direction. the agreement will improve the protection of eu consumers when nanomaterials are used in hair dyes, uv filters, etc. it will undergo a safety assessment before products are allowed to be placed on the market and the cosmetics industry will also have to notify the use of nanomaterials in its other products to the commission, which in turn can consult the consumer protection committee if there is a suspicion that nanomaterials pose a health risk. many thanks to the responsible rapporteur and to the commission for a good reworking!
the principle of 'one state, one vote' is one of the cornerstones of un cooperation and should remain so. we therefore find it very unfortunate that the european parliament is seeking to ensure that the eu has a single seat on the un security council in the future. it goes without saying that swedish foreign policy is different from polish, which is different from greek. but of course, like-minded countries can join together whenever they wish. however, the report contains several positive elements, particularly in terms of calls for respect for human rights and international humanitarian law. we consider these texts to be of such importance that we have decided to support the report despite other shortcomings.
the rapporteur draws an essentially accurate picture of africa's many challenges. she is also unquestionably right in describing the importance of both international action and international cooperation in addressing poverty, the lack of health care and the potential consequences of the global economic downturn. the rapporteur, however, is strongly in favour of giving the european parliament a greater role in relations between africa and the eu. the european parliament has also called for the eu to be given a role in the negotiations on the future of the african continent. the rapporteur also calls for the european parliament to be given greater responsibility for the design and operation of the european development fund. we consider that such a development would be extremely unfortunate. we have therefore voted against the report as a whole.
in writing. - we social democrats believe that it is important to promote both art and culture in the eu. we therefore want to see an increased exchange of experience and cooperation between member states in this area. this is particularly important to facilitate the study of students in artistic education in another member state. however, we do not believe that the content of art education in the member states should be determined at eu level. such decisions should be taken by the member states themselves. we have therefore voted against the report.
in writing. - junilista believes that cultural issues should be the political responsibility of the member states. this report is outside the legislative procedure and is nothing more than a suggestion by the federalist majority of the european parliament that the eu should intervene even more in the cultural field. we have therefore voted against the report as a whole.
in writing. - it is important that artists have the possibility to move freely, that the opportunities for non-commercial art are promoted, that the development of european digital libraries to preserve the art world goes further, etc. i interpret paragraph 9 on the nature and duration of art studies as a desire to include art studies in the bologna process and, subject to that, i can vote in favour of the report.
in writing. - this report is outside the legislative procedure and is nothing but an opinion of the federalist majority in the european parliament. the proposal argues for the completion of the ratification process of the lisbon treaty. but we believe that the lisbon treaty has failed twice. the last time the people of ireland voted against the lisbon treaty was in 2008, but before that, the voters of france and the netherlands voted against essentially the same proposal in 2005. when will the federalist majority in the european parliament realise that the ambition to create a united states of europe lacks the support of the electorate? in fact, in recital b of the draft report, it is stated: "people with a lack of knowledge of eu policies and the treaties tend to be more opposed to them". this shows the federalists' indifference, arrogance and ignorance towards voters with different political values from those prevailing in this centralised european parliament. we have therefore voted against the report.
in writing. − the hegyi report concerning the promotion of a dialogue with eu citizens is based on the assumption that people’s sceptical attitude towards the eu is due to the fact that they lack adequate knowledge. various measures are proposed in the report such as education, euronews and the creation of a museum of eu history. since the actions far more resemble propaganda than dialogue i oppose them. in a real dialogue, the opinions of the citizens are seen as valuable.
in writing. - we voted in favour of the report by mr wojciech roszkowski because we agree with the overall objectives of rural development and diversification of activities to promote local development potential. however, there are points in the report with which we disagree, both from a factual point of view and from the way in which certain points have been formulated. we do not agree, for example, that the funds for the other pillars of the agricultural policy have been significantly reduced. and although there are important environmental and regional policy reasons, we do not believe that any agricultural production should be maintained by means of aid 'at any cost'. we also believe that there are writings which anticipate the rural population's own choices. we have, however, chosen to interpret these points as unfortunate formulations of good intentions such as the prevention of desertification.
in writing. the commission proposal aims to harmonise tobacco taxes in the eu in order to curb the significant crossborder trade in tobacco which risks undermining public health policy objectives in the member states. the proposal presented by the rapporteur from the committee on economic and monetary affairs was a significant weakening of the commission's original text. as leader of the liberal group, i did my utmost to improve the report. the issue of tobacco taxes is very difficult to resolve because of the wide disparities in tax rates and views on the harmful effects of tobacco within the eu. there's been a lot of division in the various factions, including my own. despite my efforts, i finally felt that parliament's proposal did not go far enough. i therefore chose to abstain in the final vote on the new tobacco taxes.
in writing. - we voted today in favour of the own-initiative report on combating female genital mutilation in the eu by christina muscardini (a6-0054/2009). the report raises a very serious problem and clearly points to the need for action to deal with it. the report also makes various proposals on how this should be done. we welcome the cooperation between member states to tackle this problem. however, we would like to stress that regular preventive health checks on girls and women granted asylum in the eu because of the risk of female genital mutilation are a matter for each member state to deal with with respect for the rights of the individual.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'general' in the definition of the term 'general'. we have chosen not to support the alternative resolution put forward by our group in this parliament, which also won a majority in the end. the reason for this is that we believe that the original report was better. the alternative resolution contained a letter on the requirement of regular medical examinations of women granted asylum due to threats of female genital mutilation. the purpose of this letter is to prevent genital mutilation from occurring within the eu. however, we believe that this measure is an invasion of privacy and goes too far and puts an unacceptable burden on young women who are already vulnerable. a visit to the doctor should be voluntary. that is why we have decided not to support the alternative resolution.
in writing. - female genital mutilation is a cruel and inhumane practice and has no place in modern society. as junilista sees the eu not only as a cooperation for the benefit of increased growth and trade, but also as a forum for upholding common basic human values, we have chosen to vote in favour of the report. we are, however, completely opposed to several of the far-reaching texts dealing with the criminal law of the member states. legislation aimed at maintaining the functioning of society should be initiated and decided by national parliaments elected by the people, not by the european parliament.
in the light of the above, the commission proposes to extend the scope of the directive to include the following areas: this report raises a very serious problem and clearly states the need for action to deal with it. i welcome cooperation between member states in this area. i have nevertheless voted against because i believe that all health care should be voluntary. i am opposed to regular preventive health checks on girls and women granted asylum because of the risk of genital mutilation, as i consider this to be an invasion of privacy and discriminatory.
in writing. − female genital mutilation is a practice that must be condemned by the member states. it constitutes a violation of women's fundamental rights, particularly the right to personal integrity, and of sexual and reproductive rights. however, there are aspects of the muscardini report that i do not fully support such as the formulations regarding questioning the credibility of parents who request asylum on the grounds that they have refused to consent to their child undergoing female genital mutilation. i see no reason to throw particular suspicion on people who request asylum on these specific grounds. another formulation in the report which i do not support is the one proposing regular medical examinations on women and girls who are granted asylum because of the threat of female genital mutilation in their home country. i consider the practice to be discriminatory against these women and girls. if a person is granted with asylum in a member state she should have the same rights and obligations as other citizens in the country.
in writing. - the original own-initiative report on female genital mutilation was very good, but the revised version which won the vote contained a letter in which the authors wanted to introduce regular medical examinations for women granted asylum because of the risk of female genital mutilation. the aim would be to prevent genital mutilation from occurring in the host eu country. i find forced medical checks to be extremely intrusive. a doctor visit has to be voluntary. forced labour is totally unacceptable in this context. that is why i voted against the recast version.
in writing. - junilista believes that cultural issues should be the political responsibility of the member states. the report is outside the legislative procedure and is nothing more than a suggestion by the federalist majority of the european parliament that the eu should intervene even more in the cultural field. as usual, the european parliament's committee on culture and education wants to make a dent in the new costly programmes and bodies at eu level. we in junilist do not believe that a multiannual programme or a community body for linguistic diversity and language learning at eu level will bring any change to the eu's population of some 500 million, other than adding to the burden on taxpayers. nor do we think that the commission's plans for information and awareness campaigns on the benefits of language learning will affect reality. it is up to the member states to motivate their citizens to learn languages in their education policies. this is not a matter for the eu. we have therefore voted against the report.
in writing. the rapporteur states in his report that cohesion policy is one of the most successful eu initiatives. this is a startling conclusion, not least because as recently as november 2008 the european court of auditors found that 11 per cent of the 42 billion euros approved in 2007 under eu cohesion policy should never have been paid. the rapporteur seems to be fully aware of this and in paragraph 17 he urges those taking part in the debate on territorial cohesion to avoid all reference to budgetary and financial implications of the proposed policy. the lid on, in other words. the eu's cohesion policy is ill-conceived. from a socioeconomic perspective, where consideration of taxpayers' money is a must, it is nothing but a complete failure. the junilists have of course voted against the report as a whole.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. as always with our budget, this is a conscientious and competent piece of work, but unfortunately it is politically backward. as a previous speaker here said, this is not a budget for the 21st century. the starting point is obviously that eu spending will grow and grow forever. we have to put an end to this sometime! i shall briefly point out three aspects. it says that if gnp falls, as it will now, the eu budget will not be affected. that's no way to run this business. it is better to spend money in countries when they see their economy go down and collapse the way it is now. it proposes more money for new areas of activity based on the lisbon treaty. this is an arrogance against the democratic rules of the game. that treaty is rejected. at the same time, no cuts are proposed. but there is a lot of money to be saved in agriculture, the structural funds, ecosoc, the committee of the regions and so on. do it now!
mr president, i would like to thank you. as the socialist group's shadow rapporteur on this matter, i would like to thank mr reimer böge for his positive spirit of cooperation. a long-term framework is important, but a seven-year lock-in with rigid budgetary ceilings, both overall and sectoral, is an unreasonable model in today's rapidly changing world. the current long-term budget, which runs until 2014, was in practice established in 2005. what would we know about the current economic crisis, the situation in kosovo or gaza, the depth of the climate crisis or the violent fluctuations in food prices? no, that won't hold! my conclusion is that we must have a comprehensive review of the current long-term budget, the multiannual financial framework. this is of course particularly true if the committee on budgets' proposal is implemented, i.e. the extension of the current financial framework by two years, as proposed, to bring it into better coordination with the commission and parliament's terms of office. what do we want? well, a lot of things. first, the ambitions of the budget review itself. we believe that it is not acceptable to focus only on the future financial framework. the committee on budgets has adopted a number of amendments which require the commission to submit as soon as possible a proposal for a substantive revision of the current long-term budget. especially if our demand for an extension were to be granted. we also call on the swedish presidency this autumn to manage such a proposal from the commission actively and expeditiously. we need to get to the gun range. secondly, as regards the political orientation, we have received clear signals from the broad open consultation carried out by the commission. these are some of the areas in particular where the eu must become more powerful. it is climate policy, it is jobs and growth, it is foreign and development policy. the eu's ability to play a leading global role in these areas requires resources. it's obvious. today, all these areas are clearly underfunded. thirdly, a number of necessary technical changes are needed. i do not need to go into this in more detail, because reimer böge has already mentioned it. we want five years instead of seven and a rearrangement of the period so that the new parliament and the new commission can have a real say in the framework that will apply during their term. fourthly, a revised and fairer system for the eu's own resources. what is needed now is a rapid proposal and a substantial mid-term review of the current long-term budget that brings rhetoric and resources closer together and that starts the process towards a more sustainable long-term orientation of the eu budget for the next long-term budgetary period.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the problems of the european automotive industry are largely the result of the economic and financial crisis. in that respect, it is hopefully a problem that will be temporary. but there is another very important part, which is also a problem of overcapacity. it is therefore of the utmost importance that we ensure that the future of the european automotive industry is based on realistic and sound business plans and responsible ownership. this leads me to say that if we are to succeed in maintaining a successful automobile industry in europe, with the opportunities it offers for technological development and good employment and the role it plays in the european economy, the state aids currently granted by the member states must be designed to ensure survival in the recession and financial crisis, but not to distort competition between member states or between car manufacturers. state aid that distorts competition and creates distrust between member states is in fact putting the survival of the european automobile industry at risk, and it is in this context that i would like to put the question of what has now happened in slovenia and in france, and the state aid that has been distributed in france. one of the commission's main tasks is to ensure that the rules laid down are not breached, to follow up on what has happened and to create full confidence that no state aid is being granted to the detriment of any other member state or any other car industry. aid that distorts competition undermines the future of the european automotive industry and has negative effects on both employment and technological development.
mr president, i would like to thank you. what we're experiencing now is both a financial crisis and a climate crisis. we can note that there are car companies that handle these double crises better. there are car brands and models that are better able to cope with these crises, namely those that have invested in sustainable technological development. then there are companies like general motors that are demanding $350 billion from the states in which they operate. we shouldn't waste good money on bad. we're going to support the people who are in the auto industry. we will support the regions that are in difficulty, we will support the small businesses that are in the supply chain, but we will make sure that we do so with products for the market of the future. the commission can do more by opening up the structural funds, both the social fund, the regional funds and the agricultural fund, to biofuels, social measures and the regions. i also believe that the european parliament's activities in strasbourg should be discontinued.
for the pse group. president. - the debate is closed. i would like to thank the commission very much for this proposal. i would also like to thank kartika liotard for a very good report which has greatly strengthened the areas which i consider particularly important, the public health aspects and consumer protection, compared to the commission's proposal. i also recognize many of the issues we have worked on together in the past in the area of food additives. it is a question of nanoparticles and nanomaterials. they are dealt with very well in the rapporteur's proposal, but also in an amendment by hiltrud breyer, whom i know from her work on the cosmetics directive. we put forward there the same proposals that have now been adopted in that directive, and which should of course also be included here. it is therefore a matter of labelling nanomaterials, that the contents must always be visible to the consumer so that he can also opt out of foods containing nanoparticles or nanomaterials. another aspect that i recognize and welcome is that we should be able to take into account the impact of food on the environment. it's a very important issue that's growing in importance. it also has ethical aspects, for example in relation to cloning, which have now been addressed in a good and clear way in the report. it is not a question of the impact on the consumer of eating cloned meat, but above all of the ethical aspects that we really have to take into account when we decide on such legislation. i would also like to say that i agree with the rapporteur that approval must also be required for nanomaterials in packaging that come into contact with food. the socialist group thinks that the rapporteur's proposals are very good. we will also support some of the amendments. we hope that we can reach an agreement with the commission quite soon anyway.

in writing. - we believe that our main contribution to promoting continued development in the world's poorest countries is to open up eu markets to imports from these parts of the world. assistance, when deemed compatible with the ambition to increase free trade, should be initiated and decided by individual member states, not by the eu. if the eu still insists on distributing and directing aid, the trade of the beneficiary countries must not be subject to any conditions. however, provisions designed to protect sensitive industries in developing countries during a transitional period may be acceptable. in principle, however, protectionist provisions which risk undermining free access to the market should be avoided. these views have guided us in our position on the motions for resolutions on the trade agreements which the commission is now preparing. we oppose the unfortunate writings which are intended solely to increase the influence of the european parliament in trade policy. we have nevertheless chosen to vote in favour of all the resolutions, because each one highlights the importance of continuing free trade at a time when the severe winds of protectionism are gathering strength.
for the pse group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to begin by thanking stavros lambrinidis and all the other members of the libe committee for making this report so well thought out. i would also like to thank you for the support you have given to my amendments. this means support for the strengthening of the rights of users and consumers. the section on what the technology can be used for is of great importance, such as monitoring internet traffic. it is good that the european parliament is now forcefully saying that privacy and civil human rights come first. the report clarifies that monitoring of internet traffic may only take place in the event of criminal suspicion and as part of legal proceedings following court decisions. it's going to be a very important foundation for the civil rights movement. the report comes at the last minute for necessary measures. i am surprised at the amendments tabled by conservative and liberal members. their proposals weaken the rights and integrity of citizens. nor have they critically examined what technological progress may bring if we are not observant. of course, we must fight against crimes on the internet, crimes such as the exploitation of children and young people. the central issues in this context, however, are that the swedish mother government has decided on the so-called fra law, a law that deals with the examination of people who have not committed a crime or are criminals, instead of the people to examine us. the report is a scathing criticism of the swedish moderate government which, in spite of all criticism, has introduced this fra law in sweden. in sweden, authorities are now allowed to monitor internet traffic without suspicion of crime or danger to the security of the individual or society. after the decision tomorrow, i assume that the swedish government will reconsider and make sure to change the law. otherwise, you are going against the european parliament and the elected representatives of the 27 eu countries.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. a big thank you to stavros lambrinidis for having succeeded in both respecting the security of the internet and at the same time protecting and respecting the invaluable fundamental rights. i assume that the protection of our fundamental rights in accordance with this report will also be supported when we decide on the telecoms package. there are clear links between this report and the telecommunications package. i hope that we will also agree that it is important to protect civil and political rights. the internet has, as several colleagues have said, greatly increased the possibilities for freedom of expression. citizens who do not normally have access to the mainstream media's debate pages can use the internet to generate opinions and raise questions. it is a new arena for the exchange of views that we so badly need for political mobilisation. it means more scrutiny of decision-makers by citizens. the important thing is that citizens themselves have the opportunity to scrutinise legislators and other authorities. it has meant increased knowledge. above all, we have also had opportunities for exchanges and contacts between people from different cultures and different parts of the world. in discussing this issue it is also important that we ensure that we have real freedom of expression, and guarantees against censorship and control of, for example, opinions, information and opinion-forming. the fundamental human rights of freedom of expression and privacy are important democratic elements and must always be both protected and respected. the internet is therefore an important factor in our democratic society today and should remain so. i therefore want us to vote against amendment no 5 which would remove the following text: 'guarantee that the expression of controversial political views via the internet is not criminalised'. if this amendment were to pass, it would be a democratic setback. who decides what's controversial political views? it is a democratic right to express different political views. the right of internet users to permanently delete personal data contained on websites is also important. of course, cybercrime must be tackled, as all criminal activities, but it must also be done in a legally secure way, in accordance with criminal law, as in other cases of crime. cybercrime against children is particularly serious. here we follow the council of europe convention on the protection of children against sexual exploitation and sexual abuse. there are other groups that have been affected as well. i am thinking particularly of women who have been victims of the sex slave trade. the sex industry today uses the internet and the sexual violence that many women and children are subjected to. in this context, i would remind my colleagues that they can support a written declaration to put an end to this violence, written declaration no 94. i would like to conclude by also mentioning the danger we have seen in the so-called war on terrorism. this has sometimes led governments to impose unreasonable restrictions on freedom of speech and privacy. these restrictions have created a security risk for citizens. the security services of various countries have traded personal data obtained through internet surveillance. for example, when people have been forced to flee their country because of political oppression, their lives have been put in danger. i call for a very strong support for the report tomorrow!
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i am daily fascinated by the amazing tool that the internet is, but the internet, much as the eu opposes it, is a global network of computers, not a european one. to think that a decree from brussels or strasbourg could change that is quite incredible and very out of touch with the world. it can be argued that the eu is the wrong forum to solve the kind of problems highlighted in the report. let me give you a couple of examples. the report equates the right to the internet with the right to schooling. it is quite powerful when we know that the right or the opportunity to go to school is not a given in many eu countries. the protection and promotion of individuals' rights online and the balance that should exist between privacy and security are of paramount importance, but neither should be solved at eu level. it is an international problem which should be dealt with internationally in the first instance. another issue that is close to my heart is file sharing. it calls for the application of common criminal measures to protect intellectual property rights. i firmly believe that it is for the member states to decide what constitutes a criminal offence and what penalties may be imposed. it is totally unacceptable for the eu to go along with the music and film industries. especially considering they're trying to criminalize an entire generation. in conclusion, i would say that attempting to legislate at all in these areas is difficult because technology is much, much faster than policy.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the single market is the eu's absolute greatest contribution to freedom and prosperity in europe. it requires, among other things, effective antitrust legislation. what we are now considering is the question of damages for breaches of antitrust law. there are good reasons of principle for citizens and businesses to be able to claim damages. the report by mr lehne, in part unintentionally, highlights the problems and risks. the white paper talks about a european legal culture, but there is no such thing. we are not going to create regulations based on wishful thinking. the rapporteur believes that we are avoiding an americanization of the culture of damages. it's also wishful thinking. on the contrary, the risks are great. the division of responsibilities between the eu institutions and the member states is ignored. there is no objective and conditional analysis of what the subsidiarity principle says. the principle is simply not taken seriously. there are also many other ambiguities and risks of arbitrariness. the combined effects of paragraphs 7 and 11 raise concerns. the obvious starting point must be an action for damages for breach of antitrust law. it requires that the crime be established in a court of law. the court of first instance held that the court of first instance had not been entitled to rule on the question whether the contested decision was vitiated by an error of law. the combined provisions of paragraphs 15 and 18 allow the plaintiff to choose the most favourable legal system. it creates legal uncertainty and it leads to this kind of "form shopping" that becomes a real threat. the aim is to reduce information asymmetry by forcing companies to disclose information to the claimant, which results in a subjective handling of important business information that can be misused. there are far too many uncertainties and risks at this stage. the house should therefore reject this report and ask for a more thorough examination of the matter before we take a decision.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would also like to thank mr klaus-heiner lehne for a good and well-worked report which addresses important issues in competition law and provides for stronger consumer protection. the ecj grants individuals and companies the right to compensation for infringements of competition rules, but in practice, those who have suffered damage as a result of infringements of ec antitrust rules have rarely been compensated. we must therefore create mechanisms that increase trust and make it easier for people to assert their rights across borders. we know that consumers and small businesses are afraid to initiate proceedings because of concerns about lengthy procedures and, above all, high costs. changes in this direction will promote crossborder trade. if we are to have a functioning internal market throughout the eu, where people can rely on having their case tried in a fair way and receive full compensation for the damage, the loss, that has been caused, then we must also find new mechanisms to facilitate class action. when we talk about group cases, the united states always comes up and the american experience and excesses. we must of course learn from this, but we must not be intimidated. europe should have a european system, not an american one. if we do nothing, the situation will only get worse.
in writing. - we believe that the original purpose of the programme, to distribute intervention stocks to the most deprived, was oddly constructed from the outset. the mixing of agricultural policy with social policy can be heartbreaking, but it becomes complicated. the proportion of goods not coming from intervention stocks has steadily increased in recent years. at last count, about 85 percent of the food was bought on the open market! we believe that the distribution of food to the most deprived in the community should be abolished. the situation of the most deprived in the member states is to be handled by the member states or the municipalities. it is these political levels of responsibility who, through their social policy, are to ensure that all citizens have their right to a minimum standard of living. whether this is done through social assistance, food distribution or otherwise is to be decided by them. the committee on agriculture proposes that food aid be fully financed by the eu. it is astonishing what positions are being put forward in this committee just to get agricultural products on the market. as usual, we in junilistan can say that it is fortunate that the european parliament is not involved in the decision-making process on the eu's agricultural policy, because otherwise the union would fall into a trap of protectionism and heavy subsidies to various groups in the agricultural sector.
