in writing. - we understand that there can be major problems in the construction sector in spain and that there can be cases where unsuspecting citizens buy real estate in good faith, which it later turns out that construction companies did not have the legal right to build. however, these are problems which can and should be solved within the legal framework of the member state. when all domestic remedies have been exhausted, in accordance with article 35 of the echr, individual citizens may apply to the european court of human rights in strasbourg. there is no reason to introduce any legislation at eu level to deal with these issues or for the european parliament as an institution to intervene. nor do we want to get involved in the various alternative motions for resolutions tabled by spanish members, where domestic political complications are mixed up in the matter.
in writing. − we voted in favour of the report in sympathy with the complainants. however, we are of the opinion that the alleged breaches of spanish, eu and international law should be dealt with and solved by the relevant spanish authorities, the european court of justice and the european court of human rights.
in writing. - freedom of expression and the right to privacy are absolute rights which must not be undermined under any circumstances. we believe, of course, that these rights also apply to the internet and thus guarantee the right of individuals to express themselves freely. we also believe that the possibility of removing individual information from databases and websites should be taken for granted and call on companies to ensure that individuals can have their personal data deleted from databases. however, we believe that the lack of such a guarantee is primarily an international problem, which is therefore best solved through international rules and conventions. junilista is very positive about the guarantee of security and fundamental freedoms on the internet, but we are negative about some of the points in the report. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of intellectual property rights. we are strongly opposed to this, because we do not want european criminal law to be harmonized! the rapporteur also calls for the right to the internet to be equated with the right to education. we think this is a powerful statement, since the right and the opportunity to go to school in many eu countries is far from a given. however, the report's good intentions prevail and we have therefore voted in favour.
on behalf of the ppe-de group. – madam president, i have participated in a number of meetings in the arctic region focusing very much on climate change. normally the first day in such meetings is devoted to the serious effects for the region, its wildlife, the livelihood of its people etc. because of global warming. the second day is very often devoted to the opportunities in terms of geological exploitation. something of a contradiction. i would submit that rapid exploitation of geological resources would, of course, entail very serious risks. i agree that you cannot draw an exact parallel between the arctic and antarctica – i agree with the commissioner there. at the same time, since we do not have a careful, sustainable environment framework in place for the kind of activities that are now being explored by the nations in this region, i think this resolution sends a very important signal: be careful. the fact that all political groups are behind it, i think, is significant. we list three alternative ways forward: one in the form of an international treaty, with, of course, special provisions for this region compared to antarctica. second, a moratorium, pending new scientific research and a better understanding of the region and its vulnerability or sensitivity, but also pending the results of many energy alternatives that are now very progressively developing. maybe in the future we will not need those fossil reserves at all. so i think that, even if colleagues in this parliament may differ on the margin about the most responsible way forward, i think it is very significant that we are all behind this resolution. i want to stress that we want to go beyond just enhanced multilateral cooperation and dialogue; we want to ensure that the safety of the environment and the livelihoods of the people are protected.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking kathalijne, emine, michel cashman and others for a very good report. i am surprised and shocked, as are many others, that the leader of the ppe-de and the ppe-de spokesman, manfred weber, in amendment no 81 has proposed that the european parliament should reject the proposal for a directive on the grounds that it violates the principle of subsidiarity and would involve a disproportionate amount of bureaucracy, as i read in the swedish translation. commissioner špidla has commented on this. i am sure that all disabled women, and all other groups who have counted on the european parliament to ensure the civil human rights of their group as well, are deeply disappointed that the leadership of the conservative group equates human rights with bureaucracy. i therefore call on the whole house to vote against ppe-de amendment no 81 tomorrow. i also think it is important that women should not continue to be discriminated against by insurance companies precisely because they are women and older, but are healthy and live longer than men as a group. i also hope that parliament will have the courage to make it clear that tax-funded education is for everyone. religion is certainly important to many europeans, and i respect that, but we live in a secularised society. no, manfred weber, your market freedom of contract is not as important as basic civil human rights. ask the citizens of the eu, they are wiser and more modern than you in the epp-ed. the expectations are high and i hope that everyone will have the courage to vote for this tomorrow and not against it as you have advocated.
on behalf of the ppe-de group. – mr president, we have been waiting quite a long time for the commission’s plan on sustainable production and consumption. we received the proposal a few months ago. although it is comprehensive, unfortunately i have to say that the overall content is rather limited and poor. having read some of the early drafts of the commission proposals, i know that, in the environment dg in particular, there were much more ambitious plans to start with. so it is obvious that work on these issues has to continue and be more in-depth in the future. today we are discussing the ecolabel scheme. the revision offers a good opportunity for this label to move away from the margins of the market, to capture a much larger share of the market and to help push demand for eco-friendly products. the new rules represent a significant improvement. they are more dynamic, will apply a life-cycle approach and should be able to attract significantly more attention and interest among both companies and consumers. as mr dimas said, they will be able to help consolidate eco-labelling and do away with a number of voluntary schemes that are very often confusing to consumers. however, we still have a problem, namely how to inform consumers and the markets about the label. marketing support for this label has been very limited in the past. the resources allocated have been minuscule compared to the resources behind a lot of branding in the market in general. i hope this will change, first of all by businesses seeing the ecolabel as an important instrument in the future. i also hope, as mr vakalis said, that public procurement will in the future expand in green areas and use the ecolabel as a platform. i also hope that the commission will be more proactive in its support for the scheme. i thank all those who have been involved in the work. i think we have done quite a good job in a few weeks. we managed to clear up some of the confusion around food products, in particular fishery products, in the final hours. finally, i would like to refer back to ms mcavan, who made a reference to the financial crisis and the meeting in london today. i think that reference is very relevant. we are facing at least three parallel crises today – the financial crisis, the climate crisis and what i would call the ecosystem crisis, or overuse of natural resources. it is only by tackling the root causes together – that is to say, unsustainable use of resources – through investing in low-carbon and eco-friendly production and products, that we will be able to build a better future. i think this ecolabel scheme is one of many instruments that can help us to do that.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the voluntary environmental management scheme emas basically involves a company or organisation conducting an environmental inquiry, looking at the environmental impact of its activities, developing an environmental policy, setting objectives and developing action programmes. unfortunately, emas has not been so successful so far, with only 4 200 organisations having registered since its inception in 1993, which is low compared to the 35 000 organisations in the eu that have registered for iso 14001. a revision of emas is therefore justified simply to make it more attractive and less bureaucratic for companies and organisations. the negotiations between the council and parliament have included several tightening of the emu. for example, the commission will produce comprehensive reference documents for as many sectors as possible under a priority programme. the text also clearly states that the emas logo may not be confused with other product-related eco-labels under any circumstances. this is a clear improvement. a while ago my colleague, mr jens holm, asked the commission whether all the commission's directorates-general are affiliated to emas, which the european parliament is. one might think that the eu institutions should set a good example. but the answer was that the commission has no internal emission target of its own and that only five of the commission dgs have joined emas. i think that is above all criticism, and i would therefore like to repeat the question to the commission: when will the commission ensure that all its directorates-general join ema?
in writing. 'the content and organisation of education and training are a matter for national competence.' this is stated in one of the first paragraphs of mr takkula's own-initiative report on 'education for the children of migrants'. but it is not long before there is talk of what should be included in the curriculum, what teacher training should look like or that member states "must get immigrants involved in a wide range of leisure activities". while it is important to ensure that all children, including those of migrants, receive the best possible education, i wonder whether this really belongs at eu level? the answer is found in the above paragraph. i am voting for a narrower but more rigorous eu. i therefore voted against the own-initiative report, although i think it touches on a very important subject which we must look at more closely at national level.
in writing. - we would point out once again that in the current eu cooperation it is established that the member states have exclusive responsibility for the organisation of education. the draft statement contains a number of good ideas, but with all due respect to the substance of the matter, we believe that this report is outside the competence of the european union. the principle of subsidiarity says that this should be handled by the member states themselves. it follows from this principle that the european parliament's committee on culture and education should be abolished, since it deals with matters outside the sphere of eu cooperation. for these reasons we have voted against the report.
in writing. − we fully support the notions of anti-discrimination and equality in access, and strongly believe in creating inclusive school systems and inclusive schools. we believe that actions should be taken to assist all vulnerable pupils. however, we believe that member states are best suited for ensuring accessible and inclusive schooling for its citizens and residents alike. we believe that democratic control of the school system by the people it serves can only be ensured if the member states formulate and implement education policy.
in writing. the junilist is a supporter of the free internal market, which not only brings economic prosperity but also extends the freedom of citizens by allowing them to move freely between countries within europe's borders. we share the rapporteur's view that the lack of transposition of the directive in question in some member states is, on balance, disappointing and call on the member states to fully implement directive 2004/38/ec, so that the right to free movement becomes a reality. however, we are strongly opposed to increasing the funds and allocating them to a specific budget line to support national and local projects aimed at integrating citizens and their family members when they are staying in another member state. such matters are the responsibility of each member state. however, the report's clear position on the completion of the free internal market is overlooked and we have therefore voted in favour.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy. we share the overall idea of the report of improved citizenship of the union and improved free movement. however, we do not believe that the solution to the problems raised lies in further information campaigns. we also want to emphasize that the question of voting rights in local elections is a matter for the member states.
in writing. - we need to strengthen union citizenship, as it is the cornerstone of free movement. this report addresses a number of areas where people's opportunities to benefit from the eu's free movement can be improved. since the idea of cooperation is based on the dismantling of borders and greater mobility, i chose to support this report despite the fact that there was less good writing on the promotion of 'european identity' and the establishment of a 'european dimension' in schools.
in writing. - the rapporteur's praise of eu cohesion policy seems to know no bounds. this is despite the fact that this policy is a frightening example of what increased central governance means. lack of control and poor follow-up of the funds allocated each year under eu funds projects leads to significant amounts of money going to the wrong pockets. this is now common knowledge. as recently as november 2008, the european court of auditors found that 11 per cent of the 42 billion euros approved in 2007 under eu cohesion policy should never have been paid. however, this is not mentioned in the report. it's unfortunate, but hardly surprising. we have, of course, voted against the report.
in writing. - junilista believes that the eu is a union of values and therefore i am a strong advocate of a broad anti-discrimination directive. i consider that to be a matter of course for the functioning of the internal market and for the respect of inviolable human rights. it's very important to me that no one is discriminated against because of a disability. i support amendment no 87 because i believe that the taxpayers in each country should ensure that disabled people have the financial means to be considered full-fledged borrowers on the credit market. i also voted in favour of the report as a whole.
in writing. - the proposal for a resolution by the five largest groups contains many important views on european history over the last hundred years; the additional proposals from the uen group, among others, are also commendable, but for editorial reasons not all of the proposals fit into the text of the resolution. there are many tragedies and individual heroism that could be described in a resolution on the european conscience and totalitarian dictatorships. but unfortunately there is not enough room for everything and we have therefore been forced to vote against some of the amendments to the resolution. we have, however, voted in favour of the resolution as a whole.
in writing. − we of course regret the victims of all aggressive and authoritarian regimes, regardless of the atrocities that have been carried out in europe or for example in the former european colonies. we are however deeply concerned about all direct or indirect efforts by politicians or parliaments trying to influence the general perception of historical facts. this task should be left to independent academic research and public debate. otherwise there is a risk that every new majority in parliament will try to change history by describing the worst enemies of society, and that the discussion on european history will be used for short-term campaign purposes. we therefore chose to abstain in the final vote.
in writing. - cultural matters are a policy area which falls within the political responsibility of the member states to decide on. this resolution deals with matters which are outside the competence of the european union. we who take the principle of subsidiarity seriously therefore believe that the european parliament's committee on culture and education should be abolished. we have therefore voted against the report.
in writing. - we voted today in favour of the schmitt own-initiative report on better schools: an agenda for european cooperation (a6-0124/2009). the report highlights many of the major challenges facing european schools and makes a number of good recommendations. for example, to better prepare students in schools and universities for an increasingly flexible labour market where employers' requirements are changing rapidly. however, we are referring to a single paragraph in the report which attributes the increase in violence in schools to factors such as the increase in class differences and the increasing cultural diversity across the eu member states. the increase in school violence is a serious social problem with many and complex causes that should not be reduced by simplistic descriptions of causes.
in writing. − "the member states are responsible for the organisation, content and reform of education and training". this is stated in the first paragraph of the schmitt own-initiative report on 'better schools'. that's as far as i go. the eu shall facilitate exchanges between schools and the mobility of students. the eu, on the other hand, should stay away from anything else that concerns schools. unfortunately, the schmitt resolution fails to live up to its initial promise. it is quite immediate that the question of how schools are to be financed, evaluated and what should be in the curriculum, etc., is being raised. the very things that the eu should not be doing; the very things that the member states themselves should be doing. i am voting for a narrower but more rigorous eu. i therefore voted against both the own-initiative report and the alternative resolution.

member of the commission. − madam president, it is not easy in five minutes to give the commission response to seven excellent reports, but i should not miss this opportunity to thank all the rapporteurs – mrs morgan, mrs ţicău, mr chichester, mr vidal-quadras, mr mussa, mr paparizov and mr belet – and all the shadows. i also would like to thank mrs niebler who worked really hard to let us come to this report in a very limited amount of time. i will start with the internal energy market because two years ago we started with an ambitious goal: to create a truly competitive and truly european energy market for the benefit of the citizens of the european union. the tool to achieve this goal is the third internal energy market package for gas and electricity. today we are close to the adoption of this package and thus to achieving this goal. the trialogue has now succeeded in agreeing to the compromise. the commission fully supports this compromise. if adopted tomorrow by plenary, it will give the european union the clear regulatory framework needed to ensure a properly functioning internal market and to promote much-needed investment. first, it will facilitate cross-border energy trade with the establishment of an agency for the cooperation of national energy regulators, with binding decision powers, to complement national regulators. this ensures the proper handling of cross-border cases and enables the european union to develop a real european network. secondly, the new legislation will promote cross-border and regional collaboration and investment with a new european network for transmission system operators. eu grid operators will cooperate and develop network codes and security standards, as well as plan and coordinate the investments needed at eu level. thirdly, it will provide for more effective regulatory oversight from national regulators that will be much more independent and have all the necessary means. fourthly, it will ensure effective unbundling of the generation and transmission of energy so as to eliminate any conflict of interests, promote network investment and prevent discriminatory behaviour. this legislation will also ensure greater transparency, thereby guaranteeing equal access to information, making pricing more transparent, increasing trust in the market, and helping to avoid any possible manipulation or any sort of manipulation of the market. this is not just about a properly functioning internal market but more generally about ensuring that the eu can rise to the challenges we are facing in the field of energy: climate change, increased import dependency, security of supply, global competitiveness. in particular, a functioning internal market is a key element of the eu’s effort to tackle climate change. without a competitive electricity market, an emissions trading scheme will never work properly, and our aims regarding renewable energy will not succeed. the compromise reached also strikes a good balance between the positions of parliament and the council. the rapporteurs have already presented to you the key elements on which the political compromise reached strengthens the common position adopted by the council in january 2009. i would like to highlight a few key issues. parliament’s call for stronger consumer protection and the fight against energy poverty is now enshrined in the legislative texts. smart meters, allowing for consumers to be precisely informed of their consumption and promoting energy efficiency, are provided with a target of 80% of consumers to be reached by 2020. the powers and independence of national regulators have been strengthened, as well as the powers of the agency, and the rules on effective unbundling have been made more efficient. most importantly, we have also seen developments on the ground. a lot of companies have restructured their business and how they deal with networks and consumers. today at the hannover messe, i saw that smart metering is making good progress and companies are taking these decisions on board. energy efficiency is definitely one of the key policies of the european energy policy. the building sector still has considerable potential to further improve its energy efficiency, at the same time creating new jobs and stimulating growth. i warmly thank parliament for its support for the commission’s proposal on the recast of the energy efficiency of buildings directive. the discussions and proposals demonstrate that parliament shares the policy objectives and wishes to vigorously improve current performance. it is not an easy area, as there is a lot of subsidiarity, so we need to strike a good balance. the directive provides a framework to upgrade the energy performance of the eu’s buildings. there is a lot of clarification, and this reinforces the effect of the directive, like the principles on the ‘cost-optimal’ method, requirements on control mechanisms and many definitions. there is the issue of financing instruments that are very important for stimulating energy efficiency measures, but these need to be tackled in the appropriate legislation and initiatives. consequently, the buildings directive is constrained in what it can do with respect to financial and fiscal issues. very efficient buildings, whether they are called low- or zero-energy buildings, or next-generation buildings, are a new feature introduced into the directive by the commission. it is essential to make this provision ambitious but realistic, and with some flexibility given the eu’s varied climate and economic conditions. unified requirements like net zero-energy buildings would not exactly fulfil this requirement and would therefore be excessive. harmonisation is crucial for the internal market. i fully support parliament’s wish to have a single methodology for calculating cost-optimal levels of requirements. however, prescribing a common methodology for the energy performance calculation itself could be counter-productive, causing delays in the implementation of the directive by several years due to the complexity of member states’ building codes. this is therefore very complex and difficult legislation, but i very much rely on parliament strengthening this legal instrument. the rapporteur also spoke about tyres that can play a significant part in reducing road transport energy intensity and emissions. the combined impact of this proposal together with the type-approval legislation on tyres should bring around 5% fuel savings on the total eu fleet by 2020. this proposal will provide consumers with standardised information on fuel efficiency. it will also give information on wet grip, which is another essential parameter for tyres, and external rolling noise. by doing so, the label will pull the market towards better-performing tyres while avoiding improvements on one aspect being achieved at the costs of others. the report which will be put to the vote this week will add some significant improvements to the initial proposal, such as the change from a directive into a regulation, which will reduce transposal costs and ensure that the same application date for the label applies to all. the inclusion of snow tyres within the scope of the label, with the adoption of a specific grading as soon as possible, will also benefit those driving in ice or snow conditions. it is important that we find the best way of displaying the label. there is some discussion on this. we would very much like you to support our proposal to integrate the label on the stickers which are currently delivered with each tyre to indicate their dimension, load index etc. i believe we have really made remarkable progress on the energy dossier during this legislation and, most importantly, it is supported by our citizens and by industry. having been at the hannover messe, we have seen a huge drive from industry for energy efficiency, not only in the areas we have now legislated on, but also in other branches of industry such as different appliances used for end consumption and to produce tools for different types of industry. energy efficiency, energy and europe: these are the key words for what we have achieved during this legislation. i would like to thank all those involved and particularly all members of the european parliament that supported this. as a final word of apology, i could have another five minutes but i will use only one minute when i am given the floor for the second time. thank you for letting me finish this speech.
on behalf of the ppe-de group. – madam president, i would like to thank mrs morgan for her report on the electricity markets. as a shadow rapporteur it has been a pleasure to work together with her, and i think it is fair to say that we have been able to achieve a liberalisation of the energy markets. at least, we have made some important steps, and thereby we have opened up the market. i think it is fair to say that the report on electricity also played the lead in the process that we are discussing here today. i think it is important to state that this is also in contrast to those in different countries who would like to have more protected borders regarding energy markets. we have a debate in sweden in which there are those who would like to have some sort of protectionism regarding the export of electricity. but that would hinder and damage all the things that we can achieve by an open electricity market. it is by opening up the markets that we can do something about climate change and can make the best use of renewables and nuclear power. we can also ensure that we have a good supply of energy all over the union, binding countries and markets together. i think the steps we are taking with this energy market package should be seen as important ones. although there are more steps to be taken, we have contributed to the energy security of europe and to the fight against climate change.
the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? i was impressed that you at least took the initiative to try to enumerate all these decentralized agencies. there are many, many of them. i would argue that the discharge of the eu authorities has become increasingly important precisely because the number of authorities has increased. the budget and staff have also been increased. the total number of authorities has increased from 11 in 1995 to 27 today. in 2007, the budget for all the authorities was eur 1 243 500 000. in 1995, the average budget for an authority was eur 7 million, but today it is over eur 22 million. the staff has increased in the same dramatic way. in 1995, there were on average 38 people working in each office, and today there are 155. i think this increase is remarkable in itself and something we have to consider, that is, whether it is a reasonable instrument and whether it is reasonable with this kind of increase. it also requires more of us to deal with the discharge and to devote more time and energy to it. we have therefore chosen to deal with 21 of these authorities for which we are responsible in specific reports and also to draw up a horizontal report which addresses the problems common to the vast majority of the authorities. i am very pleased to note that the majority of the authorities receive pure audit reports from the court of auditors. so they were shot correctly. at the same time, many of them - yes, almost all of them - still have problems year after year with large surpluses, with compliance with the financial regulation and the staff regulations. this is something we have to consider, namely the fact that year after year we repeat certain criticisms while no change is taking place. this means that we have to think more about how we hold these governments to account and how we actually govern them. in the horizontal report i propose, among other things, that we should make flat-rate cuts when the budget is not used up enough or when we cannot appoint all the posts. it is also suggested that we should have a joint host authority to help the smaller authorities with the heavy administrative tasks. i think that's very important. we have chosen this year to look specifically at four authorities which have received dirty audit reports from the court of auditors and which have particularly serious problems. it was the european police college, the european gnss supervisory authority, which we usually call galileo, the european railway agency and frontex. i am pleased to note that three of them, galileo, the railway agency and frontex, have been able to obtain a discharge after reviewing all the information they have given us. unfortunately, cepol, the european police college, has still provided a lot of information, but far from all of it. therefore, we cannot grant a discharge yet. the remaining problem is the issue of private use of eu citizens' tax money, such as private use for furniture, private mobile phone calls and private travel. we have asked for information on this but have not received full information. that is why i and the committee are proposing that we postpone the discharge until cepol has explained all this. in addition, there is a fraud investigation in the olaf concerning this authority. it is serious, and we must show that we are taking this seriously and getting to the bottom of it. that's why we're postponing the discharge. it's not something i enjoy doing, but i'd like to say it's the only responsible thing to do. we need to have all the paperwork on the table before we can grant a discharge.
the committee on employment and social affairs has tabled an opinion. - mr president! - what is it? employment policy is part of cohesion policy. there are still some errors and shortcomings in this area and some points in the monitoring on which one can have views. some 27% of cohesion policy is the responsibility of the employment committee. as regards the payments, most of them are from the period 2000-2006. the positive thing is that 100 per cent of the payment appropriations have been used during the period. what is sometimes a problem is the lack of evidence of indirect costs and personnel costs and their overestimation. that is why we have also said that we should have a more standardised way of indicating this, combined with the better controls at member state level that we have in the new period, which could make it better in the future.
the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? it will be very tacky, but i would like to take the opportunity to thank our eminent chairman herbert bösch. i think he has led the committee in a fantastic way over the last two and a half years. there was not much debate about my reports on the discharge of the authorities. i therefore hope that all colleagues will choose to support them, regardless of how they voted in committee or what they have suggested in the past. it is important that parliament should be unanimous on these matters. since this is a joint debate on all the reports, i would like to comment on a report other than my own. i am very much in favour of the casaca report, which i think is very good, particularly paragraph 105, where we have decided that parliament should not allocate extra money to cover the deficit in the voluntary pension fund for us meps. i know that many people find these points very controversial, but for me they are at least self-evident. there are many who have criticised this fund for many, many years. i would like to say that i think the fund itself is something of a scandal. we are all affected by the global financial crisis. at a time when ordinary people are suffering from pension cuts, politicians should not save their own skin by using more of the taxpayers' money for their own pensions. this is a message which i hope the leadership of this parliament will really take to heart and not fill the fund with more tax money. on the contrary, we must put an end to this and as soon as possible.
rapporteur. − mr president, i think it is fair to say that we are now in a new era of energy policy, where there is a need for energy policy to be consistent with a policy for stopping climate change, and at the same time to be combined with a policy for energy security. those three legs – energy policy, climate policy and energy security – must function together. from my perspective this highlights the importance of nuclear power. it is important that the existing nuclear power plants are managed in as safe and secure a way as possible, but it is also important that we have clear rules to ensure that the future power plants of the european union are as secure and safe as possible. this is not only a matter of preparing the ground for more nuclear power. i think that there is rather large support for such a policy in the european union, that it is growing and that we need to live up to the responsibilities that this gives us all. i respect those who are hesitant about or are against the use of nuclear power, but whatever opinion and whatever view we have on nuclear power, there can be no disagreement on the need for the rules to be as safe and secure as possible for the nuclear power we have. it is from this perspective that i would like to present this report on the community framework for nuclear safety. there is a legal basis for this and there has been a debate in committee on whether all the procedures have been fulfilled. a letter has been sent from the committee on legal affairs to the committee on industry, research and energy saying that if it is a new proposal then there is a need for a new opinion from the group of experts. my view, and the view of the majority of the committee, is that this is a revised proposal which we have been working on in the european union for nearly seven years. it has been changed because of an opinion from the group of experts and because of the opinion given by parliament and i must say that now is the time to reach a decision. i hope that council will be able to take this decision this spring. if we do not and if we delay this process, we will see in reality that new nuclear power plants will be planned and constructed without this community framework. so this is the time for action. those who are trying to hinder this because of their attitude to nuclear power are in reality preventing the european union from having a community framework establishing as safe and secure rules as possible. i have tried to position my report in three ways. first of all to give a clear structure on the responsibilities of member states and governments, licence holders and national regulators. secondly, i have been very clear about the independence that the national regulators will have and i am strengthening the requirements on national regulators, meaning that they must be able to act when they see any risk of a nuclear power plant not fulfilling the security rules. thirdly, we are including in an annex the rules of the iaea as binding rules, thereby making this community framework clear, strict and firm, and that is the note on which i end.
member of the commission. − mr president, as has been rightly said the aim of this proposal is to create a community-wide framework. it was not easy to achieve this. we based our work on the western european nuclear regulators’ association (wenra) and on experience in the high level group on nuclear safety and waste management. we also worked together with the council, which had discussed more or less the same proposals in 2003. i believe that it strikes the right balance at this stage; i believe it gives european citizens a clear understanding that there is a community-wide framework. i am confident this framework will evolve with time, but i very much welcome mr hökmark’s report because it strengthens the proposal while keeping the balance we need. it is the national regulators who are responsible for the safety of the installations running in their countries. these issues are so sensitive that we cannot, and should not, sidestep them, but we do need constantly to improve nuclear safety standards. i believe this directive gives us that opportunity.
rapporteur. − mr president, first of all i think it is important to say that no one should be against increasing the level of safety and strengthening the safety rules. there must be no tendency, just because one dislikes nuclear power and is against its use, to underplay the importance of the rules we need. in that sense, i think it is a step forward that we have a common community framework because it creates a consistency, a transparency and an opportunity to ensure our common development towards higher standards. this directive really does apply to existing power plants because it increases the importance and the independence of national regulators – which is crucial – and it paves the way for us to secure higher and higher requirements in order to have, as it were, a ‘race to the top’ regarding safety. let us be very frank about this. we will have nuclear power in the future, whatever all of us here today agree about on this issue. in my view, it is important that we can lay the ground for having more nuclear power plants. they must be credible and there must exist a credibility in public opinion, but – even more importantly – there must be a substantial, real safety in managing nuclear power, as there should be in every other thing we do. the existing rules are good and strong, but we are making them more consistent, and that is a step forward. i would like to thank you for your cooperation and for the discussion. i think that we have achieved a better result, and i hope that the council will listen to this opinion.
mr president, i would like to thank you. as an eu sceptic, i often feel a certain amount of cynicism when various eu institutions come up with absurd and ridiculous proposals such as article 47. such proposals help to undermine the unwarranted respect that many citizens of eu countries feel for the eu's aspirations the aspiration to transfer power from democratic member states to a bureaucratic brussels. the fight against centralisation and bureaucracy is therefore facilitated by such proposals. at the same time, however, i take my role here in the european parliament seriously. we must stop this development, and i hope that a majority of members of this house feel the same way. if not, i hope that at least a majority will fear the verdict of the electorate at the beginning of june and therefore realise that in their own self-evident interest they must reject this proposal. if the subsidiarity principle cannot even persuade the eu to keep its paws off the sport fishing in the stockholm archipelago, then the future of the european project is bleak.
madam president, i would like to thank you. a global financial crisis is shaking the world economy, and proposals are now being made to prevent a repeat. more regulation, more oversight is in. the starting point, however, must be: what went wrong? let me give you 50 seconds to summarize the reasons. we have an ownerless capitalism. financial firms are run by civil servants. these can design systems that give themselves gigantic bonuses and pensions as profits rise. profits can be increased in the short term by management increasing the level of risk in companies through lower equity. when the risks are paid off, the management has received their money, the losses are taken by others. those who would change such a policy have no incentive. bank depositors know that there are deposit guarantees. everyone knows that most banks are too big to fail. they're being bailed out by the taxpayers. valuation institutes know that they will not get any assignments if they question the soundness of the client companies. the policy of central banks and the treasury is that bubbles cannot be punctured. as a result, they grow unreasonably large. are we discussing solutions to these problems? the answer is no!
the commission is not prepared to accept this. the financial and economic crisis has shown that we in europe must be able to act together. we should be glad that europe has had and has the euro, not 16 different currencies. it's lightened the load. it was only when the euro countries met in paris this autumn that the crisis could be stabilised and a recovery could begin. then the global effort followed with the g20 summit, which was the start of something new - a world where the world's great powers met on equal terms. we must now make sure that we are better equipped the next time a crisis strikes. the directives under discussion today are important and i think they are balanced. we need more openness and transparency in the market, more opportunities to act across borders and improved supervision. we must also fight protectionism and, in my view, support free trade. we must also limit risk-taking and stop over-reaching. the free market also needs its own framework and rules. i am a liberal and i can of course agree with that. however, we must be careful not to overregulate. that risk is in the present mood. let us not forget that the market economy creates wealth.
in writing. - it is not an end in itself that there must be cotton production in the european union. the union must look at the world market for cotton as a whole and enable eu consumers to buy cotton as cheaply as possible, regardless of where it was produced, as long as it is produced under certain ethical and environmental conditions. i am strongly opposed to this report. i would like to say again that it is fortunate that the european parliament does not have a say in the eu's agricultural policy, because otherwise the union would fall into a trap of protectionism and heavy subsidies to all the different groups in the agricultural sector.
in writing. - this report is essentially a report on the activities of the committee on petitions of the european parliament. but since this report refers in a few places to, celebrates and expresses hope that the treaty of lisbon will soon be ratified, i have chosen to vote against the report as a whole. i consider the lisbon treaty to be rejected in principle, since the citizens of a member state have voted against it in a referendum. in addition, there are still more member states where a majority of the voters would certainly have voted against the lisbon treaty if they had been given the chance. i cannot support the ignorance of the european parliament's committee on petitions in the report's wording.
in writing. - i wholeheartedly support the idea of putting the internal market for natural gas into competition. however, it is wrong to require member states to take concrete measures to promote the increased use of biogas and biomass gas. this is a matter for each member state. i have therefore voted against the proposal put forward by the committee.
in writing. − there are very good reasons to strive for better energy efficiency and lower emissions from our vehicles. however, a harmonised eu labelling system for vehicle tyres is problematic. in particular, previous experience with labelling is a matter for further reflection. take the labelling system for household products. the reluctance of industry to increase the requirements for a certain level of energy labelling, for example for refrigerators, has led to the fact that the labelling system is today hopelessly complex and difficult to manage. what politicians hoped would encourage further development and provide consumers with much-needed guidance has instead contributed to a slowdown in the development of better products and made it harder for consumers to make the right choices. i believe that the eu can play an important role in reducing the environmental impact of vehicles. unlike the commission and the committee, however, i am not in favour of detailed regulation, but of higher minimum requirements for tyre manufacturers who want to sell their products on the internal market. eu policymakers should create sustainable and beneficial frameworks for society and business, not indulge in small-scale governance. since the commission and the committee responsible do not seem to share my view, i have chosen to vote against the report.
in writing. the commission wants the current price regulation of so-called roaming to be extended to cover not only voice calls but also sms traffic and data roaming. calls for more limited regulation and points out that price regulation should be temporary and that the market should eventually solve the high roaming charges; i voted in favour of the european parliament's position, which is a little more market-oriented than the commission's proposal. i voted against the legislative resolution, because price regulation is de facto wrong in the long run.
in writing. - i have today decided to abstain from voting on the so-called roaming ii proposal which proposes to regulate prices in the market for mobile telephony services abroad. the proposal, which was a compromise between the major political groups, contained many good ideas, including more information for customers to avoid huge telecommunications bills after a stay abroad. the reason for my lowered voice is simple. i don't think politicians in the eu should set prices on free markets. the point of a market economy is that price is determined by supply and demand, not by what politicians in brussels perceive as 'fair'. already, price regulation is in place through the so-called roaming i. the consequence has been that operators cluster around the price range, which is not in the interests of consumers. the task of politicians is to ensure fair competition in the internal market. i agree that this is not the case for roaming services. however, instead of soviet-style price regulation, measures that promote competition, such as a ban on the large operators from pricing smaller operators seeking access to networks abroad, would have been better.
in writing. - we voted against this report on a control system for fisheries policy. we welcome the proposals for stronger enforcement measures, but we are critical of the emphasis on control of recreational fishing. it is not reasonable to regulate recreational fishing when it is in fact industrial fishing that is responsible for the problems of overfishing in our seas.
in writing. the european parliament voted today to include sport and recreational fishing in the common fisheries policy. the eu has thus begun to regulate one of our most popular pastimes. the proposal is absurd. firstly, the fishing waters are those of each country, not brussels. secondly, recreational fishermen's activities do not have a significant impact on the stock. thirdly, this is legislation which will be impossible to comply with. just look at sweden, where we have 7,500 miles of straight coastline. do the eu bureaucrats and my colleagues in this house really think that it should be possible to control what is happening at all times and in all places? a law that cannot be enforced is a very bad law. on the other hand, i am not particularly surprised by the proposal. the eu is like a steam locomotive that has started to roll towards its goal of becoming a fully-fledged federal state. it's big, heavy, and very difficult to stop once it's rolled. and anyone who gets in the way gets run over. today is a dark day for those of us who want a well-functioning and solid european cooperation, but above all it is a very dark day for recreational fishermen in all the member states.
for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i'd like to ask the same question myself. where is the council in this debate? it's the council we're looking at, its budget management. i think it is remarkable that the council is not here at the moment to answer our questions. we in the committee on budgetary control, and in particular our rapporteur, mr søren bo søndergaard, have raised several points of concern regarding the financial management of the council of ministers. we have repeatedly pointed this out but have not received satisfactory answers. two examples: in 2006, the council spent eur 12.6 million on travel. this money was intended for interpretation. i would like to ask the council, but if the council cannot answer, perhaps the commission can: where did the trips go in 2006? what does this 12 million euro represent? the council also has secret accounts, the hors budget accounts. the internal auditor has called on the council to close these accounts, but without success. i'd like to ask why it hasn't happened. how many of these accounts are there besides the budget? what's in these accounts? our job as members of the european parliament is to scrutinise the council's use of the budget. we do this because we represent the taxpayers. the council's job is to answer our questions. now we want answers to these questions. what happened to that 12 million euros? what's in the secret accounts? how many secret accounts are there? if we do not get a satisfactory answer - which we have not yet received - then we will not grant the council discharge tomorrow.
for the ind/dem group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. we are faced here with one of the really important questions of principle. it can be said that i, as a representative of an eu-sceptic party, believe that the council should be the dominant body in european cooperation. this parliament will be devoted to the internal market and cross-border environmental issues. however, there is a third issue that has now come up. the council and this parliament are in principle equally responsible for the budget and where the taxpayers' money goes. now, in my view, it is unprecedented that the council is keeping secret where money is going. that's why we don't have transparency. citizens cannot say 'i accept this because i know what the money has been spent on'. that is why i say - and as far as i can understand all the speakers in this parliament agree for once - that there can be no question of granting discharge to the council until we know where the money has gone.
mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, what is it? brian crowley is a very good colleague, and he often makes some very clever suggestions. this time, however, i find it difficult to follow his intentions. the commission proposes to increase the term of protection for music recordings from the current 50 years to 95 years. so it is more or less a doubling. that this is going too far should be something most people would agree with. the compromise now being discussed is 70 years old, so it is going in the right direction. there are still a number of questions. will an extension increase cultural diversity and facilitate the creation of new works? what has the extension of copyright in the united states meant for developments there? has it strengthened the status of the artists, or are the record companies the big winners? can we justify an extension that dramatically increases the duration of protection? is it not too easy to say that creativity and the desire to create are directly linked to the length of the protection period? for me, these questions remain unanswered. as a liberal, i believe in copyright and its purpose, and i can agree with several of my colleagues in this house. it is obviously important to ensure innovation and to have control over one's own work, including financial control. but our legal interventions in this house must be proportionate, which i do not think is the case in this example. in my country, the issue of access to works on the internet is under discussion, especially after the high-profile trial of the so-called pirate bay judgment. at a time when copyright is the subject of intense debate, i therefore believe that we would be making a mistake if we accepted the commission's proposal to extend the term of protection of musical works from 50 to 95 years. therefore, like sharon bowles, i will reject the motion in its entirety. i believe that the commission should be given a second chance. commissioner, think again, do it again and come back!
mr president, i would like to thank you. the proposal to extend copyright to 95 years is a horrifying example of how the big record companies have managed to lobby a proposal that completely suits their purposes. such a large extension of copyright protection will only affect individual consumers and hamper the development of new music. that is why we in the gue/ngl group have tabled amendments calling for the 95 years to be removed. we also call for the entire directive to be rejected. the rights to the birthday song happy birthday are said to be owned by the north american company warner. people who celebrate birthdays in public barely dare to sing that song in some places in the united states for fear of reprisals and fines. it's an absurd example of how bad things get when you let special interests and industry run politics. that's the united states. the copyright term there is already as long as 95 years. let that not be the case here in europe. reject the 95-year requirement and reject the whole directive! i wonder, moreover, whether the commission has carried out any impact assessment of how much of the future revenue will go to individual artists and how much of the revenue actually stays with the big companies.
mr president, i would like to thank you. let's get one thing absolutely clear: an extension of copyright protection would not lead to more or better music. honestly, what 25-year-old musician would say, no, i'm not going to record this record, because i'm only getting paid until i'm 75 and not until i'm 120? it does not even favor the poor musicians, since it is not their records that are still selling more than 50 years after the recording, but rather it favors either the big record companies or those who have already made a lot of money on their recordings. i think this has completely lost its proportions. the person who today discovers the cure for cancer will in and of itself receive the nobel prize in medicine but in addition receive protection for his invention for a maximum of 20 years, while the person who will record a record then will have that invention, or rather that record, protected for 95 years. it's out of proportion! i can agree that 70 years is better than 95, but whatever the case, i think the best thing would be to reject the whole proposal.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the whole premise of this report is wrong. it is based on article 95 of the treaty, which guarantees the freedom of the market and therefore does not concern public health or patients. the right to good health care in all member states should be a priority, but instead the commission's starting point is a market where health care is like any other commodity. this directive gives priority to citizens who can afford to pay large sums for travel and accommodation and to those who have good knowledge of and contacts within the healthcare bureaucracy. it's about the high-income, well-educated, not the most needy. the amendment by the committee on the environment to allow member states to grant prior authorisation before the treatment is carried out may be considered by some to be a good thing. the problem is that it is fraught with limitations and the burden of proof for member states to issue it is heavy. public planning will be hampered, and national health care is at risk of being drained. in the end, it will be for the commission and the court of justice to decide whether the prior authorization of the member states is proportionate. when the directive is based on article 95 of the treaty, it will be the market and not the quality of care that will govern.
mr president, i would like to thank you. today's decision is one of the ones i am most proud of that we will be taking here in the european parliament, namely a decision to open up europe and guarantee free movement for those who are sick and in need of care, for those for whom free movement can be a matter of life or death, a decision that everyone should have the opportunity to decide for themselves about their own care and that it should not be only those who are well informed or rich who should have that opportunity, but we should make it possible for everyone to seek care wherever they want. you social democrats want to force people to ask for advance notice. in plain english, this means that you want to force the sick to ask permission before going to the doctor, at least if they do so in another eu country. why are you doing this? mr president, i would like to thank you. all patients should have the right to care when they need it. we social democrats think it is important that people can seek treatment abroad, for example when queues are too long in their home country. that is why the swedish social democratic government took the initiative of a directive in this area. however, the thickness of the wallet must never be the determining factor in healthcare, and decisions about healthcare should be taken by people close to you, not by bureaucrats in the eu system. the european commission's proposal for a directive leaves too much power to eu bureaucrats. furthermore, it does not take into account all those who cannot afford to be out with hundreds of thousands of dollars. nevertheless, the swedish members of the european parliament have uncritically welcomed the commission's proposal. we have, however, proposed and worked hard to make it easier for people with no big pockets to go abroad for treatment. we have also worked hard to make it clear that healthcare is a responsibility of the member states, not something for eu bureaucrats to decide. we haven't gotten as far as we want. i would therefore urge all members to support our amendments to article 8 (3). we can then also support this directive and we can get a quick solution for all patients in europe.
