the rapporteur. - madam president! - what is it? it is now almost 15 years since the end of the war in bosnia and exactly 10 years since the nato bombing campaign forced serbian forces to leave kosovo. in december it will also be 17 years since i myself came to sweden as a refugee from the war that had set my former homeland on fire and made bitter enemies of the bosniaks, croats and serbs who had previously lived together as neighbours. the fact that neither bosnia, kosovo nor any of the other countries in the western balkans have since fallen back into war is entirely due to the eu and nato. but even as the guns have fallen silent, the legacy of the war lives on in the politics and society of the region. the only way for the people of these countries to overcome their past is to continue on the path towards eu membership. only the carrot and the stick, which is the central dynamic of the accession process, can get the governments of the countries to focus on carrying out the work and the reforms that can cement stability and prosperity in the western balkans once and for all. in the report i have written on this subject, which the european parliament will now vote on tomorrow, i review the various initiatives and projects in which the eu and its member states are involved in one way or another in an attempt to build societies ready to meet the tough demands of eu membership. i shall not go into the details of the report here, but there are two points i would like to highlight in particular. firstly, there is a fundamental difference between the countries in the enlargement process and those that joined in 2004 or 2007. the countries of the western balkans were ravaged just over a decade ago by full-scale war and ethnic cleansing. fortunately, the same cannot be said of hungary, estonia or romania. but it also means that the eu cannot simply copy the manual of previous enlargements and apply it to the balkans. an example of this, which i mention in my report, concerns the prohibition on the deportation of suspected criminals prosecuted in other countries. such bans are currently in place in all balkan countries, but the eu is not currently calling for their abolition. this is done on the grounds that no similar requirements were imposed on, for example, slovakia or poland. it should be obvious why this analogy doesn't hold up. i would think that there are very few suspected war criminals hiding from justice in slovakia, but i can tell you that there are many more in serbia or bosnia. justice is the foundation on which reconciliation can be built. impunity for war criminals is completely unacceptable and therefore calls on the commission and the member states to raise again the question of the possibility of getting the countries of the region to work towards a joint abolition of these bans. the second is that the process of connection, as i said, is very tough and demanding, as it should be. if you do not set strict standards and insist that they be met in full, you will not achieve any real results either. when the requirements are already so tough and difficult to meet, the last thing we can do is to put new sticks in the wheels of the countries that want to join, sticks that have nothing to do with the ability of the countries to meet the eu membership criteria. i am also thinking of those who claim that the eu is already full and cannot accept any more members for the foreseeable future. but as i point out in my report, it is technically possible to continue to admit new member states even if the treaty of lisbon does not enter into force. but this requires political will, and that is what my colleagues and i in this parliament are called upon to create.
the president-in-office of the council. - mr president, madam member, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for a very useful debate. i am also very pleased that the idea of visa liberalisation with the western balkan countries is receiving general support from the member states of the european union, and i would like to express my full support for the statement made by mr roucek, who made a very strong appeal to this aspect, because strengthening people-to-people contacts between the citizens of the western balkan countries and the citizens of the european union is certainly a positive step and will help to overcome the feeling of a certain isolation that some of the countries there have and will contribute to the creation of a europe without barriers. i am also deeply convinced that the economic crisis we are currently experiencing must not become an excuse for slowing down the enlargement process, as some speakers here have said, such as mr rouček. on the contrary, it is particularly important for the stability of the region that the process does not lose momentum. i am also very pleased that progress has been made in the examination of montenegro's application for membership of the european union, which is being sent to the commission by the council in the near future for an opinion. the presidency considers this step to be a very important signal for the whole region. we also consider it important to unblock the accession talks with croatia. we regard the burdening of the enlargement agenda with bilateral problems as very unfortunate. progress in european integration with serbia remains a challenge, which is conditional on full cooperation with the relevant international tribunal, including the detention and surrender of the remaining accused to justice. the presidency is working hard to ensure that the interim agreement on trade and trade-related matters can be applied by the european union and that the ratification process can then begin before the stabilisation and association agreement signed a year ago. the importance of the stabilisation and association process for the reform process in serbia and for supporting a predominantly pro-european government would be undeniable. the presidential and local elections in the former yugoslav republic of macedonia met most international standards and generally accepted conditions for a free and democratic electoral process. however, it is clear that without a resolution of the bilateral dispute over the country's name, its progress towards candidate status will remain minimal. as far as bosnia and herzegovina is concerned, it must move forward. dependence on the international community is not useful, but rather it violates the principle of accountability and removes responsibility from local politicians, as mr swoboda rightly pointed out here. i would like to take this opportunity to call on the political leaders of bosnia and herzegovina to become actively involved in leading their country to a better future. it is counterproductive to return to politics based on ethnic principles, as i think mrs doris pack has very aptly said. the transition from the office of the high representative to the enhanced office of the eu special representative that is being planned does not mean that the international community or the european union is abandoning bosnia and herzegovina. e european union is here to help, and is fully aware that bosnia and herzegovina is not just another candidate, but rather a very specific case with its own sensitive issues and problems. as part of its strategy, the european union is planning a tailor-made office and policy and will be ready to take over the overall coordination of the international community's activities in bosnia and herzegovina. however, we will only do so if bosnia and herzegovina itself demonstrates that it is ready for this significant qualitative change. the first big step towards europe was taken with the signing of the stabilisation and association agreement, but that was only the beginning. we stand ready to continue to provide assistance in this inevitably long process. the year 2009 is an important year and could be decisive for the future of bosnia and herzegovina. first, the transition from the office of the high representative to the office of the special representative of the european union is imminent. it represents a significant step towards bosnia and herzegovina breaking out of its dependence. the second period from the next general election in 2010 should be used to the maximum extent possible to push through the necessary reform agenda, including constitutional reform, as mrs pack rightly pointed out here. third, the whole region is moving forward. and bosnia and herzegovina simply cannot afford to be left behind. i would like to conclude, mr president, ladies and gentlemen, by saying that we all want the same thing. we want bosnia and herzegovina to make progress. we appreciate the support we receive from you as members of the european parliament.
member of the commission. - mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for this responsible debate and for your support for the european perspective of the western balkans. i share the concern expressed in this debate by doris pack, hannes swoboda and jules maaten, among others, about the political developments in bosnia and herzegovina. it sometimes seems that bosnia and herzegovina has an inexhaustible capacity to generate and even renew political tensions, when in all logic we should rather expect a reversal of developments and a political reconciliation to find a place in the political future of bosnia and herzegovina. i agree with mr maat that all the countries of the western balkans have the prospect of eventually joining the european union if they fulfil the conditions for membership of the union based on the copenhagen criteria. this also applies to bosnia and herzegovina; in the case of these conditions there is no shortcut and no sale at a discount, and anyone who assumes that there is such a thing is wrong. it would be very important for this correct view to gain a strong foothold in the political debate in bosnia and herzegovina itself, so that the citizens can draw their own conclusions about the kind of policies they have the right to expect from democratically elected politicians. i see the future of bosnia and herzegovina in the following direction, and my vision is based on our cooperation with javier solana, with whom we have drawn up several communiqués on the future of bosnia and herzegovina and the role of the european union in that country. firstly, we are aiming at a transition from the dayton to the brussels era, that is to say, from the era of the high representative of the international community to the reinforced presence of the european union in bosnia and herzegovina, which would also mean a 'two-hat' system in the case of the eusr and the head of the commission delegation, so that we can make the best possible use of the political and economic resources that the european union has. it is obvious that the protectorate cannot apply for membership of the european union and that is why this transition is an integral part, an absolute element in the process of bosnia and herzegovina's convergence with the european union, and that is why the fulfilment of the conditions of this transition is in the interest of all the policies of bosnia and herzegovina and in particular of the citizens of the country. this is also a political issue, because it is too easy to blame the international community for the problems in bosnia-herzegovina when one does not want to look in the mirror. it is too easy to blame the high representative when you should be negotiating and reconciling with your own countrymen and countrymen. i hope that in this respect the political culture of bosnia and herzegovina will develop and mature and that the media in the country will also take responsibility for ensuring that negative nationalist language is no longer used as it is at present in bosnia and herzegovina. secondly, the country needs a constitutional reform to enable it to build a functioning system of government. the current state system in bosnia and herzegovina is too expensive, too inefficient and, on the whole, incompatible with membership of the european union. that is why constitutional reform is needed, through evolution, not through revolution, and i am glad that some signs of this kind of discussion are being seen among the leading politicians in the country. thirdly and lastly, in this context, visa-free travel is an essential part of bosnia and herzegovina's european future and i am sure that this will allow bosnia and herzegovina to enter the european mainstream, which is certainly in the interests of the country itself and its citizens as well as the european union.
the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? i would like to thank mr nechas for his commitment to this debate tonight. i would also like to thank mr rehn for highlighting bilateral conflicts which have de facto become european problems and for stressing the importance of not sacrificing enlargement because of the financial crisis. bilateral conflicts are currently holding back the ability of croatia and macedonia to continue on the path towards eu membership at the pace at which they are able to carry out the necessary reforms. in my report, the european parliament joins the ranks of those who insist that bilateral conflicts must remain bilateral and not be confused with the accession process. finally, i would like to say that i believe that enlargement to the western balkans is too important for peace, freedom and prosperity on our own continent for us to jeopardise it. that is the message i want my report to send to the 500 million europeans who will soon be electing a new parliament, to the governments of the member states, to the commission and to the people and politicians of the western balkans. this is a message that is particularly important to get across at a time when the economic crisis risks leading more and more people and politicians in europe to close the door on themselves and not allow more people to live, work and trade wherever they want on our continent. i therefore hope that this is also a message which my colleagues in this parliament will take with them in the forthcoming electoral campaign. if europe has become colder and more introverted, then our main task in this house is to work towards making it welcoming and open again.
on behalf of the ppe-de group. – mr president, i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr csibi. the result of our joint efforts reviewing the eco-design directive is, in my opinion, to a large extent, a missed opportunity. to widen the scope from energy-consuming to energy-related products is, of course, good. but why stop there? the problems we face in terms of resource use go beyond energy. the commissioner should know that. the problem is the overall pressure on ecosystems and the natural resource base. many scientific reports bear witness to this, not least our own thematic strategy on natural resources. an estimated two thirds of major ecosystems in the world are over-utilised, and we are depleting natural capital. this problem is getting worse because of growing economies and populations. the fact is that the current growth model and concept is not sustainable. who but the european union could help transform the concept to a more sustainable one? the eco-design directive, in my opinion, does offer a way forward. as with energy efficiency, we could establish standards and norms for how to source materials, how to design products to facilitate recycling and reuse and enhance resource efficiency. sooner rather than later we will have to address resource efficiency. having seen earlier drafts of the commission proposals which included resource efficiency, it is a mystery to me why such proposals were not followed through. tell me, where else in eu legislation do we have measures that encourage resource efficiency? we need policy frameworks that encourage resource efficiency across the board, that encourage companies to try new business models – like functional thinking – offering services rather than products, and where revenues obtained through quality of service, and not only via increased volumes from sales, are taking place. as i said, all this could have been covered by the directive. i predict that, a few years from now, we will all regret that we were not more proactive today. as i said, a missed opportunity.
for the ind/dem group. president. - the debate is closed. tax evasion is not our main problem as the world economy shakes at its foundations. it is true that we live with tax fraud all over europe, in the united states, in fact all over the world. it's true that we have tax havens around the world where wealthy people and companies can put money in and let it stay there. however, this is not our main problem today. it's an evasion of responsibility to focus on this. what we have to do now is to take a grip on the financial crisis if we are to have any tax bases left in the future at all. what is the reason for our present situation? this is mainly because we now have an ownerless capitalism. in practice, financial firms, large banks and most large companies are controlled by civil servants. these officials may introduce bonuses, severance payments and pensions, all of which are dependent on the profits of the company that these people run. there is nothing easier in the world than to increase profitability and thus the profits of companies in the short term. it's just increasing the risk. there are lots of studies of taleb distributions, as we often call them, which means that you can expect that if you increase the risk a lot, the return goes up dramatically and gives you all these bonuses and so on, all these "margins". then, of course, the risks will fall out, but then those who run the companies will have quit or will have to quit. they can buy wine castles in france, play golf in spain. i don't feel sorry for them, but the system is simply unsustainable. furthermore, there is no incentive for any actor to prevent this development. credit rating agencies are dependent on getting clients. they don't get clients if they say those clients aren't creditworthy. depositors know there are deposit guarantees. so they do not have to worry about which bank they put their money in. other players know that you can settle with counterparties and count on them being too big to fail, the taxpayers always take the credit. this means that the member states must say in advance that they will not take all the risks. it's a terribly difficult story to execute, but it must be executed. i do not think that the european parliament will be involved in this unfortunately, but i would recommend that everyone in this parliament does.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, i would like to thank sarah ludford for her report. kathalijne buitenweg cannot be here, but i will represent her views. i must begin by asking: what is a terrorist? what kind of feeling do terrorists create? they create insecurity. terrorists also make everyone vulnerable, and prevent them from pursuing their life as they want to. then you look at the anti-terror legislation with profiling and you see it creates insecurity and it creates problems for people living their daily lives. you cannot combat terrorism with anti-terror laws that create the same feelings of insecurity. i think the european court has been very clear on this, saying that this should be banned. we can see that it is neither efficient nor legal. the carter center in the us has showed it was not efficient. the german surveillance scheme on 18-40-year-old muslim men in germany was not efficient. it does not work, and i very much hope that we can take away the last exemptions allowing airports and harbours and so on to use these inefficient methods that make people feel bad. and how can you say that you will have a high level of protection when people leave cds with 20 million people’s data on them lying in cafés, and when we know how easy it is to hack into computers? i did it myself as a kid actually. so i think this report is important. the council needs to adapt its logic and spend the resources efficiently, targeting specific individuals and focusing the time on them instead of going for ethnic registration, which is in complete violation of any human rights standards.
vice-president of the commission. - madam president, ladies and gentlemen, once again i would like to express my appreciation for the way in which parliament has handled these two dossiers. this means that the new regulation on crossborder payments will enter into force as planned on 1 november this year and the electronic money market is thus given a second chance to develop. alongside the payment services directive, these two european legislative acts will create a modern and comprehensive legal framework for the community payments market and pave the way for the european payments industry to fully develop the project of the single area for payments in euro. this project will offer european consumers and businesses a fully integrated, cost-efficient and high-quality payments market. the commission therefore thanks - and i am particularly pleased to do so - the european parliament for this further sign of its commitment to the sepa.
for the ind/dem group. president. - the debate is closed. electronic money that can be used across borders is a very big step forward. it is an important task for the eu to improve the internal market by promoting its use. i would like to take this opportunity to remind you what we are talking about. when we introduced the euro for a large number of countries in europe, it was on the basis of the analyses of the value of a monetary union. the value is that we reduce exchange costs and other transaction costs. we are reducing the cost of information by having a common currency. the price we pay for this is that we get more unstable economies. we are making it harder to maintain a level playing field and a high level of employment and to maintain stable public finances. we are seeing this at the moment when things are going to hell in the head for countries like ireland, spain, italy and greece. it is important to note that the benefits of lower transaction costs through a single currency are to be matched by the sacrifices, but those benefits are constantly falling precisely because of the enormous progress made in the payment system. in a fairly short time, we will be in a position where we find that we have such efficient payment systems that the costs have become negligible. we are faced with a single currency which is really only ensuring instability in our european economy. i told you before, you can see it afterwards. think about this!

member of the commission. − mr president, i thank arlene mccarthy very much for her words because a lot has really been done on this directive. i would, however, like this directive – which we will continue to work on – not just to be adopted, but also to be embraced, by all the stakeholders. we will not spare our efforts for this to happen. as this is our last session here, may i use the opportunity to thank you all very sincerely, especially arlene as the chair of our committee, as well as mr harbour and especially mrs cederschiöld. i expect one way or another to keep in touch with you because it was a real pleasure to work with you and i hope this will continue in another way. i would now like to concentrate on the next important steps for the directive because i do believe that this directive is very important for all of us. we need this directive for the internal market of the 21st century. let me make it clear that, before tabling the proposal, the commission carried out a comparative law analysis, the ‘compendium’, covering the laws of the member states transposing the consumer directives under revision. the commission is now going through the various provisions of the proposal in more detail with the member states in order to improve its understanding of the practical impact on national law. my services are preparing a table, which aims to illustrate the impact of the proposal on the existing levels of consumer protection across the eu. the table will cover those provisions (for example the length of the withdrawal period and of the legal guarantee) for which it is possible to assess whether the proposal will bring about an unquestionable increase or decrease in consumer protection in the various member states. the table will be available by the end of may at the latest. under the proposal, member states will be able to retain general contract law remedies for faulty goods, provided that the legal requirements for the exercise of these remedies differ from the requirements which apply to the remedies regulated in the proposal. i appreciate that this interaction between the proposal and national general contract law remedies could be made clearer in the text. this means that remedies such as the right to reject in the uk or the garantie des vices cachés in france could be maintained. however, i am also prepared to consider alternatives whereby elements of these national remedies could be incorporated into the proposal, if such a solution were to be preferred by the council and the parliament. the proposal complements the unfair commercial practices directive (ucp). whereas ucp protects consumers collectively against questionable business practices by rogue traders, the draft consumer rights directive provides consumers who conclude a contract with a trader with individual contractual rights, which they can enforce before civil courts. as regards the services directive and the e-commerce directive, the information requirement in these directives will apply in addition to those of the proposal and will also prevail in case of conflict with the proposal. finally, as regards the rome i regulation, the preamble to the proposal makes it clear that the proposal is without prejudice to the regulation. as a consequence, the national law applicable to consumer contracts within the scope of the draft directive will be determined exclusively by the regulation. the commission, in preparing the proposal, was inspired by the findings of the contract framework of reference (cfr), for example, on the black list of unfair contract terms, the passing-off and delivery rules, as well as the streamlining of the pre-contractual information requirements. for the future, it will be important to ensure coherence between the cfr and the consumer rights directive. given that the cfr will be a longer-term project, we will have to make sure that the final definitions and rules of the consumer rights directive are incorporated in the section of the cfr dealing with consumer contracts. the proposal for a directive on consumer rights was preceded by a thorough impact assessment. the impact assessment also monetised the administrative burden that would accrue as a result of the changes proposed, and compared it with the cost of compliance that traders wishing to sell to other member states have to incur as a result of the fragmentation of consumer protection rules and rome i. the assessment showed that full harmonisation of the essential consumer rights would have strong positive effects on internal market integration and consumer confidence. during the initial stages of the legislative process, it appeared that further evidence and more insight might be useful on consumer behaviour and preferences regarding sales remedies. i intend to launch a qualitative study in the near future on this matter, with a view to gathering evidence following in-depth interviews with consumers and traders. the results of the study should be available in the third quarter of 2009. the commission is committed to maintaining a constructive dialogue – and this is my political commitment to you – with the stakeholders mostly affected by the draft directive during the legislative process. to that effect, i will gather consumer and business stakeholders together at a joint meeting in june 2009 to clarify elements of the proposal and to further discuss the most important aspects of the proposal. parliament will be kept duly informed. i would like just to add that last week i met all the consumer organisations under the umbrella of beuc and we discussed this matter again. two days later at least 10 representatives from the house of lords came to discuss this directive in brussels. this is a very good sign that interest in consumer policy is increasing, as is right for the internal market.
there is now room for improvement in consumer rules. the negotiations have not started, parliament has not made its views known and the member states are at the beginning of the debate. we need full harmonisation in the consumer field. i'm getting three points here that i think are eager to get in. 1. a clearer scope of the directive; 2. a product with a defect in origin can now be returned within two years; it should be three years. 3. the right of withdrawal should be set at one year, which will increase consumer protection in most member states. clear and transparent rules and a high level of consumer protection benefit consumers and traders. vague rules are a disadvantage for serious traders and smes. the council and parliament should now incorporate these three key points in a fully harmonised proposal. as this is my last debate with meglena kuneva, i want to thank you for a very pleasant collaboration.
for the gue/ngl group. thank you, madam president. what we see today is the emergence of a new society. we are seeing a societal evolution of technological innovation where the old type of communication that was one-way, of the type "one speaks and everyone else listens", has been replaced by a more multifaceted communication, a form of participant communication, and we are many who have adopted this culture of freedom. it's about interaction, freedom of expression, creativity and the joy of making. it is an exchange of information that is often independent of commercial interests, but unfortunately old power structures feel threatened and therefore want to step in and regulate and control what happens on the internet. the pretext of access to organised crime and terrorism is used to try to restrict our civil rights, but this must not happen. that is why i have worked with my colleagues on the united left on a number of civil rights amendments to protect citizens' rights on the internet. i would like to reiterate my amendment no 166, which was adopted at the last reading. the aim of the amendment is to ensure that the end users of electronic communications, i.e. us citizens, will be able to access services and applications without undue restrictions. we need to strike a balance between people's right to freedom of expression and personal privacy and protect personal data and the freedoms and rights of others, including the right to protection of intellectual property and the protection of public safety and security. in some countries, governments want to introduce new, stricter laws that allow internet service providers to shut people off from the internet. we believe that no citizen should be excluded from the internet without a prior judicial review. as a citizen, i must also have the freedom to surf the internet and feel secure that private companies cannot access the information. we who actually love the internet and the possibilities that the internet brings, we say that the rights of users should be defined by what we use our subscription for. we do not want to be reduced to consumers, so that our rights are only what is stated in the subscription agreement. unfortunately, both the council's common position and the compromise open the way to this, but we want to be citizens of the internet, not just customers and consumers. the internet is a meeting place, a forum for free information, a kind of public. are we now going to regulate this forum and adapt it to the interests of commercial representatives? no, i don't think that's what you mean. the question is, what kind of society do we want to live in? do we want to live in a surveillance society or do we want a society where people can feel secure that the rule of law is respected? a society where people can feel that their privacy is respected and a society where freedom of speech is valued above control of people's lives. that's the kind of society i want to live in and work for. so support my amendments and those of the left on civil rights.
thank you, madam president. the telecommunications industry is one of europe's leading and most dynamic industries. it requires investment, competition and continuous innovation to enable citizens to achieve the greatest possible economic potential, but also in terms of information, democracy and diversity. what we can see in this package is that we get clearer rules on competition and a clearer has been up for discussion here. i am sometimes surprised when those who are against the european union and those who are against the treaty of lisbon demand supranationality vis-à-vis the member states against which they vote in all other contexts. i heard my colleague on the left here today, eva-britt svensson, call for an amendment which would mean that the eu would go straight into the thank you, commissioner. there is a need for greater harmonisation in electronic communications and for a clearer and simpler regulatory framework, which contributes to the functioning of the internal market. the main objective is to achieve increased competition leading to increased supply, lower prices and better quality for final customers. success requires balancing many different interests. the telecom package is not intended to be used for criminal law, penal sanctions or to influence the procedural law of the member states. improved competition should be the focus. every citizen should be able to have his case tried in court; it should not be up to the commercial forces. i therefore hope that my colleagues will now support the compromise. integrity issues arise in commercial law, data storage, criminal law and transparency law. the union has not yet found a common approach with common principles applicable to all three areas, and that balancing will be necessary to avoid conflicting legislation in the long term. it is still a future challenge to find a balance between the police, the transparency officers, the data protection officers and, of course, the it companies. the commission could help by setting up a joint task force to find common principles and a balance between the different interests in this important area. finally, i would like to congratulate mr reding and the rapporteurs on a job well done.
thank you very much, madam president. i'm sorry i'm a little late. i would like to begin by saying that i think it is important to point out that the telecommunications package, as it stands before the vote today, is basically good. it strengthens competition and user rights, and this is important to stress, because it is something which i think is often forgotten in the debate. we are almost blind to the details and sometimes miss the big picture, and that is, after all, what the telecom package is all about, strengthening competition and user rights. but the threats to our civil liberties and rights on the internet and the threats to the open and free structure of the internet are many today. never have they been more clear than in the debate over whether to ban users from the internet without judicial review. i am very proud that we in parliament have agreed that we do not think that is right. you shouldn't shut people off from the internet without getting that thing legally tested. it is important because it is a very serious restriction of civil liberties and rights. it is also a reversal of legal principles which we would never accept in any other context. we don't forbid someone who has stolen a bicycle to use the roads. i am therefore glad that we will be saying a clear no to this type of proposal here tomorrow. at the same time, this does not eliminate all threats to the internet. there are many other issues which will continue to be debated and i look forward to those debates. there are some areas where i would like to see us go a little further, but on the whole this is a step in the right direction. i am proud that tomorrow we will make it clear that the rights and freedoms of citizens on the internet, particularly the possibility of avoiding being blocked without legal proceedings, are something that the european parliament rejects.

in writing. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft directive on the protection of the environment. we think that's good. however, the amendments tabled by the committee on agriculture also contain some items which we believe should be dealt with at administrative level. since everything is voted on in a single vote, we have not been able to oppose these proposals. our vote in favour of the amendments on origin labelling does not, of course, mean that we are in any way supporting the common agricultural policy.
in writing. - we are strongly critical of the thinking behind the creation of a globalisation fund. first, they take the view that globalisation as such is a problem. we see globalisation as a path to increased prosperity, not least for poor developing countries, provided that major economic players, such as the eu and the us, reform their respective protectionist trade policy positions within the wto. eu member states are able to take national measures to assist sectors which they consider to be in need of financial support. a special eu fund is created to protect against arbitrariness, inefficiency, bureaucracy and unjustified expenditure. how should the commission assess, in a relevant manner, whether a sector has been negatively affected by globalisation? in addition, the amounts involved show that this is more like a publicity stunt for the eu. for these reasons, we have voted against the report in question.
in writing. - we, the swedish social democrats, have chosen to vote in favour of the compromise negotiated with the council, interpreting it as meaning that the negative effects we feared for the swedish hunt disappear as a result of the derogation in article 3 (2). we've all been upset by the images of the canadian seal hunt. most consumers would therefore opt out of commercial hunting for products that do not meet the requirements of swedish hunting, cause unnecessary suffering and are carried out on a large scale in uncontrolled forms. we are opposed in principle to the eu's direct or indirect interference in hunting matters which are a national matter, especially when it could undermine well-functioning swedish rules. in this case, we have chosen to make a holistic assessment. in these circumstances we have accepted the compromise, as it sends a clear signal that parliament does not accept any treatment of animals.
in writing. - this report proposes to amend an existing directive on ship-source pollution. this is to enable criminal prosecution of pollution. junilista supports measures to stop pollution from ships. however, we believe that criminal justice should be a purely national matter. in addition, discharges from ships in international waters should be addressed at un level. we therefore voted against the final vote.
in writing. − the junilists consider that transnational environmental issues are one of the eu's most important tasks. we believe that the amendments, better than the commission's proposal, offer the final consumer the opportunity to make smarter choices by improving information on the energy consumption and environmental impact of products. we are, however, critical of certain individual letters in the amendments which seek to increase the level of detail in european energy policy. however, the amendments are well-intentioned and we have therefore decided to support them in their entirety.
thank you, madam president, mr president of the commission, mr minister. one of the main points of the june summit is expected to be the appointment of a new commission president. the media have said that the socialists will not vote for your re-election, mr barroso, unless you promise to open up the so-called posting directive. this is a strange discussion. the commission's previous approach has been to try to avoid opening up a very complex and politically sensitive directive and instead to ensure that member states which have problems in complying with the requirements of the directive amend their own national laws. mr barroso, i would like to ask you very specifically, in view of the forthcoming election campaign: are you and the commission firmly of the opinion that there is no reason at present to open up the posting directive?
in writing. – protectionism is hidden in ms showing less trust for each other and less enthusiasm to cooperate. eu was established as an attempt not only to assure peace but also as a tool for cooperation. it is in times like this, during economic and financial crisis, it's important for ms not to build walls around its national borders but continuing to act together in a coordinated manner. we must maintain european solidarity while adhering to rules and principles that stem from the eu treaty. eu must make maximum use of the single market and stand up for open trade in the economic downturn. attempts to find a scape goat, such as the financial markets, will not solve the problems. abuse shall be stopped by new rules but not prevent access to capital and investments when we come out on the other side of the crisis. it is not easy to overcome the problems. protectionism is for sure not the solution. the solution after the fire alarm activities are passed is more trade, more harmonization, functioning internal market with goods and even more with services. cross border cooperation increases innovation and jobs, the only sustainable ways out of the crisis.
for the pse group. mr president, commissioner, president of the council. i'm going to concentrate on what was supposed to be a summit, but didn't happen. jean-claude juncker said yesterday that we are now moving from a financial and economic crisis to a social crisis. we are also facing an employment crisis. we are going to have increased unemployment in the next few years, perhaps 26 million people will be unemployed in the eu in a year. this is the situation, and it is at this point that the council and the centre-right and centre-left governments decide to reduce the employment summit to a troika meeting. several of the leaders will not be attending this meeting. it shows that the council and the governments do not give priority to the question of employment. i agree with mr barroso that the commission wanted a summit. is that an inevitable development? no, it's not that. more needs to be done, more needs to be done in a coordinated way, and more needs to be done now. it is about environmentally smart investments that are long-term but also provide jobs in the short term. it's about energy efficiency in housing that creates jobs now but also makes housing better for the future. it is lifelong learning that has never met the objectives of strengthening europe for the future. if we do it now, people will get the necessary training while strengthening europe for the future and reducing unemployment. they can be replaced by young people who are studying and entering the labour market instead of becoming unemployed. we can invest in consumer subsidies for the most disadvantaged groups, for pensioners, students and the unemployed. it creates jobs, it creates consumption. mobility is important, as was said in prague at the workshop. it is important, it is very important, both professionally and geographically, but if we do not ensure equal treatment, equal conditions and the right to strike in the european labour market for equal treatment, protectionism will grow. the commission therefore has a responsibility to make changes to the posting of workers directive. in short, we can do things now, we can reduce unemployment, we can strengthen europe for the future. these two things are linked, but far too little is being done at the moment.
the committee on the environment, public health and food safety has tabled an opinion. − every year, hundreds of millions of animals, pigs, cows, sheep, chickens, horses and other animals are slaughtered and transported across europe. in addition, 25 million fur animals are slaughtered. this causes tremendous suffering to the animals. of course, it would be best if we did not eat them at all and if we did not wear the animals in the form of fur. but this regulation is not really about that, it is about how we can reduce the suffering of animals in slaughterhouses. the commission's proposal is a step forward, but it needs to be tightened up on many points. in the committee on the environment, we voted in favour of my opinion, which calls for, among other things, shorter transport and standby times at the slaughterhouses, investment in mobile slaughterhouses, the inclusion of fur animals in this regulation, the possibility for member states to take the lead and have stricter rules, and the independence and strengthening of the animal welfare inspectors and the proposed national reference centres for animal welfare. it is gratifying that the committee on agriculture supports our proposal for mobile slaughterhouses and for fur animals to be included. however, i am deeply concerned about several other amendments from the committee and individual members. it is unfortunate that the committee on agriculture has completely removed the proposed time limits for transport to the slaughterhouse and the standstill time inside the slaughterhouse. please vote for amendment no 125 by the left group and we shall have these demands back. i also do not understand why the committee on agriculture wants to scrap the proposal for national reference centres for animal welfare. it is also extremely important to allow member states to go ahead and have more ambitious rules than what is provided here. i would like to vote in favour of amendment no 124. finally, i am deeply concerned that the committee on agriculture does not want to allow those member states which have a complete moratorium on slaughter without anaesthesia. this is the case, for example, in my country, sweden. we have found a balance between religious tradition and animal welfare that is appreciated by most. please vote against amendment no 28, as proposed by the rapporteur.

for the ind/dem group. thank you, mr president. we have now gone through a global financial meltdown and it continues to plague us. we must then analyse why this has happened before we take action at eu level. i'd say this: the first is that we have got an ownerless capitalism. the big companies are no longer controlled by their shareholders, as pension funds, insurance companies and other types of funds do. it leads to officials being able to govern as they please, they do it in their own self-interest which is to raise the risks enormously and then it goes like this. we've had too many big banks that are "too big to fail" as the saying goes. the glass-steagall act was intended to prevent this, but it has been repealed in the united states. we should think about whether this is part of the solution. we have deposit guarantees for the small savers and also for the rather large savers. it means that the depositors in the banks don't care a damn about whether the banks are safe, because they know that the taxpayers are protecting them. that's the problem. central bankers are not punching bubbles, but are instead being praised for continually making sure that the bubbles can grow indefinitely. alan greenspan got a tremendous reputation for something that is actually a major explanation for why things have gone so badly. subprime lending is the beginning of this and a fundamental part of it, it was introduced by politicians who now say: "now we're going to solve this by taking more power from the market". i doubt that. the regulatory system that existed in basel ii was circumvented through shadow banking. now we're talking about new rules for capital. if there's more shadow banking, this won't help. i therefore suggest that we take a different approach to this, and ask what should be done. we shall then find that very little needs to be done at eu level. this is a global problem to be dealt with elsewhere.

in the first reading of the telecommunications package in the european parliament, a majority of members voted in favour of the abovementioned amendments nos 138 and 166. parliament thus clarified that a court order is required to have someone disconnected from the internet and that users have the right to freedom of expression and personal privacy. the council, however, chose to ignore the wishes of the european parliament and rejected amendments nos 138 and 166. the european parliament and the council have now reached a compromise. this compromise does not include amendments nos 138 and 166 in their original form. we have therefore voted against the compromise in today's vote. the junilist and the danish junbeweging are keen to see amendments nos 138 and 166 actually included in the telecoms package and have therefore tabled a series of amendments known by internet activists as 'citizens rights amendments', which have been supported by a few other political groups in the european parliament. if our proposals had won the support of eu parliamentarians, there would have been a good chance that the european parliament and the council would have finally agreed on a telecoms package that seriously protects the rights and privacy of internet users.
in writing. - i vote against because there should be guarantees that part of the free spectrum goes to non-profit-making organisations and not to large telecommunications companies!
in writing. − social security systems differ depending on where you are in the eu. this is not a problem, as many seem to think. rather, it is a natural outcome of the fact that countries are different and that general democratic elections have chosen to vote for different political systems. as supporters of intergovernmental cooperation in the eu, it is therefore obvious for us to reject the texts, both in the commission's proposal for a directive and in the report by the european parliament, which aim to give the eu more power over national social security systems. it is worth pointing out, however, that the strict proposals that are put forward are primarily aimed at ensuring minimum levels. the texts do not therefore prevent member states from going further if they so wish. that is a positive thing, especially from a swedish perspective. this flexibility, and the fact that equal treatment for women and men is so clearly highlighted as a fundamental principle of a well-functioning democratic society, has led us to vote in favour of the report as a whole.
in writing. - the commission's ambition to increase investment in energy infrastructure is the latest example of the hubris that has infected the officials at berlaymont. the proposed investments are both large and costly and it has not been demonstrated so far that all these investments should be managed at eu level. in total, €3.5 billion worth of investments are proposed for 2009 and 2010, money to be channelled from member states' budgets. for sweden, this means a significant increase in the membership fee, plus sek 1.4 billion. the fact that the commission feels that it has not had time to carry out a thorough impact assessment of such a comprehensive proposal is directly shocking. the rapporteur for the european parliament's report does not seem to be particularly concerned by these objections. instead, it is proposed to increase the aid measure from eur 3.5 billion to just under eur 4 billion! our mandate to promote less costly eu cooperation leads us to reject this easy-going handling of taxpayers' money. however, it should be noted that there are very good reasons to continue to seek ways of improving and developing co2 capture and storage technologies. we have voted against the report as a whole.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to increase the number of member states that have adopted the euro by a factor of two. however, the content has become too focused on fossil fuels. in addition, there is no support for measures to improve energy efficiency. the commission proposed at an early stage that €500 million be allocated to 'sustainable cities', a proposal which was subsequently withdrawn. the 'sustainable cities' aid had enabled extensive efforts to expand district heating and cogeneration and to improve the housing stock. such measures would be cost-effective, would reduce emissions and create new jobs. it is a tragedy that the economic crisis has not given us the opportunity to give new life to this type of action.
in writing. - we, the critics of the eu, are constantly striving to make eu cooperation less costly. the taxpayers' money must be used wisely. in these turbulent times, it is important that we are careful about our common resources. budgetary restraint must remain a guiding principle for us as elected representatives. the present report, however, takes a very different approach. the commission's initial funding proposal is considered insufficient and, in a twist of fate, the major political groups in the european parliament have proposed a doubling of the appropriations for the financial supervisory bodies. on what grounds is there reason to ask? we are dealing with a complete global financial meltdown where international action must be taken on a global scale. the supervision of financial institutions in the eu area is not currently a matter for the eu. it's important to keep that in mind. the present proposal, however, gives a clue to the ambitions of the political establishment. with vague references to the financial crisis and its possible consequences for supervision and audit, this is nothing but a misguided attempt to move the eu's positions forward. we can of course only vote against the report and the alternative motion for a resolution.
in writing. the european parliament has voted in favour of the report on the renewal of the social agenda (a6-0241/2009). it is a good report which demands, among other things, that economic freedoms or competition rules must never prevail over fundamental social rights. however, the report also calls for minimum wage systems. we social democrats believe that it is important that everyone is guaranteed a decent living wage and we think that the eu should encourage this. this is particularly important in tackling the problem of the 'working poor'. the choice of the member states to guarantee their citizens a decent wage, either by legislation or by delegation to the social partners to regulate it by collective agreement, will remain a matter for the member states themselves.
in writing. - this report stresses the need for member states to modernise and reform their national social security systems, introduce minimum wages and review school curricula. the commission also proposes to increase the financial share of companies' profits for employees and to introduce a european year of voluntary service. unusually extreme example of how the eu should take over national self-determination. in addition, the report makes two references to the treaty of lisbon, which has not yet entered into force. a disobedient expression of the arrogance of power! this means that the democratic treatment of the treaty is seen as a pure game for the galleries and is not supposed to have any bearing on the outcome. that is why we voted against the final vote.
in writing. - a very good report overall with many good aspects, but because of repeated demands for growth and the fact that member states must introduce minimum wages combined with legally binding social conditions which would entail a massive transfer of power to the eu, i abstain.
in writing. the european parliament has voted in favour of the report on the active inclusion of people excluded from the labour market (a6-0263/2009). it is a good report which is particularly important in the current economic crisis, where active labour market measures are needed to ensure that the weakest members of society are not permanently excluded from the labour market. however, the report also calls for minimum wage systems. we social democrats believe that it is important that everyone is guaranteed a decent living wage and we think that the eu should encourage this. this is particularly important in tackling the problem of the 'working poor'. the choice of the member states to guarantee their citizens a decent wage, either by legislation or by delegation to the social partners to regulate it by collective agreement, will remain a matter for the member states themselves.

in the light of the recent developments in the field of the internal market, the commission has proposed that the council adopt a directive on the protection of the financial interests of the european union. the report gathers the opinions of the various committees on the changes brought about by the treaty of lisbon. the report welcomes the fact that parliament is gaining more influence over eu legislation. we have voted in favour of this report because the european parliament must prepare itself to be able to carry out the changes which will arise in the work of parliament if the treaty of lisbon enters into force. however, our votes should in no way be seen as pre-empting the ratification process of individual member states. we fully respect the right of each member state to decide for itself whether to ratify the treaty of lisbon.
in writing. - the lisbon treaty, which coincides in 96% with the draft constitutional treaty, has been rejected in ireland as a result of the referendum. before that, the draft constitutional treaty was rejected by referendums in france and the netherlands. the majority of this parliament refuses to acknowledge its political losses. this is a shocking violation of democratic principles and an equally shocking example of the arrogance of the powers that make up european cooperation. the report by mr leinen on the new role of the european parliament welcomes the steps towards a united states of europe as envisaged by the lisbon treaty and the fact that the eu is now also to become involved in the field of education, including sport, among other things. it would have been desirable if the report had instead addressed the problem of the lack of democratic legitimacy of this parliament. we are again heading for an election with a very low turnout. the voters in the eu countries still feel little commitment to the super-centralist european parliament. as long as the political debate in representative democracy is focused on the elections to national parliaments, it should be the national parliaments, not the european parliament, which should be the highest decision-making political bodies within the union. i have voted against the motion for a resolution.

in writing. - the lisbon treaty, which coincides in 96% with the draft constitutional treaty, has been rejected in ireland as a result of the referendum. before that, the draft constitutional treaty was rejected by referendums in france and the netherlands. the majority of this parliament refuses to acknowledge its political losses. this is a shocking violation of democratic principles and an equally shocking example of the arrogance of the powers that make up european cooperation. i do not think that the european parliament should have more influence on the eu budget. during my time in this parliament i have repeatedly noted how the federalist majority is generously willing to allocate funds for everything from cultural projects to structural aid and an expanded eu bureaucracy. the majority of meps believe that all the various special interests in regional policy, fisheries and agriculture should have a share of the eu's cake. in some cases, these are expenses that are pure pr gimmicks. the eu is pursuing this policy of generous spending at a time of financial crisis, when member states are having to cut spending on health, education and care. most importantly, it has been fortunate that the european parliament has not had too much influence on eu agricultural policy, otherwise the union would fall into a trap of protectionism and heavy subsidies to all the different groups in the agricultural sector. i have voted against the report.
in writing. - we moderates have today i voted in favour of hennis-plasschaert's report a6-0284/2009 on the criteria and mechanisms for determining the member state responsible for the application for international protection by third-country nationals or stateless persons. we are aware and understand that the large number of people arriving by boat across the mediterranean creates a difficult situation for some of the small countries on the southern sea border of the eu and agree that something must be done to resolve the situation. it is important that the so-called suspension mechanism is not designed in such a way as to risk removing the incentive for member states to raise the standards of the asylum process and reception, which is contrary to the basic idea of the common regulatory framework.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy. we are in favour of a generous common policy on asylum and migration, which puts human needs at the centre, in line with the commitments of the member states under the geneva conventions. although the 'asylum package' means that good steps are being taken, we chose to vote against the reports by hennis-plasschaert and masip hidalgo. we social democrats deplore the policy of the right-wing majority in the european parliament on asylum and migration. we are particularly opposed to the question of whether language information should not have to be given in a language they understand, whether detention should not have to be carried out within the framework of the geneva convention, whether medical examination to determine age should be required and whether free legal aid should be provided. we also regret that the right wing did not want to give asylum seekers the right to enter the labour market within six months.
in the light of the above, the commission considers that the community should be able to conclude international agreements with third countries in certain fields. the same provisions prevent individual member states which have previously concluded bilateral agreements with third countries, or which wish to do so in the future, from doing so, as this is considered incompatible with the ec treaty. however, the eu may, in exceptional cases, authorise a member state to conclude bilateral agreements: if the community has no interest in an agreement with the third country, if the individual member state has a special interest in the agreement and if the agreement does not adversely affect community law. the junilist is in favour of the completion of the internal market and supports the search for solutions at eu level to the environmental challenges facing europe. in these areas we accept a certain degree of supranationality. however, we oppose the above-mentioned supranational legislative measure. of course, individual member states must be able to conclude bilateral legal agreements with third countries if they consider that such agreements are better for them than those at eu level! the fact that a small measure of self-determination is now being proposed, by introducing a negotiated procedure, is certainly good, but it is a small consolation that does not change the clear but unspoken objective of creating a european union state! i have therefore voted against the report.
in writing. - an independent foreign policy is essential to the maintenance of national sovereignty; the external relations of each member state must be subject to democratic control. the eu should not pursue a common foreign policy, as such a development risks depriving the people of their ability to hold their elected politicians accountable for their actions in their relations with foreign countries. this report contains a number of important papers in support of specific aspects of human rights. i have of course voted in favour of these. the report as a whole is, however, a way of advancing the eu's foreign policy positions. that is why i voted against the final vote.
