to elect your elected representatives, is the most important instrument our citizens have. to speak of a true democracy, it takes more than the right to vote. it requires openness, it requires publicity, and it requires an open debate. i would therefore say that it is extremely important that we now reform the first reading procedure. we must take parliament's confidence seriously and show the necessary transparency also in the first reading procedure. colleagues, we are facing major challenges: an economic crisis with rising unemployment, increased exclusion and social insecurity. we have a climate crisis that has already created climate refugees. as usual, the poorest are hit first and hardest. we see a european union and a world of injustice and poverty. there are, however, political solutions to these crises, but this requires a change in policy. the policies pursued so far have not solved the problems we are responsible for solving. on the contrary, it has contributed to the crisis in many areas. we need a change in policy. we need a policy for a social europe, a policy which promotes the rights of workers against social dumping. we need policies to prevent social exclusion and poverty. we need a policy that embraces the participation of all citizens. we need a policy that does not discriminate against any citizen, whether we are talking about ethnicity, disability, gender, age or sexual orientation. i want to see a european union that defends the interests of all its citizens. i want to see a policy that creates new jobs - new, green jobs. investments in green technologies are needed which besides creating the necessary new jobs also contribute to growth and tackle climate change, which is one of the most important tasks for humanity and for europe. i want to see an eu that takes its responsibility for fair and responsible international trade. i want to see an eu with a humane asylum and immigration policy, which protects immigrants and their rights. i want to see a europe of diversity. that's how we create progress. i want to see a europe of diversity, where we protect every citizen. i want to see a europe, an eu, that takes its responsibility for human rights. when our human rights are being suppressed, no matter where in the world it happens, we can never, ever, compromise. these rights are inviolable. it's every man for himself. whether it is freedom of expression, publicity, privacy, etc., human rights are inviolable. it is our responsibility, ladies and gentlemen, to stand up for human rights wherever they are threatened in the world. i welcome the president's statement that the june elections increased the representation of women in parliament. this has been possible thanks to the joint efforts of both men and women. we have worked together to increase the representation of women. now we take it one step further. we are now also making sure that women's influence is increased in the areas of trust that parliament and the other eu institutions have. now we have the opportunity. together, my friends and colleagues, we can show our citizens in europe that we are taking responsibility and show how a modern society of diversity is emerging. today, each of you has the power over the ballot. you have the power today to send a strong signal to our citizens that we are now building a people's europe, a social europe, to show both our citizens and the world that the eu is ready to take on its responsibility for global justice, for human rights and for global environmental responsibility, and to show the power we have with our ballot paper to send the signal that the citizens of europe expect from this parliament. (applause) i would like to thank you.
acting president of the council. - mr president! - what is it? first, let me congratulate you all collectively on your election as team leader. i know that several of you have been elected with very strong support. i know, for example, that martin schulz was re-elected with very strong support in the socialist group. it is important to be able to represent their respective groups strongly. i very much welcome the dialogue we have had and the consultations which the european council at its june meeting instructed me to open. it was done by the eu minister cecilia malmström. i have also done so myself, both by telephone and in the meeting we had on an archipelago boat when we were moving through the waters of stockholm and then sat down to discuss the situation. i had been asked to examine the possibility of electing josé manuel barroso, nominated by the european council, as president of the commission for a second term. several of the issues you touched upon are the main issues we want to work on during the swedish presidency. let me just say we're putting the job first. we want to see a europe where more people have jobs. the debate must be based on how to achieve it. i think, as joseph daul pointed out, it is about innovation and education, that is, what basically drives entrepreneurship and enables employability. i think martin schulz is right that we should be careful not to have a europe where we compete on bad terms. we have that discussion in sweden, and we have it all over europe. it is not a good starting point to try to meet competition with low or no pay, but we want to meet future competition on good terms. let me mention a few other things that i think are very important in order to manage europe through the crisis. i have seen how the commission - and i think it is important - has had to defend the principle of the single market at a time when many are trying to use it to bring in protectionism. it is so easy to listen to those who say, "why didn't you save jobs in this particular country?" without seeing the consequences of everyone doing the same. we would then basically shut down free trade and the possibility of cross-border trade. the very thing that has created wealth and prosperity would be lost very quickly if we did not in this way resist the calls for protectionism. i believe that safeguarding the single market and free movement is an important starting point for safeguarding jobs. i also believe very much in what has been mentioned by several of you, such as investing in people's skills and ensuring that there is mobility in the labour market. i believe, for example, that free movement, including across borders, is one way of dealing with this. i think, as martin schultz, rebecca harms and joseph daul have mentioned, that this is also the opportunity to meet this with a green development, to bring about the low-emission economies that we talk about all over the world as a way out of the crisis as well. it is important how we manage funding and how we invest. i would also like to say - i agree with guy verhofstadt on this point - that our swedish experience of our crisis management in the 1990s was not to think that this would be a good way to lose control of public finances. i've learned that when the deficits get big and when it comes to cleanup, it's the people with the lowest margins and the people who are most dependent on welfare institutions that get left behind. so a policy that is prudent about public finances is a good policy for people who are poor or who live on the fringes. as regards the climate issue, which will be our main subject of work before the copenhagen summit, i would like to say that it is true that there is still a lot to be done. we're running out of time. i would like to say to rebecca harms that it is unusual for us in sweden to be criticised for our aid commitments. in europe, the average is 0.4 percent of gross national income. sweden is quite unique in that one percent of our gross national income goes to development aid. for me, these questions are interrelated. we have had our own review, led by our minister of aid, in the framework of the un's efforts. we have analysed exactly how we have to think about climate change in our development work. it is not possible to pursue development without also seeing the climate change and how it is already affecting poor parts of our planet. so we cannot separate development policy from climate policy, but they are interrelated and must work together. i would like to say to mr schulz, on the subject of the treaty, that my role is to ensure that there is effective european leadership in a difficult time. we must be able to deliver answers to the citizens who want to see us act on the financial crisis and on climate issues. we are all politically active and we know that in the political environment, where we become introverted and discuss names and leadership, our citizens perceive it as if we have turned our backs on them. we're looking inward. that's why i do what i can in my role. i have been instructed by the european council to ensure that our cooperation and our respect for the integrity of the european parliament are combined by being clear - this applies both in the form of the treaties of nice and lisbon - and nominating a candidate for the post of president of the commission. as for josé manuel barroso, it is important to note that he had unanimous support in the european council, that he was well known as a candidate and presented to the electorate even before the elections. that made it easier for me to act, of course, with respect for the fact that the european parliament will have the opportunity, when you consider yourself ready to take a decision, to say yes or no to the nominee of the european council. in the meantime, there is time for discussions, as i know josé manuel barroso has also stated, and for the kind of conversation about how european policy should develop in the coming years. i hope that this can now be reconciled in accordance with the agreement that has been reached. that is what the voters of europe now expect, and that is how we can act strongly together.
president of the commission. - mr president, important questions have been raised, and i shall try to answer them very briefly. first, the important question by mr schulz on the treaties. it recalled in particular the role of the commission as guardian of the treaties. the commission does indeed believe that the existing treaties must be respected. the treaty in force is the treaty of nice. you were elected under the treaty of nice, all of you sitting here. the president of the commission, if elected now, will be elected as you are, in accordance with the treaty of nice, of course. that said, we are going to have - i hope - the treaty of lisbon. the necessary adjustments will have to be made to the composition of parliament, which will no longer be the same as under the lisbon treaty, since there will be certain adjustments, and the same will have to be done for the commission. nevertheless, in all respects, the european council respected your report - the dehaene report - which was approved by an overwhelming majority. the european council, before formalising its decision, held consultations which also took into account - and this is a first - the result of the european elections, not to mention the fact that there was a candidate supported by a political force. the next step is to obtain the investiture of the european parliament. i would like to repeat today what i said in a letter to the president of the european parliament: i am ready to discuss the content of the guidelines for the next commission with all the political groups who are willing to do so. at any rate, institutionally, that is my position. i would like to stress one very important point from a political point of view. i think it is important to link the election of the president of the commission to the democratic election that has taken place, your election. you were elected under the treaty of nice and it seems to me that the president of the commission should also have the legitimacy which, in a certain sense, must result from this democratic election. moreover, in view of the economic and financial crisis - and i think those who support a strong europe and who want a strong commission will probably agree - the presidency of the commission should not be suspended now, pending the final ratification of the lisbon treaty, which we all - at least the majority of us - want, even though we do not know when that treaty will come into force. to suspend the european commission and its presidency in the midst of an economic, financial and social crisis, when very important negotiations are waiting for us in copenhagen, does not seem to me to be very sensible. in any case, it is for the european parliament to decide and i am ready to engage in a democratic debate as i did five years ago. on the second question about finance and the economy and what mr verhofstadt said: we can all have more ambition, and on that matter let me tell you that i share your ambition. but we cannot say that we did not adopt a european economic recovery plan, and that was the maximum that our member states accepted. the european commission proposed more, but that was what our member states accepted. i want to draw your attention to the fact that some member states no less influential at the beginning of this crisis suggested not to have a coordination plan. some others suggested a fiscal stimulus of 1%, and the european commission came immediately to the proposal of 1.5%, and in fact the automatic stabilizers were around 5%. apart from that, we took those important decisions in terms of balance-of-payment support to some non-euro-area members and also some initiatives at global level. so you can count on the commission to do everything we can do to reinforce the european level and a common approach, let us have no ambiguity about it. but, also, let's be honest with ourselves: we are not the united states of america we are not an integrated nation state so of course we have different situations. you can't ask germany and latvia to do the same thing. we have countries in europe that are under balance-of-payment support, so of course we cannot have a one-size-fits-all approach. we have to have a common approach but with specific national responses, because that is the reality we are facing in europe and will face in the near future. we have mainly national budgets. so i share your approach to have a more coordinated european plan, namely to get out of this crisis and to build this kind of smart green growth we want; but at the same time we have to accept that we have 27 national budgets, we have 27 finance ministers, we have 27 national banks apart from our european central bank, and it is very important to strengthen the euro and to have economic policies and financial policies that are sustainable. if not, we will put the euro, one of the great successes of european integration, at risk. finally, on the question of climate change, once again we can always have more ambition. but for me it was very important that prime minister reinfeldt was with me recently in l'aquila, when we heard the secretary-general of the united nations say: 'you are the locomotive of the world'. we can always have more ambition, but the european union is leading the fight against climate change in the world. nobody is being more ambitious than we are, so of course i would expect at least a word of recognition in terms of the work done by this commission, together with our member states, to put forward ambitious proposals. let us now try to convince others, because we need others because the problem of climate change is not just a european problem, it is a problem for our planet. with your support i believe we can achieve success at the copenhagen conference. (applause) i would like to thank you.

in writing. - we voted today in favour of the resolution on the g20. however, we have voted against the introduction of a financial transaction tax because it is counterproductive to the capital formation of poor countries and is an obstacle to the development and growth that has lifted countries and people out of poverty over the last 30 years. we are also opposed to the creation of an employment fund to compensate for the fluctuations in the international economy, because it risks preserving old and outdated structures and thus hindering the growth and development of new jobs. this would require the introduction of an international system of taxation and distribution without democratic control and with obvious risks of corruption of various kinds. it is important that people affected by the crisis can receive support and help, but this is best handled at national level, not by an international bureaucratic system.
in writing. − the financial crisis was caused in part by banks abusing their customers' trust and taking excessive risks with their money. we believe that there is a need for a more in-depth discussion on how to deal with crossborder financial transactions. we do not think that the so-called tobin tax effectively prevents speculation, but we welcome a discussion on how financial undertakings such as banks, fund companies and insurance companies can contribute to a sound and stable financial market. the financial crisis shows that there is a need for cross-border solutions to cross-border problems. this debate should therefore be conducted globally and not only within the eu.

mr president, i would like to thank you. like carlos coelho and many other colleagues, i would like to stress that the creation of the schengen area in the 1980s and 1990s is one of the most important reforms of our time. jean monnet, one of the most important figures in the founding of the european union, is reported to have said: "the idea of a european union is not to bring nations together, but to bring people together". over the centuries, the mobility of people in europe has been severely curtailed. suspicion of fellow humans has marked relations between european countries. trust between countries has at times been very weak. mistrust, not trust, has instead marked the relationships. fortunately, this is a thing of the past and we are now seeing new opportunities for europe. most of us here in the european parliament have long been able to make use of the freedoms afforded by the schengen area. it is easy to forget the unique measure of trust between the states that was the basis for its creation. it's easy to forget the arduous journey there. but free movement is a prerequisite for people to meet across national borders. mr president, i would like to thank you. of course, as carlos coelho has rightly pointed out, it is important that there is an effective and transparent evaluation mechanism for the schengen rules, so that the area remains and develops into an area of free movement. but the basis of schengen is trust between the states involved, not the mechanism itself. it is important that this mechanism be both effective and transparent. that is why i see a problem with the commission's proposal. this means that the council's present role is transferred to the commission and that the scope for cooperation is considerably reduced. my most serious objection, however, is that we, the elected members of the european parliament, are excluded from the process. we are talking about something as technical as an evaluation mechanism, but we must not forget that it is about the fundamental foundations of european cooperation: freedom, security and justice. it is therefore important that we all be involved in the new decisions to be taken on this matter. i would therefore urge the commission to address the criticism here in the house. i call on the commission to come forward with a new and better proposal as soon as possible. the implication of a new proposal must be that changes to the evaluation mechanism will be subject to co-decision between the commission, the member states and, not least, us, the elected representatives of the european parliament.

mr president, i would like to thank you. mr. minister, mr. commissioner, it is a pleasure to see you here. i would like to raise two points before the european council summit. the first point i would like to raise is the climate issue. i think it is important to go into those negotiations with the intention that the best solution to that question is one that involves everyone agreeing to a common commitment. i say this because sometimes the debate is conducted as if it were a question of devising the best solution here in europe or in a single country. but it is not enough if we cannot include china, india and a whole host of other countries that are not currently participating in the joint climate commitment. that means putting pragmatism and results first. such a solution must also be based on the willingness of all countries to make a commitment. it must not be that commitments and policies in other parts of the world are based on continued investment from europe, but they must be based on a sound economy, a sound growth and a sound development of new opportunities with the help of europe and other rich countries. the second point concerns the financial markets. i think it is important to stress that the stability of financial markets requires, above all, what we call a stable macroeconomy, that is, stable public finances. this means that the debate on how to get out of the large budget deficits is far more important than the individual regulations on the financial market as such. however, it is also true that stable financial markets require that we have stable growth, stable investment and the emergence of new jobs. this means that when we legislate on financial markets, it must mean better supervision, cross-border and transparency, but not so much regulation that investment and growth is happening in other countries. we are threatening the stability of our own economy and our own financial markets.

in writing. - the group of amendments to the eu budget for 2010, known as 'block 3', contains interventions and support measures which i am opposed to in principle. these include various storage measures, including for alcohol, and substantial eu aid to the wine sector. it is also about eu aid for milk and fruit in schools, something which may be important but is not something the eu should decide. at the same time, the group includes important initiatives on, among other things, animal welfare and the control of animal transport, which i welcome. however, since the voting procedure forces me to take a position on the group of amendments as a whole, i have therefore chosen to abstain from voting on block 3. amendment no 886 was for a laudable purpose - sports betting. but this is not a matter for the european union. that's why i voted no. amendment no 905 was based on an attitude towards migrants that i cannot accept in principle, for example, money was allocated to tell people living in africa how dangerous it is to go to europe. we are not going to build walls around our continent. that's why i voted against the amendment. amendment no 909 included funds for the surveillance of european citizens. something i don't accept and that's why i voted no.
in writing. - the group of amendments to the eu budget for 2010 which constitutes 'block 3' contains interventions and support measures which we oppose in principle (in the same way as we are critical of - and vote against - eu support for tobacco farming). these include various storage measures, including for alcohol, and substantial eu support for the wine sector. it is also about eu support for milk and fruit in schools, an important issue in itself, but one which we believe should rather be dealt with at national level. at the same time, the group of amendments in block 3 contains important initiatives on, among other things, animal welfare and the control of animal transport which we would in principle welcome because we are vigorously pushing these issues in other contexts, but because the voting procedure forces us to take a position on the group of amendments as a whole, we have chosen to abstain from voting on block 3.

for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. in the 2006 compromise, the socialist group withdrew its demand that social considerations take precedence over the freedom of service providers. otherwise there would have been no compromise, mr harbour said in a press release. the word 'country of origin' was deleted but replaced in a commission regulation on the conflict of laws, which clearly stated that in the event of a conflict between the labour market laws of different countries, the laws of the country of origin of the companies apply. the directive could have been interpreted as meaning that the eu would not interfere in national labour law. however, the commission quickly drew up guidelines which stated that service companies do not need to have a permanent representative in the country of work, and thus the union has no counterpart to negotiate with. the vaxholm judgment also unequivocally established that swedish labour law is subordinate to ec law and sweden was forced to weaken its labour law. i and the european united left see no other way out for workers' rights than to put a clear legal protocol in the treaty where trade union social rights take precedence over market freedoms.

mr president, i would like to thank you. protectionists rarely admit that their proposals are protectionist, but they hide their aims with other arguments. i would argue that mandatory origin labelling is a clear example of this. in june 2006, i stood in this house and dispelled a number of myths about this particular proposal, and this time it ended up where it belongs, namely in the garbage can. but the decision on the korean free trade agreement has made us re-examine this proposal, and that is not a dignified way of discussing this type of proposal. three years later, it is evident that a number of the myths still exist and deserve to be put to rest. so let us start with the first claim that is repeatedly made, namely that consumers demand this, and then the advocates rely on the commission's internet consultation on the matter. but they fail to mention that 96.7% of the responses in that consultation came from a single country, namely italy, where industry and organised special interests are driving the issue. today, it is possible for anyone who so wishes to label their goods as originating, and believe me: any competitive company that sees a consumer benefit in this already does so today, so there is no need. the second myth is that labelling provides consumers with relevant information and consumer protection and promotes environmental interests, as if it were a matter of geography. no, this is actually playing on people's prejudices, not protecting people. the third myth is that labelling strengthens european competitiveness. but new technical barriers to trade are not protecting european industry. this has nothing to do with competitiveness, but is only enhanced by open markets and a good business climate. if we prohibit eu countries from allowing this kind of legislation within the union because it is protectionist, why should we apply the same rules to the rest of the world without acknowledging that it is protectionist? we have rejected the idea of introducing a mandatory eu made in label, and it would be unreasonable to introduce it now in relation to the rest of the world.

the most important change is the disappearance of the division into compulsory and non-compulsory spending areas. the european parliament will thus be able to take part in the decision-making process on the entire budget, and we will have a new simplified budgetary procedure. the treaty is now clear and approved, but that does not mean that we can just go straight to the new procedure. firstly, we for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. tomorrow is the day the telecom package is decided. i want to thank catherine trautmann and all of her colleagues who have fought for a free internet. i would also like to thank all the citizens who have been involved. they have rightly shown a very strong commitment, because what is at stake is freedom of expression and our civil liberties. thanks to the commitment of citizens, the protection of internet users against abuse of power and surveillance was better than expected, but in my opinion and in the opinion of the left, not good enough. i have three reasons to vote no. firstly, because the compromise, amendment no 138, does not provide citizens with sufficient protection against the power of the authorities and the internet service providers. it only talks about a pre-suspension trial not a court trial. this could open the door to arbitrary action. the text means that the rights of end users cannot be restricted by the member states, which is good, but companies can impose restrictions if they do so in the agreements. the second reason is that my amendments on internet rights, the well-known citizens' rights amendments, are not included in the compromise. it actually opens up a network where it's not obvious that all users have access to the entire network, and where all sites don't have the same opportunity to be seen. i think we should have been very clear that it should not be allowed to lead the internet into such a trap. the end result is likely to be a collection of cable tv channels rather than free communication for all. the third reason is that the telecommunications package falls within the rules of the internal market. this means, of course, that in the event of a conflict, the court will decide. freedom of expression is not a matter for the court of justice. it is not enough to protect citizens' rights only half-heartedly, they must be fully protected.
mr president, we in the swedish pirate party support the compromise that was reached in conciliation. it is not perfect and it is not everything we would have wanted, but we think it is a good step in the right direction. nobody should be shut off from the internet without at least a prior, fair and impartial procedure that includes the right to be heard and respects the principle that you are innocent until you are proven guilty. the compromise sends a strong signal to the member states that things like the french hadopi law or the mandelson method in the uk are simply not acceptable. it is now up to activists in france and in the uk to make sure that their governments respect this. but for us here in the european parliament this was only the beginning. we need, as several speakers have said, a proper bill of rights for the internet that makes it absolutely clear that the internet is an important part of society, where our fundamental civil liberties must be respected. this includes the right to information freedom and the right to privacy as specified in the european convention of fundamental rights. we need net neutrality and we need a policy that says ‘yes’ to the fantastic possibilities that the internet and the new information technology give us all. europe has a unique opportunity to show leadership and set an example to the world for a free and open internet. this is a chance we should take. the road ahead is open. this compromise is only a first step, but it is a step in the right direction. so i encourage all colleagues to vote ‘yes’ to it.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the protection of users has been the focus of much of the telecommunications debate. this spring, eva-britt svensson and others voted down a proposal that would require a court hearing if someone were to be suspended. now we have another solution, which protects users by very clear references to the regulatory system that must be required in each member state. i think it is important to say that the difference here is not whether or not you want to protect users, but whether or not you respect the right of member states to decide on their own legal systems. it is interesting to note that one of the swedish members of parliament who is most opposed to the european union and sweden's membership wants to make it more supranational than anyone else in this house usually proposes, because she wants the european union to legislate on how the legal systems of the member states should be designed. it is a big step and the very large majority of this parliament has opposed it, because we support the compromise that is now in place and which will provide good protection for users. we also do this because we want to open up the possibility for european consumers and users to always have a choice between different suppliers and operators. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the rules on competition are respected. this is something different, eva-britt svensson, from the time when we had the big monopolies that decided on the right of citizens to see, to decide, to use information. it is a wonderful change which, unfortunately, eva-britt svensson and possibly some others will vote against. but the big thing - and i also want to congratulate catherine trautmann and the commissioner - is that we are now also tackling the spectrum issue and ensuring that we can be the leader in europe in exploiting the digital dividend. it will bring success and opportunity to europe's citizens and give european industry the opportunity to be world-leading. that is why i and the vast majority of this house support the motion we are voting on tomorrow.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the greatest failure of europe today is that we have not yet succeeded in curbing the trafficking of human beings that is taking place right before our eyes. women and children are sold like commodities over the counter all over our member states. according to europol, half a million women were bought and sold in europe in 2009. we need to talk about this openly and find strategies to eradicate it. there are institutions in the eu that aim to combat trafficking in human beings and organised crime, but at present there are many shortcomings. the cooperation between the member states, the commission, the council and parliament must be improved in a fundamental way if we are to have any chance of tackling serious crime once and for all. this includes organised crime, mafia activity and human trafficking. in just a few days, the treaty of lisbon will enter into force. i feel like the air will be a little easier to breathe. because then there is hope at last for deeper cooperation for the safety of citizens all over our member states. we will soon be able to see europol strengthened and there is effective cooperation between europol and eurojust, and then we can finally tackle the fight against organised crime in europe, where human trafficking and mafia-like crime syndicates are concerned. let us say once and for all in 2009 that human trafficking is unworthy of europe today.

madam president, i would like to thank you. andreas carlgren warned that there are many pessimists around us. it's true, and i'm glad carlgren himself didn't join them. but unfortunately the swedish prime minister, who chairs the council, and the danish prime minister, who is hosting the copenhagen meeting, do. both of these conservative leaders have, for short-term party tactical reasons, played down expectations of copenhagen and thus made a good agreement difficult. it is embarrassing and utterly irresponsible, because there will be no better time. that's what carlgren said. it is in copenhagen that three things in particular need to be specified and made legally binding commitments: (1) how much each industrialised country will limit its emissions by 2020. the science suggests that we should be approaching 40% reductions, which is not only something we can do, but also something that will make us more competitive and create more green jobs. the amount and nature of short-term financing to be provided to developing countries in the rich world. this funding must go beyond the resources already pledged by the rich world to combat poverty. it is important that the swedish presidency is also ready to change its mind and fight so that those who are most affected by climate change do not also suffer even more from hunger.

mr president, i think that after the lisbon treaty becomes reality, the meeting of the european council last week and the nomination of commissioners, it is time to set new perspectives and see the new challenges that are ahead of us. one of them is obvious and that is that the work after copenhagen will be just as challenging as the work before. it will continue to be one of the main tasks for the european union and the new commission. but, also, we shall consider the fact that now is also the time to leave behind the old concepts of east and west in europe, or new and old, and to think that it is one europe. and it is also time to see that now, after all the discussions regarding the lisbon treaty, it is time to see a new momentum for the enlargement process. croatia and iceland – i would like them to compete to be number 28. but, also, the western balkans and of course the negotiations with turkey need to be considered. those are issues that are now ahead of us and are a way of improving the strength of the european union in a new world, where we need to be strong and to lead as regards ideas and values. but it is also a matter of now ensuring that we will have a new agenda for a social europe – meaning jobs, innovation and prosperity – and the only means to achieve that is to ensure that, after the crisis, we can become a competitive and leading economy. and i would underline that there is a responsibility for the new commission to ensure that there will be no protectionism, that it will be easy for small and medium-sized enterprises to grow and emerge and act throughout europe, and that we can ensure that europe will be a dynamic economy. this is important, and i say this to the president of the commission and to all commissioners: we need to ensure that we have a competitive european economy in order to have a social europe.
mr president, i would like to thank you. we hear criticism that the eu leadership has been given to two rather unknown politicians. i think that criticism is unfair. we in parliament have at least come to know catherine ashton as an excellent leader. but the criticism must be addressed, because many now conclude that the member states are happy to see the eu function as a simple intergovernmental coordination body, not as a separate political level tasked with finding common solutions to major challenges: growth, jobs, peace and the environment. if that were true, it would be an unreasonable development. against this background, it is worrying that the commission and the council have had such difficulty in showing the will to act in the face of one of the greatest internal challenges facing the eu, namely the reform of the eu's long-term budget. now we must find the resources to invest in the single market. it's about transportation and energy infrastructure. we must provide the resources needed to put the words of the new lisbon strategy to 2020 into action. we need to solve the issue of climate finance at least partly through the eu budget and we need to prepare for future enlargement. so my question to the presidency and the commission is: when will we see concrete proposals for, on the one hand, a midterm review that affects the non-existent budgetary margins for the remaining three years of this financial perspective, the current ceilings prevent any reasonable handling of the challenges that cannot wait until after 2013, and, on the other hand, a proposal that lays out the guidelines for a long-term budgetary reform?
mr president, i would like to thank you. i voted today in favour of the eu's strategy for freedom, security and justice, the so-called stockholm programme, but when the council's concrete legislative proposals are returned to parliament through the commission, i intend to be both tough and tough on the issues of openness and transparency in legislative work. this is particularly important in the context of the asylum process. the possibility of seeking asylum is a fundamental right and european cooperation is about breaking down walls, not building them up. the member states must therefore respect the definition of a refugee and an asylum seeker under the refugee convention, something i have now clarified in an amendment. in its final form, the stockholm programme must stand for european values such as freedom and respect for human rights. this is worth fighting for. i will.
the european parliament and the council have adopted a joint resolution on the situation of women in the labour market and the fight against unemployment. we are convinced that free trade and market economies are conducive to economic development and are therefore opposed in principle to financial aid to countries or regions. however, the financial crisis has hit the economies of the member states very hard, and the economic downturn is deeper this time than any other downturn that europe has experienced since the 1930s. if the eu does not act, the unemployed will suffer very badly in the regions of belgium and ireland that have applied for eu aid. the risk of social exclusion and permanent exclusion is high, and we as liberals cannot accept that. we feel strongly for all those affected by the effects of the economic downturn and welcome bridging measures for individuals, such as education. we therefore support the aid measures for the unemployed in the textile sector in the belgian regions of east and west flanders and limburg and in the computer manufacturing sector in the irish counties of limerick, clare, north tipperary and the city of limerick.
in writing. - we believe it is of the utmost importance that women are not subject to violence or sex trafficking. similarly, we take it for granted that we will respect human rights and international conventions relating to refugees. the fact that we as eu citizens should have a stable legal system and that we should all be equal before the law is a matter of course, and that we should also feel safe in the way the authorities treat our personal privacy. many of the 144 points in the motion for a resolution and the 78 amendments tabled to parliament's resolution were of course worth supporting. the resolution and amendments also cover a number of issues, including human rights, discrimination and privacy, which are already covered by previous programmes and also by the treaty of lisbon. we have chosen to vote against a number of amendments in order to obtain a resolution that is even stronger on the issues not already covered by previous programmes and treaties. although there are points in the resolution which were voted on which were not included in the table, we have chosen to vote in favour of the resolution as the advantages far outweigh the disadvantages. it is more important to send a clear and unambiguous message from the european parliament that we support the stockholm programme.

intimate partner violence is a private matter, a family matter. it is not that, but it is a structural violence and a social issue. the community must take responsibility for breaking the violence. against women, i argue, is a clear proof of the unequal power relationship between men and women. at the same time, it helps to maintain this power structure. therefore, the work to stop men's violence against women and children must be based on the recognition that it is a matter of power, control, ideas about gender and sexuality and a prevailing social structure in which men are superior in writing. the european parliament has already adopted a resolution on the situation in the former yugoslavia. today, more than a billion people suffer from hunger. moreover, sharp price fluctuations and a dramatic rise in the price of food on the world market have caused a global food crisis, making it even more difficult for the world's poor to obtain food. we would point out, however, that we do not share the view of parliament that market support measures and aid payments to farmers should be abstained from under the eu's common agricultural policy. we do not believe that these aid measures and the payments in the extension of the aid are contributing to food security in the developing countries - on the contrary, these measures have the opposite effect. subsidized, cheap european food is exported to developing countries and, through its competitive prices, often outstrips the developing countries' own food production, leaving these countries with no chance of more long-term self-sufficiency.

president-in-office of the council. − mr president, i have two brief remarks to make. firstly i would like to thank members for their kind words – also other words, but mainly kind words and kind comments. we now have a new european union based on the lisbon treaty, and i could say, being involved in the rotating presidency, that, to make this europe work for the future, it will be a combination of taking responsibility on the part of member states, the commission and this parliament. it will be very difficult, without full responsibility taken by all these parties, to get this work on track. just a few comments on trying to coordinate 27 member states. that takes time, but, without doing that, we get a situation where this european union is managed by just a few or by someone else. we have taken that time. i know how much time you need to make this coordination, and i think that will be obvious also for herman van rompuy and also the still ongoing rotating presidency. my second comment concerns copenhagen. i frequently hear this: europe is not leading, and in my country the opposition says that sweden is not leading: it is the will to bash on ourselves. then show me who the leader is. i would like to know that, because it would be perfect to see that leader and to follow their initiatives. i have not seen that yet. we are committing ourselves to reductions, legally based, earlier on the table, with concrete financing that i have not seen from other parts of the developing world. also, when it comes to copenhagen, i think it is very important to remember that we need to keep the 2°c target. i am not sure that we will be able to deliver that. i know that europe has done its part and is ready to move to 30%, but we cannot solve the problem alone. we only stand for 13% of global emissions. if this is to be a global answer, it must be global response, and then we also need the other major emitters to make bigger commitments. a few words on sweden, since that was mentioned. i think it is very important, after making these kinds of commitments – whether it is kyoto or now an agreement in copenhagen – to go home and do your job. we updated just yesterday the reduction of emissions that has been done in sweden since 1990. we are now down to -12%. we are following how this is internationally respected, the way it is presented. we could always say that that is the wrong way of dealing with it, but that is the global agreement the world has. concerning that, we have presented these kinds of figures. of course it is troublesome that some other countries are going in the other direction, and they are getting criticised for that. so it is not just striking a deal: it is also about making the changes in your economy using emission trading and other means to get the change in place. that is also an area where you see a lot of european countries acting in a way that is lacking in other parts of the world.
president of the commission. − mr president, just two remarks: the first on copenhagen, and the other on the successful swedish presidency. first of all, regarding copenhagen, i am also very surprised when i see many european colleagues with a self-defeating rhetoric. in fact, if there is a field where we can be proud of the leadership role of the european union, it is precisely on climate change. show me one relevant player or a group of countries that have committed as far as we have been committing ourselves. as i said earlier, some others have announced their intentions through press statements. the european union has announced its intentions through legislation which is already binding: legislation which originated from the european commission, which received the support of the european council and of this parliament, and – unilaterally and unconditionally – the european union has already put the reduction of greenhouse gases at 20% by 2020. no other player has done anything comparable so far. so let us ask others to do something similar to our effort. (applause)is this enough to reach the 2°c? no, it is not enough. that is why we are telling others that we can negotiate with each other. politicians and diplomats can negotiate, but we cannot negotiate with science; we cannot negotiate with physics. so let us have a global deal which allows us to reach an agreement compatible with what science tells us. that cannot be done only by europe, because europe is responsible for about 14% of global emissions, and the trend is to go down in relevant terms. so, even if europe, tomorrow, stops completely its greenhouse gas emissions, it will not solve the problem. so we need americans on board, we need chinese on board, we need indians on board. during these six months, together with prime minister reinfeldt, we spoke with obama; we spoke with hu and wen; we spoke with singh; we spoke with medvedev; we spoke with lola. and i can say that, in all those meetings, we were the ones asking them to come with more important offers. this is what we are now doing at copenhagen – not to forget – because sometimes people tend to forget – that it is not just a game between those players but also with developing countries: the poorest, most vulnerable, the african countries. we also spoke with meles zenawi of ethiopia and others. that is why the european union was the first to put some money on the table. so let us be honest with each other. we can always have more ambition, and the european union has been showing its ambition. but let us ask also for more ambition from others. because only with this ambition can we have a deal which is compatible with our ambition. it is a global problem and we need a global solution. finally, let me say a word to prime minister reinfeldt and the swedish presidency. this is the last time we will have a president of the european council only for six months, so it was the end of many years of european union work. i want to say – and i said it to president reinfeldt during these six months – that he was the 11th president of the european council with whom i have worked, so i really welcome the fact that now we are going to have a permanent president of the european council. but i would like to say to prime minster reinfeldt that he was the 11th in the order of working with the commission, but certainly he deserves a place on the podium as one of the best presidencies we have had during this period for the european union. thank you for everything you and the swedish presidency have been doing during these six months.
mr president, i hesitate to phrase it in this way because it does not sound very modest as a swede, but i think it is fair to say that the european union and europe will not be the same after this presidency. it is a different european union – a stronger and a better union – for a number of reasons, some of which i as a swede am very proud to mention. first of all, of course, the treaty that is now in place is changing the institutional balance of this union, but it is also making it more capable of achieving our political goals. i would like to point to the fact that we have opened up the process of enlargement by the agreement between slovenia and croatia, which is important for croatia, but also in the perspective of the western balkans and their ongoing future process of enlargement. this is one of the strengths of the european union, but it is also an opportunity for all of us. i think it is also important to point to the fact that, during this presidency and while we are standing here, the european union is, for the first time, a leading global actor in one of the most important international issues mankind is facing. this is new and it gives great responsibilities for the future because it is obvious that, whatever is achieved in copenhagen, the european union has been playing a fundamental and crucial role in setting the agenda for the things that we should achieve. however successful we are, the job will not be finished, but it underlines the great responsibilities of the european union. then we have the economic recovery, with strict rules for getting public finances in order and for hindering protectionism. i am a swede, so i may be a little subjective on this issue, but i think we all have a reason to be proud of what we have achieved during this period. but we should, in all modesty, also remember that those achievements give us a great responsibility for the future.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by saying that the presidency has lived up to the high expectations of an efficient and diplomatic machine. it has been appreciated, not least because of the chaos that partly prevailed during the czech presidency. the final steps of the lisbon treaty have also been handled in a very positive way. finally, the permanent president of the council and the new eu foreign minister were also presented. unfortunately, the political impact on issues that have a direct impact on people's daily lives is less. the european workers have received no support in stopping the wage dumping in the wake of the laval case. nor have they seen any new initiatives to combat unemployment and create more jobs. the environmental movement is disappointed that sweden has not stood up for environmental issues. sweden has rather taken a step backwards rather than seizing the opportunity to push for higher environmental and climate ambitions. the fact that sweden has not been given a more important role at the climate summit in copenhagen is due to the fact that prime minister reinfeldt himself, for party tactical reasons, played down expectations for the summit at an early stage. it violated the eu's negotiating strategy and angered a number of other european leaders. but more serious than that, it undermined the prospects for a good climate deal. finally, i would like to mention the stockholm programme, one of the few things that will survive the swedish presidency. as a stockholm resident, i am concerned that my home town may be associated with a political programme that is more concerned with building walls around europe than with guaranteeing human rights. we swedish social democrats are glad that you have finally listened in part to our demands and those of parliament to include more women's and children's rights in this programme. we have high expectations that cecilia malmström will do her utmost to further strengthen those elements in her new position.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the swedish presidency has worked well, both practically and institutionally. it's been like a nice engine, well-oiled, but it's been idling. where has the social legislation gone? the anti-discrimination work is stuck. the proposal to introduce a euro coin to curb emissions from lorry traffic has stalled, as has climate policy. the leadership on these issues has been taken over by the scientists and the european parliament the council has failed! the council acts as a crawling hole for the forestry, shipping and aviation industries in the climate negotiations. where is the money for the developing countries, the concrete 30 billion that parliament called for? where are the emissions targets? parliament called for cuts of 32-40%. now we're being told that the documents the council is working on are further weakening our emissions targets. the council's climate policy seems as crude as a blue whale fishing net, or worse! finally, the waterfall. stop the lawsuits against vattenfall! they're working against climate change. you have the power over this company. so at least make sure vattenfall does its job and stop questioning the environmental laws of the eu and germany.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i also give the swedish presidency a very high mark in terms of organisation. the swedish state administration has lived up to all expectations. unfortunately, i cannot be as positive when it comes to the political evaluation. there are two areas in particular which need to be criticized. firstly, in terms of openness and publicity. sweden is usually considered an example in this area, but now we have seen passivity and this is particularly serious when the freedom of communication of citizens is threatened. i would like to mention the data retention directive, the telecom package, etc., and the secret acta agreement. the presidency has been called upon to act to make documents available, which has been possible since the 2001 amendment to the transparency regulation, which requires that 'all documents relating to ongoing international negotiations shall be made available to the public'. why has the swedish presidency not acted on such a matter? the second area is about climate and, i would argue, the betrayal of poor countries that it means to use aid money to repair the worst damage the rich world has suffered and is suffering. even though the unfccc, the bali plan and the kyoto protocol say that money for climate finance should be new. once again, the most vulnerable are the ones who bear the brunt of the actions of the rich world. it is those who lack clean water, those threatened by malaria, those affected by hiv, and above all the world's poorest women and children who are now paying the price. it's a policy that's shameful to the poor world.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is strange to hear some whiners, including swedish colleagues, criticise the swedish presidency. what does carl schlyter compare it to? is it the czech republic or some other big-mouth presidency? getting a high score from mario borghezio is not something you should expect either. getting a bad grade from borghezio is actually a good grade. a lot has been done. the treaty of lisbon has been signed. the two highest posts in the eu are occupied. the commission has not yet presented a number of important proposals: the stockholm programme, the future financial supervision and, in particular, the telecommunications package. the copenhagen climate summit can also move in the right direction if we in the european parliament want it to. on the downside, i'd like to put the failure on patient mobility. this means continued legal uncertainty and unnecessary suffering in the care queues. but overall, congratulations to prime minister fredrik reinfeldt, eu minister cecilia malmström and everyone else in the presidency team. you all deserve a happy new year!
mr president, i would like to thank you. building bridges is never easy. building bridges across which 27 member states and 500 million people will cross from the old eu to with the new lisbon treaty a more open, more transparent and more democratic eu is particularly difficult, as the european parliament now has much more influence. in the fall, we've had both a climate crisis and a jobs crisis to deal with. nevertheless, the swedish presidency has succeeded in getting some very important eco-efficient legislation in place. i am particularly pleased that the eu energy labelling of household products has now been introduced. other examples of legislation now in place are energy efficiency requirements for buildings and eco-labelling of car tyres. finally, i would like to thank the government for an effective and coherent presidency. i would like to pay particular tribute to ambassador ulrika barklund larsson, who was so hastily taken from us in the autumn. she did a great job and we miss her very much. now the final, biggest and most long-term task is the completion of the copenhagen climate summit. good luck with that.
mr president, i would like to congratulate the swedish presidency for the vision of a citizen-centred europe laid down in the stockholm programme. we can never underline enough the historic importance of finally having a vision that meets our citizens’ concerns for security and at the same time for respecting individual rights. finally we can move forward to a europe for the citizen, by the citizen. i also would like to welcome the asylum support office, which is an important and concrete step towards meeting the concerns of countries that want to fight illegal immigration while at the same time providing for a more humane migration policy. the stockholm programme will remain with us for five years, and i hope we can move forward to implementation. i thank the swedish presidency. your footprint will remain with us for five years.
in writing. - sweden has applied for support from the european globalisation adjustment fund in view of the redundancies in the swedish automotive sector. we are convinced that free trade and market economies are conducive to economic development and are therefore opposed in principle to financial aid to countries or regions. however, this economic crisis is deeper than any other crisis europe has experienced since the 1930s and has hit car manufacturers in sweden particularly hard, and volvo cars in particular. the redundancies carried out by volvo cars are considered by the commission to have 'significant negative consequences for the local and regional economy' in western sweden. volvo cars is of great importance for employment in western sweden. if the european parliament does not act, the workers of volvo cars and its subcontractors would be hit very hard. the risk of social exclusion and permanent exclusion is high, and we as liberals cannot accept that. we feel strongly about all those affected by unemployment and would like to see educational initiatives for them. sweden is a net contributor to the eu and therefore it is important that employees of companies operating in sweden also receive support from the eu when the economic crisis affects these people.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is almost impossible to take in the terrible statistics mentioned here, and yet we know that they are accurate. however, there is a feeling, perhaps shared by many, that when it comes to the poorest of the poor countries in sub-saharan africa, the commitment is not as strong and as concrete as it should be. there has been mention here of military power. i think we all agree that the fight against poverty and corruption is essential if we are to achieve any improvement in the situation of the people of a country which has suffered so horribly. we like to talk about afghanistan and a lot about the terror there and, quite rightly, about what the taliban are doing. but here we have a people who have suffered and are suffering under the most horrible conditions. i would like to stress that there are non-profit organisations that can make a difference if they are linked to state aid and eu aid, but that can often seem very difficult.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' in the definition of the term 'competent authority'. the plan is an important step towards collectively recovering the eu from the financial and economic crisis. however, a large part of the recovery plan consists of investments in ccs technology. we are in favour of further research in this area, but we believe that too much emphasis should not be placed on this technology without first also comparing ccs with other existing methods of reducing co2 emissions. such a comparison then allows us to invest eu funds where they have the greatest impact in combating climate change. we support the budgetary agreement and voted yes to all the parts, except the requirement to introduce a permanent milk levy, which we voted against.
in writing. - we believe that agricultural policy must be liberalised and market-oriented in order to benefit consumers and rural areas. a thorough reform of the cap is therefore necessary. all forms of export and production aid to agriculture should be abolished as soon as possible, by 2015 at the latest. the abolition of eu and us subsidies to their agriculture is also central to fighting global poverty and hunger. during the same period, all trade barriers for agricultural products and fish will be removed, so that full free trade also applies to food. the part of the eu budget 2010 concerning agriculture, 'heading 2 conservation and management of natural resources', which constitutes the so-called 'block 3', contains interventions and support measures we we oppose. these include various storage measures, including for alcohol, and substantial eu support for the wine sector. it is also about eu support for milk and fruit in schools, an important issue in itself, but one which we believe should rather be dealt with at national level. at the same time, the group includes important initiatives on, among other things, animal welfare and the control of animal transport, which we would in principle welcome. however, since the voting procedure forces us to take a position on the group of amendments as a whole, we have therefore chosen to abstain from voting on block 3.
